# THEOPHRASTUS
# THE GERMAN,
## PARACELSUS,
### easily the Foremost among all Physicians and Philosophers everywhere.

*On the restored true Practice of both Medicines.*
<note>original: "De restituta utriusque Medicinae vera Praxi." In the 16th century, "both medicines" typically referred to the unification of internal medicine and surgery, though in a Paracelsian context, it often implies the harmony between the physical body and the spiritual or "astral" body.</note>

BOOK THE FIRST.
Collected into this order by Gerard Dorn, <term>Doctor of Physics: a historical title for a physician or someone learned in natural philosophy</term>, and German translator.

To the most Illustrious and Powerful Prince, Lord FRANCIS OF VALOIS, Duke of Anjou, Berry, Alençon, Touraine, etc.
<note>Francis, Duke of Anjou (1555–1584), was the youngest son of King Henry II of France and Catherine de' Medici.</note>

<margin>
Gift of Lord <unclear>D.</unclear>
</margin>

->LET NO MAN BELONG TO ANOTHER<- ->WHO IS ABLE TO BE HIS OWN<-
<note>original: "ALTERIUS NON SIT QUI SUUS ESSE [POTEST]." This was Paracelsus's personal motto, emphasizing intellectual and spiritual independence from the rigid dogmas of traditional academia.</note>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page and its verso. It begins the formal dedication of the work to Francis, Duke of Alençon. The text establishes the philosophical foundation of the book, rooted in the Paracelsian belief that knowledge is a divine gift inherent in all humanity.</meta>

<note>The page is framed by a decorative border of floral and scrollwork motifs.</note>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS 
# AND POWERFUL PRINCE, 
# FRANCIS DE VALOIS,
## Royal Son of France, Duke 
## of Alençon, etc.

<center>*</center>

<note>A decorated initial letter "T" depicts two figures in a landscape setting.</note>

**T**HE great German, Theophrastus Paracelsus—the most recent reviver of Medical Philosophy, which had long been slumbering and buried alongside the other arts—teaches us that all wisdom depends upon a single father: God, the giver of lights <note>This is a reference to the New Testament, James 1:17: "Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, and cometh down from the Father of lights."</note>. This wisdom flows down to us as his children, partly through hereditary right and partly as a gift of his generosity. Indeed, that good parent of all bestows wisdom and the arts upon everyone universally and to each individual equally, so that no experienced or industrious man may boast over even the most unlearned <note>original: "idiotam." In this historical context, the term refers to an ordinary person or a layman lacking formal academic training, rather than its modern derogatory meaning.</note> or idle person, nor presume anything of himself. Just as God gave no one more or fewer limbs than others, but gave to this one the same as to that, he likewise willed that the minds of all should be made equally capable and receptive to his wisdom. It is therefore unjust for one man to learn from another what neither possesses more fortunately than the other. In every person, the arts lie hidden as if they were sleeping; while a faculty may be awake in one, it is granted by heaven to awaken that dormant power in another, yet not to introduce it from the outside—for these arts are present in all from the beginning. For if one deliberates on a doubtful matter 
<margin>* 2</margin> <note>This "2" with an asterisk is a signature mark, used by early modern printers to keep the folded sheets of the book in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>This page contains the opening of a dedication to Francis, Duke of Alençon, invoking the authority of Paracelsus to argue that all wisdom is a divine gift inherently present in every human being.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Duke of Alençon, Paracelsus, Medical Philosophy, divine wisdom, egalitarianism in learning, Father of Lights, innate knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Francis, Duke of Alençon. The previous page established that all wisdom comes from God and is latent in all human beings, waiting to be awakened. This page elaborates on the equality of human potential and introduces Paracelsus as the one who seeks to awaken this dormant knowledge through the "Light of Nature."</meta>

->E P I S T O L A<-

If several people gather to deliberate on matters unknown to each of them except for one—who, by untying the knot <note>original: "nodum expedienti." A classical metaphor for solving a complex problem or revealing a hidden truth.</note>, gains the agreement of the rest—it becomes clear to everyone that the same reason and knowledge existed in all of them. For they approve of the counsel that lay sleeping within them once it is awakened by the one in whom it was already active. Otherwise, they could not possibly have agreed with the opinion of a single person, nor could they admit that he had spoken things which had not occurred to their own minds, just as they had to his. 

What reason, then, does that man have to boast that this judgment flourished in him at that moment? For in others, something else—perhaps better and more excellent—could be awakened in its own time, which might happen to be sleeping in that very man. Conversely, no one can rightly complain that less was granted to him than to another. Indeed, just as one might sleepily forget one's duty while slumbering, he is able to remind himself through wakefulness and diligence of the aptitude and industry that lay hidden within him. Or, he might patiently follow those who awaken him, using his talent for interest and profit <note>This is an allusion to the Parable of the Talents in the New Testament (Matthew 25:14–30), where servants are expected to invest the "talents" or money given to them by their master.</note>, rather than burying it and uselessly scorning it. 

Moved by pity for this calamity and by a zeal for charity, Paracelsus strives to direct all his strength toward this goal more and more every day (although he has yielded to fate, he does so now through his writings alone) to awaken those sleeping in the medical and philosophical professions, and to call them back, as if from darkness to light. 

Therefore, since he saw that they were taught by men, and not from the Light of Nature <note>original: "luce naturæ." A core concept in Paracelsian thought; it refers to the innate wisdom of the created world which a physician must read like a book to understand the secrets of healing.</note> (which is a divine light, exercising its brilliance everywhere throughout this great world), he places this mirror before their eyes. He does this so that from it, no differently than from a book, they might be able to see, learn, and discern between good and evil, as well as truth from falsehood, more accurately and perfectly than they have been able to do thus far from the various writings of their teachers. Therefore, everywhere he emphasizes...

.ex

<summary>This page argues that all humans possess latent knowledge and that Paracelsus's writings serve as a "mirror" or "book of nature" to awaken this dormant wisdom. It emphasizes the "Light of Nature" as a divine guide for medicine and philosophy, superior to traditional academic teaching.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Light of Nature, latent knowledge, medical philosophy, Parable of the Talents, awakening, divine wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Francis, Duke of Alençon. Having established that all humans possess latent knowledge, the text now explains how Paracelsus seeks this wisdom by studying the "Macrocosm" (the great world) to understand the "Microcosm" (the human being).</meta>

->DEDICATION<-

...from the anatomy of the great world <note>original: "magni mundi." This refers to the Macrocosm—the entire universe—which Paracelsus believed was perfectly mirrored within the human being, or Microcosm.</note>, worldly wisdom must be sought, as if from the light of nature <note>original: "luce naturæ." A central concept in Paracelsian philosophy referring to the innate intelligence and divine order found within the natural world, accessible through observation and reason.</note>. In the same way, every kind of vice must be recognized and avoided by looking at the darkness of that same nature, as shown through many different examples.

Man (he says) is not only a son of God, but also a child of all His creatures encompassed by nature. Since he was indeed the final creature, fashioned from the mud <note>original: "limo." Referring to the biblical creation of Adam from the dust or "slime" of the earth (Genesis 2:7).</note>, he is composed of those things created before him and for his sake—namely, from both the animal nature <note>original: "brutina." Used here to describe the instinctual, "brutish" aspects of biological life.</note> and the nature of the heavens. Indeed, it is obvious not only that animals live in the manner of men, but even more so, that the lives of men are often transformed into the nature of animals.

However, because a son cannot be properly understood through himself, nor any better than through his father, it is necessary to recognize the properties of the animal nature, the firmament, and the elements within man. Birds build nests; men build houses and palaces. Animal instinct itself persuades them to meet and mate, male and female, and to remain and live together; for we see that animals do exactly the same. Bees, herrings <note>original: "haleca." A reference to the social, schooling nature of fish, used as a model for human society.</note>, and cranes grant the first place to their leaders and kings and offer them obedience; in this very respect, they even surpass us humans. Ants, too, convict us of laziness; they serve as an inspiration, teaching us to gather in summer what we will need in the winter, and for the young to prepare the necessities of life for old age.

Why, I ask, are we less moved by these examples, which display a specimen of virtue, than by those things which are diametrically opposed to virtue? Is it not because that most depraved and corrupt nature within us attempts to extinguish the light with its own shadows, twisting our animal nature toward its own vices? For example, when we see dogs fighting and tearing each other apart over a mate in heat, what else do men do over a common woman? A comparison to this through wars...

*comparison

<summary>This page argues that human nature is composed of the same elements as the animal and celestial worlds, meaning we must study nature to understand ourselves. It uses examples of animal social behavior—like the order of bees and the foresight of ants—to shame human vice and laziness.</summary>
<keywords>Macrocosm, Light of Nature, human nature, animal instinct, virtue vs vice, Paracelsian philosophy, social order, anatomy of the world</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle to Francis, Duke of Alençon. The author is explaining how human nature contains the qualities of all other creatures—both the "brutish" instincts of animals and the "astral" influences of the stars—and how wisdom allows a person to rise above these base inclinations.</meta>

->EPISTLE<-

...the nature of fornication is related to **canine behavior** <note>original: "caninū opus." In Paracelsian thought, human vices are often seen as the outward expression of an internal "animal" nature.</note>. Likewise, dogs are powerful in their envy, driven by a greed to possess things and leave nothing for others; yet men who are filled with such vices do not merely resemble dogs, they far surpass them. They embody not only those traits, but also the cunning of **foxes**, the cruelty and ferocity of **lions**, and other qualities of that kind.

On the other hand, man is a **son of the firmament** <note>The "firmament" refers to the sphere of the stars and planets, which Paracelsus believed exerted a physical and psychological influence on the "brute" or biological part of humans.</note>, because the animal nature is subject to the stars. For example, the **Lamb** is associated with the **Sun**, and so is the man who is gentle in his character and life. The **Wolf** is associated with **Saturn**, as is the man who lives by plunder, while the **Bear** represents the man who lives by robbery. The **Rooster** is a son of **Mars**, as is the man driven by war and anger. 

Thus, the firmament rules the animal nature within man through its inclinations, and nothing more. Man, by contrast, is able to resist the stars and the firmament through **wisdom**, and not through his animal nature. Regarding this wisdom—which belongs to a different and more sublime place—we shall not discuss it here. Our discourse concerns only **worldly wisdom**, which, when compared to the other [divine wisdom], is mere folly and does not appear before the face of God, since it dies along with the animal nature of the person.

For this reason, those followers of worldly wisdom believe they please God more than others who understand less; they stubbornly look down upon them, even though those others are far more fortunate. If we were to believe that knowing nothing makes life happy—because he who has received nothing is not required to give an account of his talent—it would be much more blessed to be burdened less by wisdom.

These things are said not so that men are held back from the study of wisdom, but rather so that, having recognized the distinction between the **animal** and the **rational**, they might move closer to that part of wisdom which joins **virtue** to **necessity** (the reason for which the arts were invented). This is better than following the path of those who indulge their own appetites solely for profit, like irrational beasts. Likewise, those who have been made greater sharers of reason should bear with others, who are of lower...

<summary>The author explains that human vices mirror animal instincts (like the greed of dogs or the ferocity of lions) and that these are governed by the stars. He argues that true wisdom allows humans to overcome these celestial inclinations, whereas worldly wisdom is fleeting and often leads to spiritual pride.</summary>
<keywords>microcosm, macrocosm, firmament, animal nature, canine work, astrology, worldly wisdom, virtue, Paracelsus, Mars, Saturn</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle. While the previous page discussed how man’s internal "brute" nature is influenced by the stars, this section explains how wisdom and medical knowledge are often found in humble places—like the observations of peasants or the natural behaviors of animals—through the "light of nature."</meta>

# DEDICATION

...those who have attained their goal more humbly, because they are men, should consider themselves worthy of honor. Who, I ask, taught the physician the powers of the **snakeroot herb** <note>original: *herba serpentaria*. In the 16th century, this usually referred to plants like bistort or birthwort, believed to cure snakebites due to their physical resemblance to a serpent—an application of the "Doctrine of Signatures."</note>? Was it not the **light of nature** <note>original: *lux naturae*. A fundamental concept in Paracelsian philosophy, referring to the innate knowledge of the physical world and its properties that God implanted in all of creation, accessible through observation rather than just ancient books.</note> through the serpent? 

And perhaps the first person who noticed this—observing a snake healing itself when injured—was a simple peasant <note>original: *rusticus*</note>. It is no wonder that the lot of such people is better suited to the woodlands. In this way, it is discovered that the physician and the wise man are taught by the peasant, rather than the peasant being taught by them. Therefore, nothing should be rejected that comes to light and experience from the **light of nature**, whether it is made manifest through a nobleman, a city-dweller, or a rural man through diligent observation. 

We see, therefore, that anyone can be stirred by the brute animals, no less toward virtues than toward vices. To reject the latter from oneself and to choose the former as an example to follow in life and conduct is not unjust, but worthy of a wise man (since all virtue comes from God alone, even in the brutes). We may learn:
*   **fidelity** from the *dog*, casting aside its flattery and envy; 
*   **prudence** from the *serpent*, leaving behind its traps; 
*   **patience** from the *donkey*, neglecting its laziness and sluggishness; 
*   **simplicity** from the *dove*. 

Nor are there lacking among other creatures those who observe governance and equity, as can be gathered from their histories. Whoever, therefore, is taught from the **book of the light of nature**, must be considered the legitimate sons of wisdom, because they have their arts from God. For everything is full of divine light and wisdom—at least for those whose "eye" is the mind. 

On the other hand, those who rely solely on their imaginations and doubtful opinions, or are instructed only by the doctrines of men, sow a **bastard seed** <note>original: *spurium germen*</note> among all nations. They do and hand down everything in a monstrous and backward way, and they take great care to cloak their illegitimate teachings <note>original: *nothos*</note> so that they appear decorated on the outside as if they were legitimate. Thence, in place of 
          * 4          health...

<summary>The author argues that true wisdom and medical knowledge come from the "light of nature," which can be observed even in animals and by simple peasants. He contrasts these "legitimate" sons of wisdom with academic scholars who rely on human opinion and imagination.</summary>

<keywords>light of nature, serpentaria, snakeroot, animal virtues, rustic wisdom, legitimate wisdom, medical observation, Paracelsus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle. The previous page concluded by discussing how all virtue originates from God, even when observed in the natural behaviors of "brute" animals. This page transitions into how Paracelsus applied these natural observations to reform the "errors" of traditional medicine through alchemical practice.</meta>

->E P I S T O L A<-

of health in Medicine, and from the introduction of death, various teachings arise: no differently than in other arts, such as Philosophy, where the overthrow of kingdoms, hatreds, dissensions, and other such things are born—the unspeakable and portentous monsters of all calamities. To turn aside such great errors, especially in Medicine, Paracelsus laid the true foundations with tireless labor and the greatest zeal (as is fitting) in the **light of nature** <note>original: *luce naturae*. A core Paracelsian belief that nature itself is a revelation of God's wisdom, and by studying the physical world, one learns divine truths.</note>, yet to be sought **through fire** <note>original: *per ignem*. This refers to alchemy or "spagyrics"—the process of using heat and chemical separation to extract the pure "virtue" or essence from raw materials.</note>: so that the hidden thing might come into the light, in which the medicinal virtue lies concealed, a **perfect and precious pearl** <note>original: *perfecta preciosáque margarita*. In alchemical literature, the "pearl" often symbolizes the *Arcanum* or the ultimate curative essence extracted from a substance.</note>. For just as the brute animals do, the elements no less stir man in the **lesser globe** <note>The "lesser globe" refers to the Microcosm (the human being), while the "greater globe" refers to the Macrocosm (the universe).</note> just as the stars do in the greater globe through the firmament. Anyone who is strong in judgment and intellect is compelled to admit from the preceding points that no other, nor more powerful, foundations can be held than these which he places before our eyes, and by which he strives to stir us and ensure that we return to the true goal of medicine, from which there has been wide and far-reaching wandering until now.

Therefore, **Most Illustrious Prince** <note>The identity of the Prince depends on the specific edition, but Paracelsian works of this era were often dedicated to German nobility or the French King, Henry IV, who was a patron of such "new" medicine.</note>, when several years ago I had rendered into Latin many books of this same most weighty author (originally written in the German tongue) for the sake of those nations who do not hold that language, and when I saw that, above all others, the **French** <note>original: *Gallos*. Paracelsianism saw a massive surge in popularity in France during the late 16th century, leading to fierce debates between traditional Galenic doctors and the new "chemical" physicians.</note> took delight in that kind of philosophizing and healing: many distinguished and eminent men among them urged me not to cease from what I had begun. Among these, especially (as one well-versed in the noble arts), **Pierre Degrantrye** <note>Latin: *Petrus Degrantrye*. Likely an official or courtier serving the dedicatee.</note>, the regular Master of your Illustrious Highness’s court, did not hesitate to greatly promote these efforts of mine, even to the point where your Illustrious Highness deigned me worthy of the regular duty of translating physics and interpreting these same books, although I am unworthy
                                                                           reason

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle. The previous page discussed Paracelsus's restoration of medicine through the "light of nature" and alchemical processes ("through fire"). This page transitions into the translator's personal commitment to the patron, promising to bring the author’s German works into the Latin and French spheres to satisfy the intellectual "thirst" of the French people.</meta>

->D E D I C A T O R Y<-

Reason therefore demands that whatever strength remains in me, I should diligently spend it all—even to the point of exhaustion, if necessary—on this matter, and act as befits a faithful servant and interpreter. Let this present little work be my witness from the beginning; though it is small, it is not to be despised because of the utility it brings to readers. For it will grow in its own time (God willing) so that it may even find its place among other works in that regard.

I frankly confess that I owe much more excellent things to Your Illustrious Excellence <note>original: *Illuſtriſſimo C.T.*, an abbreviation for *Celsitudini Tuae*, a formal address for a high-ranking patron or prince.</note>. Once they have emerged into the light from the caves in which they have been held until now, and when all the German writings of this author <note>Referring to Paracelsus, who wrote primarily in his native German rather than the academic Latin of the period.</note> come into my hands, I will endeavor (with the favor of the powers above) to bring them into your library, translated into Latin or French with the greatest faithfulness and diligence, by which I am bound to Your Illustrious Excellency.

In this way, all noble minds who are studious of the secret things of nature may return thanks and praise to the name of Your Illustrious Excellence. By your benefit and at your expense, it will now be a pleasure to satisfy that thirst—contracted in France over a long stretch of time—with philosophical waves and medicinal springs <note>A metaphor for the revitalized knowledge of alchemical medicine.</note> far more fruitful than those that came before. What is more worthy of such a great Prince, or more glorious in this life, than to raise up and advance the study of virtues? And how much more so to wish that the arts—dulled by the injustice of the times and corrupted by the envy of men—be both awakened and restored, and returned, as if from the dead, into living Ideas?

In this matter, the ancestors of Your Illustrious Excellency, the Kings of France, have always shone and earned for themselves perpetual praise. It is clearer than light that Your Illustrious Excellency follows in their footsteps, as you see to it with the greatest diligence that these books are brought into France for the common use and benefit of all. Therefore, first, this book of Practice...

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface to the reader by Gerard Dorn, following his formal dedication to his patron. While the previous page focused on a specific gift of the "Ethics," Dorn now addresses the general audience to explain his editorial process—specifically why he has condensed Paracelsus’s massive "Practice" into a smaller volume and his reasons for bringing these secrets to France.</meta>

-># GERARD DORN’S PREFACE TO THE READER.<-

**M**aybe you will wonder, friend reader, that the *Practice* <note>original: "Practicam"; refers to the practical application of Paracelsus’s medical and alchemical theories.</note> of Paracelsus—a mass almost immeasurable—is reduced here into an atom <note>Dorn uses the word "atom" in the classical sense of something indivisible or a very small, concentrated point, highlighting his effort to condense a vast body of work.</note>. If, however, you consider the goal of our study, I hope you will not take it ill, nor will you disapprove of these labors of mine, which I have willingly undertaken for your sake. For in these past days, while I was in France, I saw that many were complaining about the confused sequence of this writing, and not without cause. But it escaped their notice that scarcely a thousandth part <margin>A bracket in the original margin highlights the following four lines, emphasizing the scarcity of authentic Paracelsian texts.</margin> of this author's writings has come to light until now, and even that which has appeared is mutilated and severed from its members, so that one could hardly gather the fragments or crumbs falling from the table. 

We enjoy these fragments for the time being until greater things may happen. The Almighty Lord has procured for us no small favor in this matter from the Most Illustrious Prince Francis of Valois <note>Francis, Duke of Anjou and Alençon (1555–1584), the youngest son of King Henry II of France and Catherine de' Medici.</note>, son of the King, now in France. Through his virtues and authority, it will eventually be possible to see in France far greater and more excellent monuments of Paracelsus than these which I brought there—not without some reproof from certain of our fellow Germans. Those people were saying that I ought not to tear away the secrets of a German man from his native soil to other nations through translations. When they saw that I did not desist from my undertaking, but rather proceeded more eagerly—by what guidance I do not quite know—they themselves also began to translate this man's books. I see that nothing more pleasant could have happened to me than to see my efforts carried further in this way; for my primary aim is that, not only in Germany but throughout the whole world (if it were possible), the sick might be helped in a brotherly spirit. But let me return to where I left off...

                                                                           And <note>The catchword "Ea" (And/Those) at the bottom of the page indicates the first word of the next page.</note>

<summary>Gerard Dorn explains his efforts to condense and translate Paracelsus’s voluminous and disorganized writings for a wider audience, defending himself against German critics who wished to keep these medical secrets within their own borders.</summary>
<keywords>Gerard Dorn, Paracelsus, medical practice, translation, France, Francis of Valois, intellectual property, medical ethics, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes Gerard Dorn’s preface to the reader. It follows his discussion of the scarcity of Paracelsus’s authentic writings and his motivation for condensing the "Practice" into a portable volume for the benefit of students and physicians.</meta>

This was my intention: namely, to collect the *Practice* <note>original: "Practicam"; the application of medical treatments and recipes, as opposed to the underlying philosophy.</note>—which is currently scattered across various small books—into a single volume. By doing so, the reader staying at home may find right away (as if from an index) the specific place where you ought to consult the author’s mind in the *Theory* <note>original: "Theorica"; Paracelsus's philosophical and cosmological framework for medicine.</note> regarding individual diseases. Or, if you are traveling abroad, you may accomplish as much with this single book as if you were carrying all the others with you.

For this reason, I have left a sufficiently wide margin for you next to each recipe. In this space, you are permitted to write down, at your own discretion, annotations drawn from the *Theory* with your pen. I have also destined—God willing—the remaining books of the *Practice* and *Theory* to be completed according to this same order. 

Nor did I think it enough simply to provide you with this; rather, wherever anything particularly obscure occurs, I have revealed it through certain elucidations and brief interpretations, according to my ability. As soon as I finish the *Practice* (of which you now hold this first book), you will see me proceed to the *Theory* itself. I will strictly preserve the order established by our author, especially in medical matters; for he maintains with the most invincible arguments that the *Practice* must be sought from the *Theory*, as if from the <term>light of nature</term> <note>original: "luce naturae"; a core Paracelsian concept referring to the innate wisdom and divine knowledge imprinted in the natural world, which a physician must learn to "read."</note>. 

In the meantime, kind reader, use and enjoy these works as your own, at least until we prepare more. If I perceive that these efforts are pleasing to you, I will not allow the rest to be delayed any longer, provided that we have an abundance of them available. Farewell, and consider me to be as bound to you as I know you are bound to this medical profession.

[*]

<summary>Gerard Dorn explains his editorial decision to condense Paracelsus’s scattered practical recipes into a single, portable volume with wide margins for student notes. He emphasizes the necessity of linking medical practice to Paracelsian theory and the "light of nature."</summary>
<keywords>Gerard Dorn, Paracelsus, Practice vs. Theory, medical recipes, Light of Nature, medical education, book portability</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the transition from the legal front matter (the Imperial privileges and copyright protections from the previous page) to the actual beginning of the medical treatise. We move from the administrative voice of the Emperor's court to the professional voice of the physician, starting "Book One" of the practical cures.</meta>

1.

<note>A decorative border of repeating floral motifs frames the top of the page, signaling the start of the main text.</note>

# ON THE RESTORED TRUE PRACTICE
## OF BOTH MEDICINES <note>The "Both Medicines" refers to the two prevailing schools of the era: Galenic (traditional herbal/humoral medicine) and Spagyric (chemical/alchemical medicine influenced by Paracelsus).</note>
# BOOK ONE.

->Containing the method for curing diseases in alphabetical order.<-

### A
**The Cure for Corroding Ulcers.** <note>original: "Aesthiomenum," a term derived from the Greek word for "eating," referring to gangrenous or phagedenic ulcers that rapidly consume the surrounding flesh.</note>

Take: 3 ounces of Orpiment <note>original: "Auripigmenti," a deep yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used in both alchemy and medicine.</note>.
6 ounces of Burnt Alum.
Reduce them and let an alkali be made.

**Additions may be made.**
To 6 ounces of this preparation, [add]:
2 ounces of Mummy Liquor <note>original: "Liquoris mumiæ," a substance obtained from embalmed human remains, highly prized in early modern pharmacy for its supposed life-preserving properties.</note>.
1.5 ounces of Rose Oil.
Musk <note>or perhaps moss</note> of darkness, and Psyllium seeds up to the total weight of all the other ingredients. Let a poultice or ointment be made from these.
If, however, burning or inflammation is present, the ulcers should be smeared with camphor oil before this ointment or poultice is applied.
From the *Book of Preparations in Arsenic*.

**The Cure for Burns.**

Take: as much fresh butter as you wish, and once melted...

...once melted, throw it into fresh, cold water and stir it with a wooden spatula until it turns white. Afterward, discard the water and let the substance be kept in the form of an ointment. Afterward, let it be treated with ointments made from butter or pig lard and applied until the heat is fully gone. Once this is achieved, it should be healed with the stictic ointments for miners, made with colophony <note>original: "colophonia," a sticky, yellow-to-black resin derived from pine trees, used to help ointments adhere to the skin.</note>.

# BOOK ONE. 3

Burns caused by Mercury mainly occur during amalgamations and the sublimations of cinnabar <note>original: "cinabaris," mercury(II) sulfide, a common pigment and ore from which mercury was extracted.</note>, from which workers sometimes contract tooth pain or tremors of the limbs <note>These are recognized today as classic symptoms of mercury poisoning, which was a frequent occupational hazard for early modern alchemists and miners.</note>. The teeth should be washed with lavender water. The limbs, however, should be washed with aqua vitae <note>original: "aqua vitæ," literally "water of life," referring to distilled alcohol used as a medicinal solvent.</note>. Afterward, stictic plasters <note>original: "sticticis emplaſtris," specialized medicinal patches intended to seal wounds and "stitch" the skin together.</note> should be used as in other burns.

*The cure for burns arising from salt, vitriol, and alum.*

Take <note>The original uses the symbol ℞, the traditional apothecary's abbreviation for "Recipe" or "Take."</note>: Nut oil, half a pound <note>original: "lb. ß."; "lb" is the libra or pound, and "ß" is the symbol for "semis," meaning half.</note>.
Deer suet, 1 pound.
Butter quenched in water, 1 and a half pounds.
From these ingredients mixed together, let an ointment be made, with which they should be treated twice every day until the heat is extinguished; afterward, let them be further treated with wound-healing ointments, balms, or powders, so that smaller scars are left behind by the treatment. Such burns are very frequent among dyers, because of the alums they use <note>Alum was used as a mordant to fix dyes to cloth, but in its concentrated form, it could cause severe skin irritation and chemical burns.</note>.

# BOOK ONE

Juice of nightshade <note>original: "solani," likely *Solanum nigrum*, used in historical medicine for its cooling and soothing properties on the skin.</note>.
Juice of water lily.
Juice of crayfish, etc., equal parts of each <note>original: "ana," a medical shorthand meaning an equal portion of each ingredient listed.</note>.

Once the juices of these mixed ingredients have been squeezed through a cloth, the area should be washed and moistened with them to extinguish the heat. Afterward, it should be treated with wound-healing ointments and the plasters used by miners <note>original: "fodicatorum," referring to laborers in the pits who often suffered from chemical or heat-related injuries.</note>.

Many different cooling applications <note>original: "extinctiones," literally "extinguishings," referring to the cooling of the "fire" or inflammation in a burn.</note> can be made from the juices of snails, earthworms, frogs, river crayfish, frog spawn, stonecrop <note>original: "sedi," a succulent plant valued for its ability to hold moisture and cool the skin.</note>, nightshade, water lily, and from fallen fruit, as well as shoemaker’s ink <note>original: "atramento sutorio," a term for copperas or ferrous sulfate, which acted as an astringent.</note>. However, the one made from rose vinegar and the juice of river crayfish is the most excellent of all.

Truly, if the burn should appear lethal, the heat must be quenched from within, especially by drinking water or the juice of small Damson plums <note>original: "prunorum cypriorum minorum," a variety of plum traditionally used for cooling the internal "humors."</note>, or the juice of the parsnip plant <note>original: "pastinaccæ," which may also refer to wild carrot in early herbals</note>, milk, weak beer <note>original: "Zytho minori," literally "small zythum," an ancient style of beer; "minor" refers to a lower alcohol content or "small beer."</note>, apple juice, and so forth.

From Part 1, Book 3, Chapters 6, 7, 8, 9, and 10 of the *Great Surgery* <note>original: "Chirurgiæ magnæ," referencing the foundational medical text by Paracelsus.</note>.

# 10 ON CURING DISEASES

...of burnt [copper] <note>the previous page context suggests burnt copper or similar minerals</note> 3 pounds each. *Red powder of Antimony.*

<note>A large curly bracket in the original text groups the following instructions for preparing the salt base.</note>
First, let a Salt be made from <term>tartar</term> <note>potassium bitartrate, the sediment from wine casks</note> calcined until it reaches whiteness, then dissolved in warm distilled water. After stirring, let the mixture rest, and let the water be separated by pouring it off into another vessel. Let other fresh water, similar to the first, be poured on and separated as many times as necessary, until no more acrimony <note>sharpness or bitterness</note> is perceived in the tartar, but rather it leaves the earth "sweet" <note>neutralized of its harsh alkaline properties</note>. Let all the collected waters be driven off into the air by distillation, so that the salt of tartar remains at the bottom.

Take 3 ounces of this salt; let it be dissolved in half a pound of water. Cast in half an ounce of ground antimony; let them be boiled together for a short time, and it becomes a very clear water which, as it cools, turns red and congeals. Soon thereafter, let a little distilled vinegar be poured on, and the acrimony of the salt and the "malice" <note>original: "malitia," the toxic or impure qualities of the raw mineral</note> of the antimony will fall to the bottom. Let that liquor be poured off, and let sweet, warm distilled water be poured on several times until no acrimony is perceived by the taste. Finally, let it be dried into a most red powder. Its dose is one grain or a half.

12 ON CURING DISEASES
*The cure for common abscesses* <note>original: "Apostematum," referring to a collection of pus or a swelling</note>
*by means of Oppodeltoch.* <note>Oppodeltoch (or Opodeldoc) was a famous medical plaster or liniment popularized by Paracelsus, typically made of soap, camphor, and various oils.</note>
Take 3 ounces of litharge <note>lead monoxide</note> cooked
into a wax-salve.
Half an ounce of the four "incarnatives" <note>medicines intended to promote the growth of new flesh to heal a wound</note>.

Let a plaster be made according to the [medical] art. It would be
best to cut the veins from which they arise, to
remove the redness and the corrupted blood;
not only in these cases, but also in all others.

# BOOK ONE. 13

### The secret of the stone of the philosophers.
Take the element of mercury and separate the pure from the impure. Afterward, let it be reverberated <note>reverberation involves heating a substance in a furnace where the flame is "reverberated" or deflected back onto the material</note> until it reaches a state of whiteness. This should then be sublimated by means of sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride, often used in alchemy as a flux or to assist in sublimation</note> for as long as it takes to be resolved. Let it be calcined <note>reduced to a powder through heat</note> and dissolved again, and then digested in a <term>pelican</term> <note>a circulatory distillation vessel with two side-arms that return condensed vapors to the body of the flask, resembling a pelican pecking its own breast</note> for one month. Finally, let it be coagulated into a body. This substance is no longer consumed, nor is it used up in any way, but remains in the same state. The bodies which it penetrates become permanent in the cupellation furnace <note>original: "cineritio," a furnace or vessel used to refine precious metals using bone-ash</note>; they can by no means be reduced to nothing, nor can they be diminished or altered. This stone removes from both sensible and insensible things the lesser essence and any superfluous quality, as has been described.

### The secret of the mercury of life.
Take essentialized mercury, which you have separated from all its superfluity, just as the pure is separated from the impure. Afterward, sublimate it with antimony so that both may ascend and become one. Then, let them be resolved upon a marble slab <note>this refers to "deliquescence," where a salt absorbs enough moisture from the air to dissolve into a liquid</note> and coagulated for a fourth time. When this is finished, you will have the mercury of life, by which all things are renewed and restored.

### The secret of the tincture.
Take the essence of the members <note>this likely refers to the essential components of a substance or perhaps biological extracts</note>, from which you shall separate the elements. Afterward, you will leave their fire placed in digestion for so long until nothing more resides in the bottom, nor does any matter appear there at all in a substantial form. Next, take the matter and the glass vessel, as it is sealed with clay <note>original: "lutatum," referring to "lute," a clay or paste used to hermetically seal glass joints in alchemical apparatus</note>...

*Oil of Orpiment, or Balsam of Arsenic.*
<note>Orpiment is a deep orange-yellow arsenic sulfide mineral. While highly toxic, it was used in historical medicine as a caustic to treat skin conditions.</note>

Take: Ground orpiment; add to it twice its weight of saltpeter <note>original: "salis nitri"; potassium nitrate, used here as an oxidizing agent</note. With charcoal ignited in the open air—on account of the harmful vapors <note>a warning regarding the lethal fumes released when arsenic is heated</note>—let the <term>crucible</term> <note>original: "tigillus"; a heat-resistant container used for melting minerals</note> be heated until it glows. Cast the mixture in gradually until the entire substance of the saltpeter has ceased its bubbling <note>original: "flatus"; literally "breaths," referring to the gas released during the chemical reaction</note>. Once this is observed, increase the fire for 3 or 4 hours; from this, it will come to pass that the orpiment is seen melted at the bottom like butter. When the material has cooled, white and shining, let it be ground thoroughly upon a marble slab and dissolved into a liquor in a damp place. This may then be distilled through a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>original: "cucurbitam"; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</note>; it is most effective for any ulcers, even those that are putrid and weeping.

<note>original: "Aurum potabile"; a legendary alchemical remedy consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid, believed by early modern physicians to be a "universal medicine" capable of curing all diseases and rejuvenating the body.</note>

This powder should be washed frequently with <term>celestial water</term> <note>original: "coelesti aqua"; likely refers to highly rectified spirit of wine or pure distilled rainwater used as a solvent</note> until all saltiness is removed. Alternatively, sheets of gold may be <term>calcined</term> <note>the process of heating a metal to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or oxide</note> by means of lead sheets in a <term>cement</term> <note>a dry mixture of corrosive minerals used in the refining of precious metals</note> for 24 hours, and then washed. To the resulting powder, add enough spirit of wine so that it stands four finger-breadths above it, and let them digest in a closed vessel...

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for preparing "Potable Gold" mentioned on page 19, focusing on the various chemical methods to "calcine" or powder the gold so it can be dissolved for medicinal use.</meta>

->20 ON CURING DISEASES<-

The instruction provided requires some interpretation where it states: (of leaf gold, powdered, and dissolved into juice). By "powdered," I understand "calcined" <note>reduced to a powder through heat or chemical reaction</note>, which is usually done in various ways.

<margin>How calcined gold is made</margin> **First**, when it is mixed with heated quicksilver <note>original: "argento vivo"; mercury</note> over a moderate fire; they call this "amalgamation," a process not unknown to goldsmiths. Afterward, the whole of the quicksilver is driven into the air <note>evaporated</note> by fire, with a reddish powder of gold remaining at the bottom of the vessel.

<margin>1</margin> [A vertical bracket in the left margin groups the following lines.]
There is also another method of grinding gold: if a thin plate of it is placed over the mouth of a suitable vessel, and beneath it in the vessel, either sublimated quicksilver or molten lead is held over glowing coals so that it receives the vapor. 
<margin>2</margin> By this, the gold is rendered so brittle that it can be crushed by the fingers and reduced to a powder.

It can also be reduced to a fine dust in another way: if it is melted by the intensity of fire in a cauldron and mixed with six or more parts of its own weight of silver. 
<margin>3</margin> Afterward, having been fashioned into thin plates with hammers, both are dissolved in <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "aqua forti"; literally "strong water," referring to nitric acid</note>. All the silver passes into the liquid, while the gold settles in the form of a blackish powder. 

This powder, like the others, must be <term>reverberated</term> <note>original: "reverberandus"; a process of heating a substance in a furnace where the flames are reflected or "reverberated" down onto it</note> with <term>sal gemmae</term> <note>rock salt</note> or common salt using a stronger fire, so that it loses its metallic form (if possible), about which more will be said below. <note>In the original text, a horizontal line is drawn under the phrase "with sal gemmae or common salt to be reverberated by fire."</note> Afterward, it must be washed with common water, or as in the previous steps, with distilled vinegar—repeatedly, until it entirely lays aside the sharpness of the <term>realgaic</term> <note>original: "realgaicorum"; referring to realgar, a toxic arsenic sulfide, used here to describe the corrosive or harsh nature of the chemical vapors</note> vapors, the strong waters, and the salts. This can easily be detected by applying it to the tip of the tongue. Once this is finished, it is ready for digestion with the water of life <note>original: "aqua vitæ"; high-proof distilled alcohol</note>.

It remains... <note>original: "Restat"; this is a catchword, indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

<summary>This page explains three distinct alchemical methods for calcining gold (reducing it to a powder) using mercury, lead vapors, or nitric acid, as a prerequisite for creating "Potable Gold." It emphasizes the importance of washing away corrosive chemical residues before the gold is used medicinally.</summary>
<keywords>calcination, amalgamation, mercury, gold, aqua fortis, sal gemmae, alchemy, potable gold, purification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of chemical terminology and transitions into practical cures for sensory ailments caused by loud noises, specifically focusing on the definitions of "Anthos" and "Cheiri" used in the previous medicinal recipes.</meta>

BOOK ONE. 21

It remains for us to declare, according to the author's opinion, what is signified by *Anthos* and *Cheiri* in the formula for *aqua vitae* <note>original: "aquæ vitæ"; literally "water of life," a term for concentrated alcohol or a potent medicinal spirit</note>. Whenever plants are being discussed, he wishes nothing else to be understood by these terms than literally the plant flowers of rosemary <note>original: "roris marini"</note> and others which people commonly call "clove-scented" <note>original: "caryphillatos"</note> because of their scent. Otherwise, when minerals are treated in other places, I advise you to turn your mind toward minerals, and consider *Anthos* to be the red Elixir of gold, and *Cheiri* the white Elixir of silver. That the previous passage speaks of minerals is judged not only by the preceding materials entering the recipe, but also, where "handfuls" <note>original: "manipulis"; a traditional measurement in herbalism</note> are discussed, it can easily be gathered that physicians—both of the Spagyric <note>referring to alchemical medicine based on separating and recombining elements</note> and other schools—were accustomed to measuring mineral powders by weight, not by handfuls.

Book on Contractions, Treatise 2, Chapter 2.

*The cure for ringing in the ears and deafness, and for dullness of vision, resulting from the noise of cannons or the sounding of bells.*

The hanging parts of the ears <note>the earlobes</note> should be pricked while in a bath, so that blood may flow out. If it does not help, cupping glasses <note>original: "ventosæ"; a medical practice using suction to draw blood or fluids to the surface</note> should be applied behind the ears. If it still does not help, an incision of the vein should be made under the tongue. After this, there is no other remedy.

For afflicted eyes and weakened vision, a frequent fomentation <note>a warm, moist medicinal application</note> should be made of vetch <note>a type of legume</note> and barley flour cooked in water, by holding such poultices over 
b 3 the eyes.

**Gold of Life.** <note>Known in Latin as *Aurum Vitae*, this was a prestigious and potent alchemical medicine made from gold and mercury, often used as a "universal" remedy.</note>

Let an amalgam be made from two parts of cleansed mercury and one part of purified "sun" <note>original: "solis"; the alchemical name for gold</note>. The mercury that is not amalgamated should be squeezed out through goatskin; let it be amalgamated and squeezed again, and this so many times until seven or eight ounces of mercury are combined with one ounce of gold. Let the amalgam be dissolved in *aqua fortis* <note>original: "aqua forti"; literally "strong water," referring to nitric acid</note>, made from two parts of vitriol <note>sulfate minerals</note> and one part of saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note>, and purified with silver plates; of which the portion should be two ounces. Let it be separated by distillation and poured back on again, doing this by "cohobating" <note>the process of repeatedly distilling a liquid off the solid matter and pouring it back over the residue to concentrate its "virtue"</note> five times. In this manner, a very deep red powder is produced, which, when placed in a covered melting pot and left among glowing coals until it glows moderately bright, is then removed. Once cooled, it is washed with rose water, dried, and kept for use. The dose of it is: for youths, the weight of half a *denarius* <note>a historical unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 1.5 grams</note>; for the strong and for adults, a whole *denarius*. It is used for obstructions, skin diseases, the "venereal disease" <note>original: "morbum venereum"; typically referring to syphilis</note>, the plague, leprosy, etc. It is for the most desperate ulcers, whether it is taken by mouth or mixed into ointments and plasters.

### ON THE CURE OF DISEASES
A most certain cure for cancer and fistulas.

Take: 1 ounce of the greatest prepared Mercury <note>original: Mercurij maximi; likely a highly refined or "fixed" form of mercury used in chemical medicine</note>.
1 *verto* <note>original: vertonem; a measure defined in the text as 3 ounces</note> of oil of bricks <term>Oil of bricks: a substance produced by heating bricks soaked in olive oil to high temperatures, used for joint and skin ailments</term>. <insert>3 ounces</insert>
Half a pound of turpentine. <insert>below</insert>
2 ounces of cloves, added by mixture.
Let them be mixed and distilled by means of a water bath <term>Water bath: also known as a *balneum Mariae*, a vessel of water used to heat another container gently and evenly</term>; to the distillate let there be added afterward:
Mastic,
Myrrh,
Frankincense, 3 ounces of each.
1 *verto*—that is, 3 ounces—of mummy <term>Mummy: bituminized remains used in early modern medicine for their supposed drying and healing properties</term>.
When all are mixed together, let them be distilled again through an alembic <term>Alembic: a type of still used for distillation</term> into an oil; to 1 ounce of this, let there be added 1 ounce of the prepared Mercury mentioned above in the recipe. It is to be understood that the mercury should not be mixed with the others in the second distillation, but only in the first.
Let a liniment be made from all these, with which the said ulcers are to be anointed. Afterward, let them be bound with a stictic plaster <term>Stictic plaster: a "healing" or "scar-forming" adhesive plaster used to close wounds and promote scabbing</term>.
However, the wash described below should be used before the liniment mentioned above.

->The Wash<-
Take: Half an ounce of alum <note>a mineral salt used as an astringent to dry out wounds</note>.
1 ounce of salt.

BOOK ONE. 33

of the recognized [tincture] 1 pound. Let it be placed in a glass covered with a **blind alembic** <note>original: *alembico cæco*; a distillation lid without a spout, which forces vapors to condense and fall back into the vessel rather than being collected elsewhere.</note>, and together with it 2 ounces of the **quintessence of gold** <term>Quintessence: the "fifth essence" or purest spiritual component of a substance, extracted through repeated distillation.</term>. To these let 1/2 ounce of the **greater circulate** essence be poured <term>Circulatum Maius: a high-level alchemical solvent, often associated with the essence of sea salt, used to dissolve and spiritualize metals.</term>. Let all these be digested together in **horse dung** <note>original: *in fimo*; fermenting manure was used as a natural, steady heat source for "digestion" or slow cooking.</note> for a month, and afterward in ashes for one month, with a slow fire, so that night and day the vapors may ascend and descend. Afterward, let the fire be increased so that certain drops adhere and fall back drop by drop for two months. Finally, let them remain in horse dung for four months, so that they may have their digestion without interruption; and the **Elixir of Balsam** will be completed, which works upon the spirit of life of man no differently than leaven works in dough, and it introduces a living tincture into the body.

*The Elixir of Salt, a preservative for the body.*

Take the best prepared Salt, whitest and cleanest. Let it be placed in a **pelican** <note>original: *pellicanum*; a specialized circulating vessel with two "arms" that lead from the top back into the body, resembling a pelican pecking its own breast.</note>, with such a quantity of solvent water that it exceeds the weight of the salt six times over. Let them be digested together in horse dung for one month. Afterward, let the solvent water be separated by distillation and poured on again, and separated as before, and this so many times until the salt is converted into an **oil**. Let an eighth part of the quintessence of gold be added, and let them be digested together in a pelican and in a **horse's belly** <note>original: *in equino ventre*; another term for the warm, moist heat of a dung bath.</note> for four months. Afterward, let them be circulated for a month. Then, let another part of **circulated wine** <note>likely highly rectified spirit of wine, or brandy distilled many times.</note> be added, and let them remain in **ascent** <note>the state of being vaporized and condensed</note> for yet another month. That time having passed, the Elixir of Salt will be fulfilled, of no less virtue in conserving and preserving the body than the Elixir of Balsam.

34      ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES
        The Elixir of Sweetness.
Take **Troni** <note>original: *Troni*; in alchemical texts, this likely refers to a specific mineral salt or a "celestial" substance collected from the air or dew.</note> as much as you wish, let it be placed in a **pelican** <note>original: *pellicanum*; a specialized glass vessel with tubes that return vapors to the base, allowing for continuous "circulation" of the substance.</note>, and let it be exposed to the sun for **digestion** <note>Slow, gentle heating used to break down or "mature" a substance.</note> for two months—this is best done during the summer. Afterward, let a fourth part of the **quintessence of gold** be added, and in this manner let them be circulated together for two months. Keep this.

        The Elixir of Quintessences.
Take of the **Quintessence of Celandine** <note>original: *Chelidoniæ*; a flowering plant traditionally used to treat the liver and eyes.</note>,
   and the **Quintessence of Melissa** <note>Lemon balm.</note>, 2 ounces of each.
   **Quintessence of Gold**.
   **Quintessence of Mercury**. 1 and 1/2 ounces of each.
   **Quintessence of Saffron**,
   **Quintessence of Myrobalans** <note>An astringent fruit used in historical medicine as a restorative and purgative.</note>
        of all types, 1 ounce of each.
Let all these, mixed together, be placed in a closed **blind alembic** <note>original: *alembicum cæcum*; a distillation lid without a spout, which forces vapors to condense and fall back into the vessel rather than being collected elsewhere.</note>, and let them remain in the digestion of the sun for two months. Afterward add,
   **Quintessence of wine**.
   **Magistery** <term>Magistery: a potent medicinal preparation where the raw material is purified and "mastered" but remains a whole substance rather than just an extracted essence.</term> of the same, 1/2 ounce of each.
And let them be digested again for a month as described above. Keep this as a treasure for the preservation and restoration [of health].

        The Elixir of Subtlety.
Take of olive oil, of honey,
   and of **burning wine** <note>original: *Vini ardentis*; high-proof distilled alcohol or Aqua Vitae.</note>, 1 pound of each.
Distill them together according to chemical practice, and do this three times. Afterward, separate all the **phlegm** <note>The watery, inactive byproduct of distillation.</note> from the oils, which are distinguished by their colors. You should place all these colored oils into a pelican, adding to them the quintessence of

### *The Corallated or Coralline of Mercury*

**Mercury** is prepared according to the process of its **ladanum** <note>original: *ladani*; likely referring to a resinous preparation or a specific Paracelsian chemical process</note>, and it is soaked in **rectified water of vitriol** <note>Distilled sulfuric acid</note>, as is customarily done with **spirit of tartar**. Finally, it is distilled away for as long as it takes until it becomes red, like **coral**. The **dosage** is 1 grain, taken with **Mithridate** or **Theriac** <note>Two famous ancient poly-pharmaceutical "cure-alls" used as vehicles for potent drugs</note> according to the requirements of the disease.

->*Or Otherwise.*<-

Water distilled from **egg whites** is congealed, and once ground, it is soaked in another water drawn by distillation from egg whites and half a part of **eggshells**. It is distilled off and poured back on as many times as needed until it takes on a **red and coral color**. Afterwards, having been thoroughly infused with **spirit of wine**, it is **digested** <note>Heated gently over a long period</note> until it sheds its color into the said spirit, which is then separated from the **coralline essence**. The dosage of this is 3 drops, to be drunk in the noblest wine, for the purpose of fundamentally uprooting **gout** <note>original: *podagram*</note> and extirpating the **venereal disease** <note>original: *venereum morbum*; usually referring to syphilis</note>.

### *Balsam or Oil of Copper*

This is to be converted back into vitriol <note>A sulfate mineral, likely copper sulfate in this context</note>; that into water, and its **colcothar** <note>The reddish residue, or "dead head," left after the distillation of vitriol, often used as a potent medicinal or alchemical substance</note> into a red oil. ### *A Cure for Affected Skin by Means of an Ointment*

**Take** 7 plants each of both types of **Serpentine** <note>original: *Serpentinæ vtriusque*; likely referring to *Polygonum bistorta* (Bistort) and *Dracunculus vulgaris* (Dragon Arum), both named for their snake-like appearance or use in treating bites</note>.

Master Raphael.
He rejects the rest as apostates. <note>Paracelsus was famously critical of contemporary medicine and often fell out with his students and peers, leading to a very small "authorized" circle.</note>

->Great Surgery, Part 5, Book II, Chapter XXII.<-

### *A Cure for Toothaches*

**Take** water-pepper <note>original: *persicaria*; a plant known for its pungent, "peppery" heat</note> and henbane bark <note>original: *iusquiamus*; a member of the nightshade family used historically as a powerful anesthetic and sedative</note>, as much as you like; boil them in a decoction of rose vinegar. Wash the teeth with the liquid and hold it very hot in the mouth until it cools; take it hot again, and do this frequently, and all pain will be removed. <margin>superstitious</margin> What remains of the herbs from the decoction should be buried in a damp place and weighed down with a heavy stone so that it rots; once it has rotted, all pain will be deadened <note>original: *mortificatus*; literally "killed" or "made dead"</note>, so much so that it is observed never to return again.

->From the Little Book on the Preparation of Persicaria<-

### *A Cure for Diarrhea through Cachymy*

**Take** prepared cachymy <note>original: *cachimiæ*; a Paracelsian term for the "scum" or mineral dross found in ore, considered an immature form of metal with unique healing properties</note>, as much as you wish to use for dysentery, and a little nutmeg oil to incorporate it. Let it be reduced by the second degree of heat <note>A medium heat, often achieved using a sand bath, stronger than a water bath but not a direct flame.</note> into the form of a bolus <note>a large, soft medicinal pill.</note>

### *An Addition*

**To 1 drachm** of this prepared cachymy, add **3 drachms of theriac.** <note>original: *teriacæ*; a famous "universal" herbal jam containing dozens of ingredients, often including opium and viper flesh.</note> Let them be formed into a paste. The dose by mouth is from 1 to 2.5 drachms, taken in the morning, around noon, and in the evening. 

Afterward, for the next three days, this paste must not be taken. When those days have passed, let it be given again three times in a single day as described above. Repeat this process until the third cycle is completed.

->From the Book of Preparations, "On Cachymy"<-

### *Balsam of Hellebore, which I consider to be the soul from the animated body* <note>original: *Ellebori Balsamus quem puto animam ab animato*; this reflects the alchemical belief that the "balsam" is the life-preserving essence or "soul" extracted from the physical plant matter.</note>

**Take the herb and root of Black Hellebore.** <note>original: *Ellebori nigri*; a potent and toxic plant used as a violent purgative in ancient medicine, here refined through alchemical means.</note> Let them be **crushed in their own juice** and placed in the **heat of smoke** <note>original: *fumi calore*; a very gentle, steady heat such as that found in a chimney or a specialized furnace.</note> in a vessel that is very well closed and sealed.

Afterward, let the liquid part be separated from the putrefied matter. Then, let the putrefied matter be separated once more from the dregs, so that they are resolved into a single body. At that point, it will be a Balsam, possessing two degrees of power in the liquid and twenty-two degrees in the putrefied substance. <note>This "grading" refers to the concentrated potency of the extract after the process of putrefaction (decomposition), which alchemists believed released the plant's true medicinal power.</note>

The dose of the putrefied part should be measured according to its "degrees," while the dose of the liquid part should be measured by volume.

### *Separations of the Elements, and first from Metals.*

<margin>
**Strong water**
<note>original: *Aqua fortis*; a historical term for nitric acid, a powerful corrosive used to dissolve metals.</note>
</margin>

Let "strong water" be made from equal parts of saltpeter, vitriol, and alum. Pour the distillate back onto its own dregs and repeat the distillation. Clarify the liquid by throwing in small plates of silver. Let sal ammoniac <term>sal armoniacum</term> (ammonium chloride, used in alchemy as a flux and solvent) be dissolved in it. 

Through this water, you will dissolve metal plates thrown into it, which are then separated by means of a water bath <term>balneum mariae</term> (a "Mary’s bath," or double boiler, used for gentle, even heating). Pour the distilled liquid back and separate it again; do this as many times as necessary until you find an oil at the bottom. 

This oil will be:
* From **Gold** <note>original: *ex Sole*; the Sun was the alchemical symbol for gold</note>: a reddish-brown color.
* From **Silver** <note>original: *ex Luna*; the Moon was the symbol for silver</note>: an azure blue.
* From **Iron** <note>original: *ex Marte*; Mars was the symbol for iron</note>: a red and very dark color.
* From **Mercury**: white.
* From **Lead** <note>original: *ex Saturno*; Saturn was the symbol for lead</note>: a livid and leaden color.
* From **Tin** <note>original: *ex Ioue*; Jupiter was the symbol for tin</note>: yellow.
* From **Copper** <note>original: *ex Venere*; Venus was the symbol for copper</note>: entirely green.

Note that the metals must first be prepared: mercury must be sublimated, lead must be calcined <term>calcinandus</term> (reduced to a powder by heat), copper must be "turned into flowers" <note>original: *florificanda*; referring to the creation of verdigris or surface oxides</note>, iron must be turned into "crocus" <note>original: *crocificandum*; referring to *Crocus Martis*, or red iron oxide</note>, and tin must be reverberated <note>treated in a reverberatory furnace</note>. Gold and silver yield themselves easily to the process.

Afterward...

<meta>This page continues the instructions for processing metallic oils started on page 44. It details the specific alchemical procedure for separating the four Aristotelian elements—Earth, Air, Fire, and Water—from various metals using "strong water" and controlled heat.</meta>

BOOK ONE 45

After the metals have been brought into a liquid substance in this manner, and arranged for the distinction of their elements—which can by no means happen in a metallic nature [otherwise], for every thing must first be prepared for the use to which it is suited: To one part of the metallic oil mentioned above, add two parts of fresh strong water <note>original: *aquae fortis*; nitric acid, a powerful corrosive used to dissolve metals</note>, and having stored them together in a well-closed glass vessel, place it in horse manure <note>original: *fimum equinum*; decomposing manure was used by alchemists to provide a steady, gentle, and "living" heat for long-term digestion</note> for one month. 

Afterward, you shall distill it thoroughly with a slow fire, so that the material is coagulated at the bottom and the strong water rises, which you shall distill by means of a water bath <term>balneum</term> (a vessel of hot water used to heat a secondary container gently). In this manner, two elements are obtained; but not from all metals in an equal way: 

For from **Gold**, earth and water remain in the water bath, but air is present in all three of these: the element of fire, however, remains at the bottom, because the substance or the tangibility of gold is coagulated from fire. Therefore, the substance agrees with that which is substantial. 

From **the Moon** <note>original: *luna*; the alchemical name for Silver</note>, the element of water will remain at the bottom, and in the water bath, the elements of earth and fire. For from the moist and the cold is the substance, or the "corporeity" of the Moon (if I may speak so) derived, which indeed is of a fixed nature, so that it cannot be raised. 

From **Mercury**, fire remains at the bottom, but earth and water are raised upward. 

From **Venus** <note>original: *venere*; the alchemical name for Copper</note>, fire also remains at the bottom, and both earth and water are indeed in the water bath. 

From **Saturn** <note>original: *Saturno*; the alchemical name for Lead</note>, the element of earth remains at the bottom, while fire and water are held in the water bath. 

From **Jupiter** <note>original: *Ioue*; the alchemical name for Tin</note>, air remains at the bottom; fire, water, and earth are raised from it.

It is therefore to be noted that air exhibits a body in Jupiter, but in no other metal whatsoever. Although

Now let us come to the remains or residues. The physical element which remained at the bottom should be reduced into an oil by means of fresh strong water <note>original: *aquam fortem*; nitric acid, a powerful corrosive solvent</note> and a water bath <term>balneum</term> (a gentle heating method using a vessel of hot water); and this element will be perfected: keep it aside.

You shall separate the others in this manner and by means of a sand bath <term>balneum arenae</term> (a vessel filled with sand heated from below to provide a steady, higher temperature than water). Place those in the sand, urging them with a light fire; first, indeed, the water will be raised, and afterward the fire, which is distinguished from the water by its color—in cases where those two, namely fire and water, had settled. If, however, the elements of earth and water had remained at the bottom, the water ascends upward first, and afterward the earth. If earth and fire had resided at the bottom, the earth first, and afterward the fire is raised. But if water, fire, and earth had remained together at the bottom, the water ascends first, the fire afterward, and finally the earth. These must be kept in specific glass vessels, each one separately in its own nature: as from the Sun <note>original: *ex sole*; the alchemical name for Gold</note> comes the "hot and dry" without any other property; likewise the "cold and moist," the "cold and dry," and so it is to be understood regarding the others. It must not be ignored that the corrosive nature of the strong water must be extracted, just as we set forth in the book on the *Fifth Essence* <term>quinta essentia</term> (the "quintessence" or purest medicinal core of a substance).

BOOK ONE. 47

Of talc, garnet, cobalt, or another [mineral], take 1 pound. Of salt of nitre <note>original: *salis nitri*; saltpeter, used as a flux and oxidizing agent</note> take the same amount. Crush them into small pieces, burn them together, and draw them off through an alembic, out of the cucurbit <note>original: *cucurbitam*; the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a still</note>, and whatever has ascended, save this. What remains at the bottom, once ground, should be dissolved into water using strong water <note>original: *aqua forti*; nitric acid</note>, to which you will pour the previously separated water, and distill it into an oil, as you heard regarding the metals. By their process, you will separate the elements of these in the same way, so that golden marcasite <note>original: *marcasita*; in this period, a general term for various metallic ores, often iron pyrites</note> is to be treated in place of gold, and likewise silver [marcasite] in place of silver: lead-like ores as Saturn <note>alchemical symbol for lead</note>, copper-like as Venus, talc-like as Jupiter <note>alchemical symbol for tin</note>, mercurial as Mercury, and so on.

This process suffices for marcasites of every kind.

### Separations of the Elements from Stones.

Take the stone, very well ground, to which you shall add twice as much native sulfur <note>original: *sulphuris viui*; raw, naturally occurring sulfur</note>. All these being mixed together, placed in a luted <term>luted</term> (sealed with "philosopher's mud" or clay to make it airtight) cauldron and in an athanor <term>athanor</term> (a furnace designed to provide steady, prolonged heat), let them be urged for four hours, so that all the sulfur is consumed. Afterward, what remains should be washed of the dregs and the sulfur. Let this stony calx <term>calx</term> (the powder remaining after a substance has been heated or "calcined") be dried, dissolved in strong water, and proceeded with as we spoke of before regarding metals. 

<margin>Analogy of Stones to Metals</margin> Therefore, stones are compared to metals: thus clear gems, which are neither white nor dark, are to be compared to Gold. But white, blue, and sea-green ones are compared to the Moon <note>alchemical symbol for silver</note>. More common stones, however, correspond to other metals: such as Alabaster to Saturn [lead], marble to Iron, flint to Jupiter [tin], and those that are fragile, not solid, and spongy to Mercury.

Elementorum <note>catchword: Elements</note>

->48 ON CURING DISEASES.<-
*Separations of the elements from*
*oily substances.*

Take oily woods, roots, seeds, fruits, and the like, crushed, ground, or in whatever way broken down into small pieces; let these be wrapped in linen cloth and tied, then placed in horse dung <note>Horse dung was used by early chemists as a source of steady, gentle heat for fermentation or "putrefaction."</note> so that they may completely putrefy—which happens more quickly for some than for others. Once they have putrefied and been placed in a cucurbit <term>cucurbit</term> (the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a still), pour in common strong water <note>original: *aquae fortis*; typically nitric acid</note> just enough so that it rises four fingers' breadth above them, and let everything that can rise be distilled in a sand bath <note>original: *in arena*; a vessel filled with sand heated by a furnace to provide a uniform, high temperature</note>. All the elements except for earth will rise, and they are distinguished by their colors: first "burning water" <note>original: *ardens aqua*; likely ethanol or a volatile spirit</note> ascends, afterward air, then water, finally fire, and the earth remains at the bottom. <margin>A vertical bracket is drawn in the left margin next to the text from "just enough so that it rises" down to "as shown below."</margin> Pure oils, however, do not require putrefactions; their elements are to be separated simply by distillation alone, and are also to be recognized by their distinct colors. This same process should be followed for resins of a liquid substance, such as pitch, resin, turpentine, gum, and the like. But solid resins must first be prepared, as shown below.

*Separations of the elements from*
*solid resins.*

Take sulfur, ground very finely; let this be cooked in twice its weight of linseed oil into a "liver" <note>original: *hepar*; a chemical term for a dark, sulfurous mass resembling the color of a liver.</note>, which you shall grind; and, enclosed in a bladder, let it be placed in horse dung to putrefy for four weeks. Afterward, let it be distilled through an alembic <term>alembic</term> (the cooling cap or head of a still) slowly over an open fire. First of all, air will rise,

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for observing the chemical separation of the four elements (Fire, Air, Water, and Earth). # FIRST BOOK. 49

...air, and water with various pale colors. Afterward, once the fire has been increased, the element of fire will ascend, while the earth remains at the bottom. The colors appear pure: the air is yellow; the water is as thick as milk, so much so that it can hardly be distinguished from actual milk; the fire is like a glowing ruby, transparent and showing every sign of its fiery nature; but the earth is entirely black and scorched. Each element is thus separated into its own proper "complexion" <note>In Galenic medicine and alchemy, a "complexion" refers to the specific balance of qualities—hot, cold, moist, dry—inherent to a substance.</note>, perfect and without any mixing.

<margin>
*Separations of the elements from herbs.*
</margin>

Take sage, or another herb, and crush its leaves. Then, place them to putrefy within a sealed bladder <note>original: *vesica*; this could refer to a literal animal bladder or a bladder-shaped glass vessel used for airtight fermentation.</note>. Afterward, you shall distill it using a "horse's belly" <note>original: *ventrem equinum*; a common alchemical term for a digestion process powered by the steady, gentle heat of a heap of fermenting horse dung.</note>. First, the element of fire will ascend until the colors begin to change. Eventually, a thickness of water follows, and after that, the greatest part of the earth, though some portion of the earth remains fixed at the bottom. 

Take this water (which is now mixed) and distill it in the sun for six days. Afterward, using a bath <note>original: *balneum*; likely a *balneum Mariae* or water bath, which provides a more controlled heat than a direct flame.</note>, the element of water will ascend first; this is small in quantity and can be identified by its taste. Once the color changes, the element of fire ascends until the taste is also altered. Finally, a part of the earth is raised upward—though only a very small amount—and the other part of the earth remains at the bottom. Air, however, remains mixed with all the other elements. 

This same process applies to other "airy" and "aquatic" herbs, in which the air ascends first, followed by the water, and lastly the earth, etc., just as in the process described for Sage.
