50 ON CURING DISEASES.
*Separations of the elements from fleshy substances.*

In those bodies that live by blood, such as living animals, the predominant element is not only the most abundant but also the most excellent. For example: in fish, the element is water; in worms, it is fire; and in edible meats, it is air, and so on.

*From fish.*
Once they have putrefied, let them be thoroughly distilled using a "horse's belly" <note>original: *ventrem equinum*; an alchemical technique where the vessel is buried in a heap of fermenting horse manure to provide a gentle, steady heat for digestion.</note>, and a great deal of water will ascend. Repeat this putrefaction and distillation until no more water is willing to rise. Afterward, distill what remains through sand <note>original: *per arenam*; a sand bath, which allows the chemist to reach higher temperatures than a water bath without breaking the glass.</note>; then finally, the fire will ascend in the form of an oil, while the earth remains at the bottom. In this way, the entire substance of the fish is separated into its elements. The same process applies to other such things.

*Separations of the elements from watery,*
*juicy, or aqueous substances.*

♃ <note>The symbol for Jupiter, often used in these manuscripts to denote tin or, occasionally, a specific stage of a process or a new recipe heading.</note>. Take urine or another excrement and drive it through distillation; the water, air, and earth will ascend together, but the fire remains at the bottom. Afterward, mix everything back together and distill again; repeat this four times in the same manner. In the fourth distillation, the water will ascend first, then the air and the fire, while the earth will remain at the bottom.

♃. Finally, place the air and fire in a specific vessel and set it in a cold place; certain small spikes <note>original: *pinnacidiola*; likely referring to needle-like crystals that "point" upward like small pinnacles.</note> will congeal, just like those formed from saltpeter <note>original: *sale petræ*; potassium nitrate, a common salt in early chemistry known for forming long, distinct crystals.</note>, and these are the element of fire. Although they may also congeal during the distillation itself, they certainly do so in the cold

->FIRST BOOK. 51<-

...rather in the cold. Finally, make the water boil by means of a horse’s belly <note>original: *ventrem equinum*; a technique where a vessel is buried in fermenting horse manure to provide a gentle, consistent heat</note>, and the earth itself will settle at the bottom; but that which has ascended should be putrefied for its proper time, and afterwards distilled through a water bath <note>original: *balneum*; likely a *balneum Mariae*, similar to a double boiler</note>, and the water will ascend first, and afterwards the fire <note>In alchemy, "fire" often refers to a distilled oil or spirit that carries the "heat" of the substance</note>.

Dungs, vitriol <note>a sulfate mineral</note>, tartar <note>the potassium bitartrate sediment from wine</note>, alums, salts, and other things of that juicy kind should be distilled through ash <note>original: *per cinerem*; an ash bath, which provides a medium, dry heat</note> for as long as they are no longer willing to ascend. Water and air will ascend, while the earth will remain at the bottom; afterwards, through fire <note>meaning direct, intense heat</note>, the fire will ascend, and the earth will remain at the bottom.

*It should be noted in this place that, although the four elements have been separated, four hidden elements nevertheless still remain in the earth as if fixed—just as the "dead head" remains from vitriol. Let this be sublimated with sal ammoniac and it will be resolved into an oil, in which are water and fire, and the earth itself remains substantially at the bottom. Separate the two prior elements that ascended, and the water will ascend again, and the fire will remain at the bottom.* <note>The preceding section was underlined in the original manuscript for emphasis.</note> <note>original: *caput mortuum*; literally "dead head," the worthless dregs or residue left after a chemical process. *Sal armoniaco* is ammonium chloride.</note>

Understand the same concerning tartar and salts. Many more separations of liquids are known which we shall declare in the section on transmutations. It should also be noted that in corrosive substances, earth predominates over the other elements; for which reason their elements are to be separated through ashes and sublimation.

### Separations of the elements from glass and glassy substances.

Glass must be calcined <note>burnt to a powder</note> like stones with sulfur; afterwards, they are to be washed and separated by means of saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note> and *aqua fortis* <note>nitric acid</note>, just as we spoke of marcasites <note>original: *marcasitis*; iron sulfide or similar metallic minerals</note> above on [signature] d 2.

->52 ON THE CURE OF DISEASES.<-

...as we have said. Their elements are also recognized by their colors during distillation, and not merely by their appearance to the eye. With these few words, we wish to conclude the matter of glass vessels in separation.

### *Separation of the elements from fixed substances.*

The separation of fixed things <note>in alchemy, "fixed" substances are those that resist the action of fire and do not easily evaporate</note> is done through sublimation <note>the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then becomes solid again on a cooler surface</note> as we previously taught regarding salts and liquids; with this distinction, however: that the fixed things must be calcined <note>original: *calcinanda*; reduced to a powder through high heat</note> with saltpeter <term>saltpeter (potassium nitrate), used here as a powerful flux to help break down the substance</term>, and afterwards <insert>sublimated.</insert>

### *Separation of the elements again from any separated element.*

#### *From fire.*

It must be known that from the separated element of fire, the four elements can again be separated in this manner. When the fire burns most vehemently and is kindled, let it be collected in a perfectly closed receptacle; afterwards, let it be kept in horse manure <note>original: *fimo equino*; a common alchemical method for providing a steady, gentle heat through fermentation</note> for a month. From this it will happen that in one element, you find the four elements once again. <insert>Distill through a steam bath</insert> <note>original: *balneum vaporarium*</note> and the air will ascend as a vapor, falling into the receptacle. Distill what remains through a [water] bath and the water will ascend. Finally, distill the remainder through ashes <note>original: *per cineres*; an ash bath, providing a medium-high dry heat</note>, and the fire will ascend; the earth will remain at the bottom of the vessel. We shall explain more extensively in other books what the powers of these similar elements are and why they have been declared in this manner in this place.

#### *From air.*

Let the element of air, enclosed in the best glass and sealed with the Seal of Hermes <note>an airtight seal made by melting the neck of a glass vessel shut</note>, be exposed to the sun and digested for an entire summer, in this manner

->BOOK ONE. 53<-

...while through circulation <note>the process of continuous distillation where the vapor returns to the liquid</note> it is transformed into a watery state that increases day by day, which you shall separate as you will soon hear. Let it putrefy in horse manure <note>original: *fimo equino*; a common alchemical method for providing a steady, gentle heat through natural fermentation</note> for four weeks. Afterward, you shall distill it through a water bath and ashes, just as was described regarding the element of fire. More shall be said elsewhere concerning its specific powers and virtues.

### *Separation from water.*

Let the water be kept in a glass vessel that is entirely full, without even the slightest empty space, closed with the Seal of Hermes <term>Seal of Hermes; an airtight seal made by melting the glass neck of a flask shut</term>. It should be held in very hot sand for a month, so that it receives heat daily as if it were boiling—which it cannot actually do because the vessel is completely full. Once this time has passed, let it putrefy for four weeks. Afterward, when the vessel is opened, let it be distilled through a three-beaked alembic <note>original: *alembicum trium rostrorum*; a specialized still with three exit tubes to separate different vapors at once</note>. In this way, three elements are separated, and the "earth" remains at the bottom.

### *Separation from earth.*

The process for earth is to be followed just as it was for water, with the only exception being the distillation. The distillation must be performed in the same manner as was described for the element of fire, and conducted by that same path.

### The stictic plaster, and by what means it becomes attractive.

<margin>Stictic plaster <note>original: *emplastrum sticticum*; a medicinal plaster used to close and heal wounds</note></margin>
To any stictic plaster, add whatever substances possess attractive powers <note>meaning "drawing" powers used to pull foreign objects or toxins from the body</note>, such as **Asphalt** [bitumen] and **amber** <note>original: *carabe*</note>, which attract wood, straw, and fragments of cloth. The **Magnet** attracts iron; **Tin** and **tinted amethysts** attract copper; **Alabaster** attracts lead and stones. **Gum**, **turpentine**, and **asphalt** attract bits of flesh and bone. **Serpentine** [the stone] is attractive of all things. Even though these substances have specific powers of attraction, when they are joined together, mixed into ointments, and placed over wounds, they nonetheless draw out everything all at once.
*Small Surgery, Book 1, Chapter 5.*

->*Common cure for Erysipelas*<-
<note>Erysipelas (often called "St. Anthony's Fire" in the past) is a bacterial skin infection resulting in large, red, inflamed patches</note>

Afterward, let the ulcer be washed with a decoction of **oak leaves** or salt water; keep it clean, and it will be healed no differently than if the ointment were still being applied. The nature of this medicine is such that it does not work before three weeks have passed.

->Method of extraction through the nostrils.<-

**Take:** 1 scruple each of **Darnel** <note>original: "Lolium"; a grass often considered a weed but used here for its purging properties</note> and **Nigella seed**.
1 scruple of **Hellebore** <note>A powerful and toxic plant used in small doses to provoke sneezing or purging</note>.
Half a drachm each of **Marjoram** and **Sage**.
2 grains of **Musk** <note>An aromatic substance harvested from deer, used here to round out the scent</note>.
Let a **sneezing-powder** <note>original: "sternutatorium"; a medicine meant to be inhaled to induce sneezing and clear the head</note> be made, which should be inhaled through the nose every day in the morning, a little at a time.

->Extraction through the ears.<-

**Take:** 1 scruple of **Scammony** <note>original: "Diagridium"; a resinous juice from the root of the Convolvulus scammonia plant, used as a harsh purgative</note>.
1 drachm of **Bdellium** <note>A semi-transparent oleo-gum resin similar to myrrh</note>.
1.5 scruples of **Wax**.
Let a **plaster** be made, which is to be placed on the ears in the form of a **small tablet** <note>original: "pinnacidium"; a term for a small medicinal patch or compress</note>; when they have exuded foam or water, it will be well.

->Through the urine.<-

**Take:** Half a drachm of **Oriental saffron**.
Half a drachm of **Zenith hair** <note>The text clarifies below that these are the tufts of fur from a hare's tail</note>.
5 **Spanish flies** <note>original: "Cantharidum"; dried blister beetles used in historical medicine to stimulate the urinary tract, though they are highly toxic</note>.
Let a mixture be made for a **small pouch** <note>original: "sacculo"</note> to be placed under the channel <note>The urethra</note> and beneath the bladder; it will provoke urine for one or two days, until the discharge <note>original: "sanies"; a thin, greenish-flesh colored discharge from a wound or sore</note> stops appearing in it. "Zein" are the tufts of fur found around the tails of hares.

->Through the belly or intestines.<-

**Take:** As much **Honey** as suffices.
1 scruple of **Scammony**.
1 drachm each of **Hermodactyls** <note>The root of a plant, likely meadow saffron, used as a purgative</note> and **Turpeth** <note>The root of a plant in the morning glory family, used as a strong laxative</note>.
Let there be made...

62 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES

Let **suppositories** be made according to the medical art, without salt; however, in cases of constipation, you will be able to use them.

->Through sweat.<-

**Take:** 2 drachms <note>A unit of weight; approximately 3.9 grams</note> of **Theriac** <note>original: "Teriacæ"; a legendary "universal" poly-pharmaceutical paste consisting of dozens of ingredients, used as an antidote and general cure-all</note>.
2 ounces of **Alcohol of wine** <note>original: "Alcoolis vini"; high-proof distilled spirit</note>.
7 grains of **Euphorbium** <note>The dried, caustic sap of the spurge plant, used here to provoke a strong physical reaction</note>.

Let a **draught** <note>original: "haustus"; a liquid medicine to be swallowed</note> be made to be taken before entering the bath; let the patient sweat, and afterward, being warmly covered, let him lie down in bed and sweat further. This should be repeated three or four times, and as often as necessary until the foul odor departs.

*Book on Abscesses, chapter 58.* <note>original: "Lib. de Apost. cap. lvij"</note>

->Cure for Fevers.<-

**Take:** 1 scruple <note>A unit of weight; approximately 1.3 grams</note> of **prepared Jacinths** <note>original: "Iacinthorum"; the gemstone zircon or hyacinth, which was finely ground and "prepared" for medicinal use, believed to have cooling properties</note>.
1.5 scruples of **Gum Labdanum** <note>original: "ladani"; a sticky resin obtained from Cistus shrubs</note>.

Let them be mixed and distilled. The dose is 4 or 5 drops for preservation, to be taken twice every year, and during all **conjunctions of the sun with the moon** <note>The New Moon; early modern medicine heavily emphasized astrological timing for the efficacy of "chemical" medicines</note>.

*Book of Preparations, on Gems.*

->Cure for Fevers and acute diseases.<-

**Take:** 2 ounces of **Gold leaf**, liquefied in honey-water.
The same amount of **rectified alcohol** <note>original: "Alcool correcti"; alcohol that has been purified through repeated distillation</note>.

Let the honey-water be separated. The dose is from half a drachm to 1 scruple.

->The following can be added:<-

To 4 scruples of this preparation:
2 ounces each of **Centaury juice** <note>original: "succi centaureae"; a bitter herb traditionally used to break fevers</note> and **Saltpeter** <note>original: "Salis nitri"; potassium nitrate, used in this period to "cool" the blood</note>.

The dose is from half a scruple up to 1.5 scruples.

*Book of Preparations, on Gold.*

BOOK ONE. 65

Rhubarb.
**Select Manna**. <note>original: "Mannæ electæ"; the sweet, dried sap of the flowering ash tree, used as a mild laxative.</note>

One spoonful of this water, shaken and stirred each time, should be drunk in the morning and evening, followed immediately by three spoonfuls of wine. By these little potions, a **fistula** <note>An abnormal tunnel-like passage in the body, often caused by chronic infection.</note> is cleansed internally and heals on its own, without any other medicine being applied externally. If, however, you wish to apply something, let it be this plaster.

->**Plaster for fistulas.**<-

**Take:** 2 ounces of **the four gums**. <note>A standard pharmaceutical base typically composed of galbanum, ammoniacum, opopanax, and sagapenum gums.</note>
1 pound of wax.
Let them be cooked together, with 3 ounces of **turpentine resin** <note>original: "visci de botin"; the sticky, medicinal resin of the turpentine tree, used to draw out infection.</note> added; let the plaster be made.

->FROM THE *LESSER SURGERY*, BOOK II, CHAPTER VII.<-

->**The cure of fistulas.**<-

**Take:** 1 ounce of **Orpiment**. <note>original: "Auripigmenti"; arsenic trisulfide, a bright yellow mineral. In historical medicine, it was used as a caustic agent to "eat away" diseased flesh, though highly toxic.</note>
3 ounces of **calcined tartar**. <note>Potassium carbonate produced by heating the sediment from wine barrels; used here as a chemical reagent.</note>

Let these be placed in layers and reduced in the **fourth degree of fire** <note>The highest level of heat in traditional chemistry, usually involving a glowing red furnace.</note> for 24 hours. The material was prepared in this manner: afterward, having been removed, let it be ground and cooked in water. A white powder will fall to the bottom, which is the **prepared orpiment**. When this is stored in glass or placed upon marble in a damp location, it turns into an oil. This is to be injected into fistulas by a **syringe**, or threads may be soaked in this oil, or the powder itself may be sprinkled [into the wound].

*There can be added:*
To half an ounce of this prepared orpiment:
Resin

*The cure for a sooty aposteme.* <note>original: "Fuliginosi apostematis"; likely referring to an abscess that appears dark, charred, or necrotic in color.</note>

Let a mixture be made in the manner of a plaster, to be used after the aposteme has been opened.

**Take:** **yolks of eggs**...

Afterwards, you shall treat it with an **Opodeldoc plaster** <note>original: "emplastro Oppodeltoch"; a famous medical plaster attributed to Paracelsus, used for its healing and "consolidating" properties.</note>, for the final closing of these **consuming ulcers**, etc.
From the *Great Surgery*, part 4, book 9, chapters 3 & 4.

*Cure for Gangrene.*
Use the same medicine as for a **malignant tetter** <note>original: "Tetiginem pravam"; a "tetter" was a general term for any itchy or spreading skin disease like ringworm or eczema.</note> and for **consuming ulcers**; except that to these sorts of curative and **styptic** <note>original: "sticticis"; referring to plasters that contract tissue or stop bleeding.</note> plasters, you should add to 1 pound of them: half an ounce of **scammony** <note>original: "Scamoneae"; a harsh purgative resin derived from the root of *Convolvulus scammonia*, used here for its particular ability to resist the "corrosion" of ulcers.</note>, which possesses a particular power against the corrosion of ulcers.
From the *Small Surgery*, book 2, chapter 7.

BOOK ONE. 69
->Treatment for frostbite of the limbs.<-

**Take:** **Long pepper**.
**Cardamom**.
**Grains of paradise**, <note>original: "Granorum paradyfi"; a pungent spice from West Africa related to ginger, prized for its warming properties.</note> of each 1 drachm.
**Euphorbium**, <note>original: "Euphorbii"; a highly caustic resin from the spurge plant, used here to stimulate circulation in "frozen" tissue.</note> 2 drachms.
**Mastic**, 1 and a half drachms.
Let them be powdered in a mortar, and pour in 2 measures of the **urine of boys**, or of some **choleric man**; <note>In the humoral system, a "choleric" person was considered naturally "hot and dry"; their urine was thought to possess more "heat" to counteract the cold of frostbite. The urine of prepubescent boys was a common ingredient in early modern chemistry and medicine, valued for its perceived purity and ammonia content.</note> boil them until an eighth part is consumed. Let it be strained through a cloth: with that which remains, let the frozen limb be anointed three times every day. The dead tissue shall be separated from the living, <note>original: "Separabitur à vino," likely a printer's error for "vivo" (living), as the contrast is between "mortuum" (dead) and "vivum" (living).</note> and the living part shall be treated with wound-healing ointments. Indeed, after the healthy limb has been anointed with this ointment by the fire, it will be protected from the frost for that entire day.

Similarly, with double or triple **paper** <note>original: "papyro"; likely referring to thick paper or parchment-like material.</note> soaked and wrapped around the limb, the limb is preserved from frost, with a linen cloth placed over it, for it does not allow the air and wind to penetrate.

If the frost has penetrated to the interior, so that death is imminent, let **aqua vitae** <note>original: "aqua vitę"; literally "water of life," referring to distilled spirits or concentrated alcohol.</note> mixed with **camphor** and a small amount of **theriac** <note>original: "theriaca"; an ancient, complex medicinal paste containing dozens of ingredients, used as a universal antidote and restorative.</note> be given as a drink, after which sweating should be induced. It would be better to use a noble wine boiled with **ginger** and **aromatic calamus**. 

Also, **aqua vitae** with aromatic spices and especially **saffron**: each of which, when drunk, protects those crossing frozen mountains from the frost. Uncontrolled frostbite is sometimes followed by sudden death, or leprosy, or at the very least, the itch, etc.

*The Great Surgery*, Part 1, Book 3, Chapter 12.
c 3 Glands

<summary>This page provides recipes and preventative measures for frostbite, utilizing warming spices and "hot" substances like boy's urine and theriac to restore heat to frozen limbs. It also offers advice for mountain travelers to avoid the internal "penetration" of cold which could lead to death or chronic skin conditions.</summary>
<keywords>frostbite, long pepper, grains of paradise, euphorbium, theriac, aqua vitae, Paracelsus, humoral medicine, mountain travel, warming remedies</keywords>

70 ON CURING DISEASES
*Treatment for Swollen Glands and Napta.* <note>original: "Naptae"; an archaic term for a soft tumor or cyst, often associated with fatty or sebaceous growths.</note>

It must be removed with a thread of feathery alum <note>original: "Aluminis plumosi"; a fibrous form of alum, likely used here as a caustic or astringent ligature.</note> moistened in oil of vitriol. <note>original: "oleo de gilla"; Gilla is a Paracelsian term for white vitriol or zinc sulfate, often used for its drying and healing properties.</note> Afterwards, by anointing and removing the surface, the small veins may be consolidated by the vitriol.

76    ON THE CURING OF DISEASES.

**Take:** 1.5 grains of the **greatly prepared mercury**. <note>original: "mercurij preparati"; in Paracelsian medicine, this is mercury that has been purified through chemical processes to remove its "toxicity" while retaining its "life-giving" essence.</note>
3 grains of the **quintessence of alandahal**. <note>original: "alandahal"; an Arabic-derived term used by Paracelsus for colocynth or bitter apple, a powerful and sometimes violent purgative.</note>
2 drachms of **rose sugar**.
3 grains of **saffron**.
Let them be mixed and a <term>bolus</term> <note>a soft, rounded mass of medicine, larger than a pill, intended to be swallowed.</note> be made, which should be administered three or four times, or as often as needed until the swelling subsides. Afterward, let this potion be administered morning and evening, until the pale color is transformed into a lively, healthy one.

**The cure for Dropsy and Jaundice** <note>original: "Hydropisis & ictericię"; dropsy refers to the swelling of soft tissues (edema), and jaundice to the yellowing of the skin and eyes.</note>

**Take: Rock salt**. <note>original: "Salis gemmę"; a pure, transparent form of common salt used as a base in many alchemical recipes.</note>
**Juice of tithymalus**, that is, **lesser spurge**, 1 drachm each.
**Cherry gum**, to the weight of all the other ingredients.
Let a **bolus** be made, which is allowed to be **reverberated** <note>heated in a furnace where the flame is reflected back onto the substance</note> at the third degree of fire for two hours, and let the **alkali** <note>the soluble salt or "ash" resulting from the calcination</note> be extracted. The dose is from 8 to 12 grains.
The following may be added:
To half a scruple of this **prepared salt**:
**Rebolus**, that is, **true mummy**, <note>original: "mumiæ"; in this period, "mummy" was a medicinal substance supposedly derived from embalmed bodies, used to stop bleeding and treat internal ailments.</note>
**Liquor of centaury**, 4 scruples each. <note>Centaury is a bitter herb traditionally used to treat fevers and digestive issues.</note>
Let them be mixed. The dose is from 4 to 12 grains, taken in an egg.

**The cure of other diseases by the same salt prepared in another way, as follows:**

Take 1 drachm of that **prepared salt**. 2 drachms of the **juice of catapotia**. <note>original: "catapotiaę"; likely referring to the juice of a plant used to make purgative pills, often colocynth.</note> **Wheat flour** to the weight of all the ingredients. From this, let a well-baked bread be made, of which 1 drachm should be given in 2 drachms of liquid.

*Book of Preparations, concerning rock salt.*

78 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.
*Cure for Persian fire.* <note>original: "Ignis persici"; a historical term for various inflammatory skin conditions characterized by a painful, burning sensation, such as erysipelas or anthrax.</note>

*Removal of burnt flesh from healthy flesh, by means of a plaster.*

**Take: Colophony**, 3 pounds. <note>original: "Colophoniae"; a translucent resin derived from pine trees, used to give plasters their stickiness.</note>
**Liquor of Myrrh**, 6 ounces.
**Four gums**, half a pound. <note>original: "Quatuor gummi"; typically a standard medical mixture of four resins, often galbanum, sagapenum, ammoniacum, and bdellium.</note>
Let a **plaster** be made and applied to the place where the living and burnt flesh meet; the burning will soon cease, and the living flesh will be separated from the burnt.

*Treatment for fresh flesh.* <note>Likely referring to the treatment of newly exposed tissue or fresh wounds to promote healing.</note>

**Take: Salt water from radish**, 1 ounce. <note>original: "Aquæ salis ex raphano"; a preparation using radishes, known in early medicine for their cleansing and "cutting" properties.</note>
**From culicula**, 3 [drams]. <note>original: "culicula"; likely a reference to *caulicula* (small plant stalks) or a specific distilled spirit, though the exact botanical identity is obscure in this context.</note>
**Digested juice of plantain**, 8 ounces. <note>original: "Succi plantaginis digesti"; plantain was a staple of the medieval medicine chest for treating sores; "digested" means the juice was matured through gentle heat.</note>
Once these are mixed, bind it to the area morning and evening.

*Another remedy for the same.*

**Take: Juice of celandine.** <note>original: "chelidoniæ"; a plant traditionally used to treat skin eruptions and irritations.</note>
**Water of rock alum.** <note>original: "aluminis rupei"; potassium alum, used as an astringent to dry out wounds.</note>
**Water of Yemen alum**, one and a half ounces of each. <note>original: "aluminis iameni"; a high-quality variety of alum prized for its purity.</note>
**Dissolved nitre**, 2 scruples. <note>original: "Nitri resoluti"; saltpeter dissolved in liquid, used for its cooling and cleansing effects.</note>
Mix together and use as prescribed above.

*A wax-salve for the same effect.*

**Take: Oil of wheat.** <note>original: "Olei de frumento"; wheat germ oil.</note>
**Oil of eggs**, 2 drams of each. <note>original: "Olei de ouis"; a soothing oil extracted from the yolks of hard-boiled eggs.</note>
**Oil of acorns**, 5 ounces.
**Wax.**
**Common pitch**, equal in weight to all the other ingredients combined. <note>original: "Picis communis"; a thick, resinous substance used to protect wounds and "draw out" impurities.</note>
Form into a **wax-salve** <note>original: "Ceratum"; a stiff ointment made primarily of wax and oil.</note> and use according to common practice.

*Of Burnings* <note>original: "Usionum"; the catchword indicates the next page will continue with treatments for burns or scorched skin.</note>

FIRST BOOK. 79

->*Methods for extinguishing burns.*<-

Burns caused by **nitre** <note>original: "nitro"; likely referring to saltpeter or soda ash, which can cause chemical burns.</note> are extinguished by **vitriol** <note>original: "vitriolum"; a sulfate of certain metals, such as iron or copper, which had been purified and dissolved.</note> that has been purified and dissolved. Burns from **fire** <note>original: "æthinæ"; likely a derivation from the Greek word for burning heat or a reference to Etna.</note> are treated with **naval bitumen** and **dissolved camphor** in equal weights; those from **burning wood** are treated with **myrrh** and **frankincense**.

For burns from **sulfur and resins**, use **virgin's milk** <note>original: "lac virginis"; a traditional pharmaceutical term for a milky-white liquid made by precipitating resin or lead in water.</note> drawn from sulfur.

Let cloths be soaked in these kinds of liquids and bound [to the wound]; do not allow the area to become dry, but let it always remain moist.

->*The cure for esthiomenus.*<-
<note>original: "Æstiomeni"; a historical medical term for a "gnawing" or corrosive ulcer that eats away at the flesh, often associated with gangrene or lupus.</note>

**Take: Metallic salt**, 2 scruples.
**Water of true bitumen**, 6 ounces.
When these have been mixed and **digested** for four days, let the ulcers be washed every third, fourth, or fifth day, for as long as it takes until all pain is removed. Then, finally, the following ointment must be used:

**Take: Ashes of Saturn**, half a pound. <note>original: "Cinerum ex Saturno"; Saturn is the alchemical name for lead.</note>
**White olive oil**, 6 pounds.
**Litharge prepared into a paste**, 9 ounces. <note>original: "Litargyrij"; lead monoxide, a common ingredient in historical skin salves.</note>
Let a mixture be made over **warm ashes** for half a day; bind this to the area morning and evening.

->*Another extraction of salts from ulcers.*<-

**Take: Salt of urine**, 3 ounces. <note>original: "Salis vrinæ"; likely refers to ammonium chloride or other salts extracted from distilled urine.</note>
**Metallic salt of lead**, 5 ounces.
Let them be **digested** together in **distilled vinegar**, until a ferment like an **elixir** is made from them. Mix this with a sixteenth part of **liquefied camphor**,

*The cure for fistulas.*
**Take:** Oil of iron.
Oil of Saturn <note>lead</note>, in equal parts, 1 dram.
Mercurial oil, 1 and a half scruples.
With all these mixed together, apply it around the circle of the fistula <note>a narrow passage or duct formed by disease or injury</note> morning and evening for nine days, and it will be cured.

*Plaster for fistulas.*
**Take:** Wax.
Colophony <note>a type of pine resin</note>, in equal parts, 3 ounces.
All the greater gums <note>referring to major medicinal resins such as galbanum or ammoniacum</note>, in equal parts, 1 ounce.
The four resins <note>a traditional pharmaceutical blend of four specific resins</note>, in equal parts, half an ounce.
From these, let a **cerate** <note>a waxy ointment</note> be made with oil of laurel and with turpentine. Let it be tempered into a paste that is not too hard.

*Cure for fistulas by way of a potion.*
**Take:** Water of dogwood leaves.
Water of mothwort leaves <note>original: "tinearum"; likely referring to plants used to repel moths or treat "gnawing" sores, such as tansy</note>, in equal parts, half a pound.
Water of Sophia <note>original: "Aquæ Sophiæ"; flixweed, historically used for treating wounds and sores</note>, 3 ounces.
Once mixed, let them be given to the sick to drink with an equal amount of wine.
[Reference:] *The Great Surgery*, Part 3, Book 7, Chapter 1.

->BOOK ONE. 81<-

**Myrrh** and **Frankincense**, 1 dram <note>original: "ʒ.j."; a unit of apothecary weight</note> of each.
Let them be purified <note>original: "rectificentur"; to refine or purify a substance, often through distillation or digestion</note> in the sun by digesting in a glass vessel. Place cloths soaked in this liquid over the painful part, and when they have dried, let them be applied again after being soaked, and do this until all the pain is mitigated.

### Subsequent care.

**Take:** Hardened turpentine, 2 pounds <note>original: "℔.ij."</note>.
**Opopanax** <note>a medicinal gum resin obtained from the herb *Hercules-allheal*</note>, half a pound <note>original: "℔. ß."</note>.
**Colcothar** <term>iron oxide residue remaining after the distillation of vitriol, used as a drying agent or astringent</term>, 3 drams.
Let them be reduced into a plaster: to which can be added hot, skimmed honey that has been thinned, so that cloths can be dipped in that liquid and placed over the cut limb.

### How you shall harden the turpentine
Cook it in a pot over a slow fire. With a small stick dipped in and then pulled out, see whether it has hardened; if it has, let it be removed from the fire. It will have the appearance of glass. Let this powder be ground, and let it dissolve in two measures of vinegar together with the **opopanax**, cooked slowly. Afterward, let it be strained through a cloth and cooked until the vinegar evaporates and it thickens; finally, let the <term>colcothar</term> and turpentine be thrown in. While the material is still hot, let certain oblong masses be made: for when it cools, it hardens in the manner of glass. When you wish to use it, let it be dissolved in skimmed honey that has been added and lightly warmed, and let this be bound over the cut limb.

->From the *Book on Abscesses* <note>original: "Lib. de Apoſt"; likely referring to a treatise on *apostemes*, an archaic term for inflammatory swellings or abscesses</note>, chapter 32.<-

Mixed together by restraining twelve times—that is, always pouring the liquid back onto its dregs again and again.

### It can be added.

To the weight of the **litharge** <term>lead(II) oxide, historically used in ointments and plasters for its drying properties</term> prepared in this manner, the weight of those substances which follow below must be equalized.

This must be used until the limb emits no more vapors.

This is the description by **Geber** <note>the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy</note>, in which **Petrus de Argillata** <note>a famous 14th-century Italian surgeon and physician</note> greatly erred.

**Vitriol water**, **oak galls**, etc., as much as is sufficient.

BOOK THE FIRST. 83

**Nitric acid** <note>original: "aqua fortis"; literally "strong water," a highly corrosive mineral acid</note> is poured on, and they are left together for so long until small stones appear, clear like crystal. Then, once the moisture has been drawn off through sand, **distilled vinegar** is gradually poured on, and everything is digested in a water bath <term>a "balneum" or "bain-marie," used to provide gentle, even heat</term> until dissolved, for ten or twelve hours. Once the vessel has cooled, the mixture is cooked, and the clear liquid is taken out and distilled. To this dried material, more distilled vinegar is poured, and it is cooked as before, until all the material is dissolved. Once this is accomplished, the vinegar is drawn off by distillation, poured back, and drawn off again, until the material remains at the bottom in the form of an oil. This must be removed while still hot, so it does not thicken into the consistency of honey. It is used like oil of lead to heal any fresh wounds and ulcers within 24 hours.

*Cure for pains in the joints.*

**Take:** **Mastic** <note>original: "Masticis"; a resin obtained from the mastic tree, used for its anti-inflammatory properties</note>, 6 ounces <note>original: "℥. vj."</note>.
**Turpentine**, 2 ounces.
**Galbanum** <note>original: "Galbani"; an aromatic gum resin from Persian plant species</note>, 5 ounces.
Distill through an **alembic** <term>a classic distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling pot and a cooling cap with a delivery tube</term>.

*Additions can be made:*
To the weight of that distilled mastic, 6 ounces.
**Distilled laurel oil** <note>original: "Olei laurini"; oil of bay</note>, 1 ounce.
**Ardent wine** <term>distilled alcohol or spirits</term> and **vinegar**, 1 ounce of each <note>original: "ana"; a medical shorthand meaning 'of each'</note>.
Let an ointment be made.
*From the Book of Preparations, regarding mastic.*

*The production of milk in the breasts, by means of reverberated crystal.* <note>In early modern medicine, rock crystal (quartz) was often associated with milk production through the "doctrine of signatures," where the white or clear appearance of a mineral suggested its use for white bodily fluids.</note>
**Take:** **Crystal**, 1 pound <note>original: "℔. j."</note>.
**Water of ental** <note>original: "Aquæ entalli"; likely a mineral water or a solvent prepared from "ental," a type of fossilized dentalium shell used in alchemical preparations</note>, 2 pounds.
Let a mixture be made by **imbibition** <term>the process of a solid absorbing a liquid until it is saturated</term>, which is to be **reverberated** <term>heated in a reverberatory furnace, where the flame is reflected onto the material</term> for 24 hours, so that from one pound a "varto" is made—that is, a fourth part of a pound. The dose is 2 drams <note>original: "ʒ. ij."</note>.

*Additions can be made:*
To 2 drams of that reverberated Crystal.
**Seed-essence of lemon balm** <note>original: "Spermatis melissæ"</note>.
**Lettuce**, of each half a dram <note>original: "ana. ʒ. ß."</note>.
Let a powder be made, to be administered in **almond water**.

*Sublimation of Crystal.*
**Take:** **Crystal**, 1 dram.
**Sal ammoniac** <note>original: "Salis armoniaci"; ammonium chloride, commonly used in alchemy to aid the sublimation of minerals</note>, 3 drams.
Let both be finely powdered and **sublimated** <term>the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then collects as a solid on a cooler surface</term>, and let this be done six times, removing the material that has risen each time.

*Additions can be made:*
To half a dram of that elevated Crystal.
**Common distilled water**, 4 ounces <note>original: "℥. iiij."</note>.
Let an alkali be made, of which the dose is half a dram.

*Distillation of Crystal.*
**Take:** **Elevated crystal** <note>the material collected from the sublimation process described above</note>, 1 dram.
**Nitre water and alum water** <note>original: "Aquæ nitri & aluminis"</note> prepared without distillation, 2 drams of each.
Let them be digested very well, and afterwards let them be distilled; let this distillate be dried and **resolved** <note>dissolved or reduced back to a liquid state</note>, and then let it be coagulated over a slow fire. In a humid place, it will dissolve into a "water." The dose is 1 **scruple** <note>original: "℈. j."; a unit of apothecary weight equal to 20 grains or 1/3 of a dram</note>.

According to <note>original: "Iuxta"; a catchword for the following page</note>

# BOOK ONE.

Following the process now established for crystals across three preparations—namely, **calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to change its physical or chemical properties, often reducing it to powder.</note>, **sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance so it turns directly from a solid into a vapor and then cools back into a solid, used here for purification.</note>, and **distillation**—these transparent gems can be prepared, each according to its own specific effects: **Garnets**, **Sapphires**, **Emeralds**, **Hyacinths**, and **Rubies**, with their additional ingredients as shown below in the *Book of Preparations, concerning gems.*

*Ladanum of mercury.* <note>original: "Ladanum mercury" — while Ladanum usually refers to a plant resin, here it refers to a purified, oil-like alchemical extract of mercury.</note>

Let mercury be purified as well as possible by any superior method, and let it be sublimated with equal weights of **vitriol** <note>Sulfate minerals, often used as strong acidic agents in alchemy.</note> and **saltpeter** <note>Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in chemical reactions.</note>, having first been ground in a mortar with a wooden pestle using the strongest vinegar; the more often this is done, the better. Afterward, let it be sublimated again with calcined alum and saltpeter; then finally it will be thoroughly purified. Let its powder be drenched with **burning water** <note>original: "aqua ardente" — a term for distilled alcohol or brandy</note>, and let it be drawn off [distilled]; let more be poured on and drawn off until there remains at the bottom a substance in the form of a very white oil. Let it be dried, crushed into powder, and dissolved upon a marble slab in a damp place. Finally, let it be dried out and kept for use. It is called the **Theriac of Metals**. <term>Theriac: Traditionally a complex "universal" antidote; here, the author claims this mercury preparation serves as a supreme medicine derived from the mineral kingdom.</term> Its dose is one grain taken with theriac or mithridate <note>Another famous historical semi-mythical herbal antidote</note>, and a very excellent wine.

*A cure for freckles.* <note>original: "Lentiginis cura" — also refers to skin spots or blemishes.</note>

| Laurel berries | 10 drams of each |

88      ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.

*Cure of Leprosy and Elephantiasis.*

Gold should be reduced to its first matter <note>original: "primam materiam" - in alchemy, the base substance from which all things originate; here, a philosophical mercury</note>, that is into mercury, which is then coagulated back into a solid and consistent state, and administered over a year; for in a shorter time, these conditions cannot be properly cured. Other remedies must be avoided. The dose is 1 scruple <note>original: "℈. j." - a historical unit of weight equivalent to about 1.3 grams</note> in 1 ounce <note>original: "℥. j." - about 28 grams</note>.
      *From the Great Surgery, Part 4, Book 9, Chapter 9.*

*Treatment of diseases arising from leprous humidity, pustules, paralysis, types of gout, and moist dropsy <term>hydropisis: an old term for edema or the swelling caused by the accumulation of excess fluid</term>, by means of laxative Mercury.*

℞. Mercury coagulated with egg whites, 1 ounce.
      Alum water, 6 ounces.
Let them be distilled in ashes <note>original: "in cineribus" - a technique for applying gentle, even heat by placing the vessel in a bed of hot ash</note> so that a powder is formed from it.
      The dose is 3 to 5 grains <note>original: "grana iij. vsque ad v." - a very small unit of weight based on a grain of wheat</note>. It is a most effective purgative.
      *From the Book of Preparations, concerning mercury.*

*Treatment of Lethargy <note>original: "Lethargiae" - historically used to describe a state of deep, prolonged sleep or loss of function</note>, Paralysis, and Numbness of the limbs.*

℞. Feather alum <term>aluminis plumosi: a fibrous, hair-like variety of the mineral alum</term>, 6 ounces.
      Resolved colcothar alum <term>aluminis colchotarini: alum prepared from "colcothar," the red oxide of iron left after the distillation of vitriol</term>, 4.5 ounces.

*Oil of Litharge.*
Let it be drenched in distilled vinegar and digested in a warm place for some time; let it be poured off, and another fresh portion of vinegar added, digested, and poured off; and this should be done as many times as necessary until the vinegar is no longer colored. This is a most effective remedy for ulcerations, burns, tumors, and pains arising from excessive heat. *Oil of Litharge.*
Take: Alum, vinegar, and litharge <term>litargirium: lead monoxide, a common byproduct of silver refining used in plasters and ointments</term>. Cook them together until the litharge turns white. Cook it out again; by this very process it will be reduced to an oily state, which Archilaus <note>likely referring to a Greek alchemical or medical authority</note> also mentions.

[A decorative horizontal divider consisting of a row of seven repeating floral motifs]

The method for removing spots from the face and the skin of the entire body.
An animal, shrub, or fruit—similar in all respects to the shape or image that the spot itself resembles <note>This reflects the "Doctrine of Signatures," where the physical appearance of a medicine corresponds to the ailment it treats; for example, a heart-shaped leaf for heart trouble.</note>—should be tied over the area while living or green. It should be left there until it either dies, dries up, or rots of its own accord. By this method, you will have extracted the spot only from the flesh, and not yet from the skin: unless you first moisten the spot on the skin with **aqua fortis** <note>Nitric acid</note> or **aqua regis** <note>A highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acid used to dissolve gold</note>. Then, and only then, the skin will turn black for fifteen days or more and be cast off, and another skin will grow back in its place, similar to the natural skin.
From the *Book on Worms*, chapter 12.

92 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.
*The cure for the dead evil.* <term>malum mortuum: a historical term for a chronic skin disease, possibly a form of leprosy or necrotic scabies, characterized by dark, crusty sores that do not bleed.</term>

*A Cautery.* <note>In this context, a chemical agent designed to "burn" or open the skin to release morbid humors.</note>
**Take:** Melted sulfur.
Liquid colophony <note>original: "Liquoris de colophonia." A solid resin obtained from pine trees, used here in liquid form.</note>.
Oil of amber <note>original: "Olei de carabe." Carabe is the Arabic-derived term for amber used in alchemy.</note>, 4 ounces <note>original: "℥. iiij."</note> of each.

Fir resin, 7 ounces <note>original: "℥. vij."</note>.
Let a mixture be made over the fire; let it be applied hot twelve times every day, and [the sore] will be opened.
From the *Book on Abscesses, chapter 8.*

*The Mastery of Metals.* <term>magisterium: in alchemical medicine, a "mastery" is the process of converting a raw substance into its most potent, purified medicinal form.</term>
**Take:** The best circulated spirit <note>original: "Circulatum." A highly refined solvent that has been repeatedly distilled or "circulated" in a closed vessel.</note>, purified to the highest essence, into which you place the thinnest metal plates—using whichever metal you choose, provided it is perfectly filed, hammered, and cleaned. Let these two [the spirit and the metal] circulate together under sufficient weight for four weeks. By this tempered process, the plates will be reduced into an oil and into the form of a fat floating on top, colored according to the nature of the metal used. You shall separate this from the circulated spirit using a silver separator. This is **potable metal** <note>original: "metallum potabile." One of the great goals of Paracelsian medicine: making metals like gold or iron drinkable so their "virtues" could heal the body from within.</note>.

*The Mastery of Gems.*
**Take:** Gemstones, first crushed and calcined <note>reduced to powder through intense heat</note> according to the art of the reverberatory furnace <note>A furnace designed to reflect heat onto the substance from the roof, allowing for very high temperatures without the fuel touching the material.</note>. To these, add an equal weight of saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note>, specifically 1 pound <note>original: "℔. j."</note>. Let them be burned together into a fine ash and washed with burning wine <note>original: "ardente vino." High-proof distilled alcohol or brandy used to extract the pure salts of the gems.</note> for as long as it takes until no more superfluous matter is found. After this washing, calcine the material again, and perform the process in all ways as before.

*The Mastery of Pearls.* <note>original: "Magisterium vnionum." In alchemy, a "magistery" (magisterium) is a preparation that extracts the essential power of a substance while keeping its full medicinal properties intact. "Uniones" refers specifically to large, high-quality pearls.</note>

Corals, however, must first be ground and calcined <note>Heated to a high temperature to be reduced to a powder.</note> from the beginning with saltpeter <note>original: "sale nitro."</note>; afterward, they are to be prepared and dissolved just like gemstones.

*The Mastery of Marcasites.* <note>original: "Marcasitā." Marcasite was a general term in early chemistry for various metallic ores and pyrites, often thought to be the "seed" or "immature" form of metals.</note>

Then let it be distilled and separated, as we previously taught regarding metals.

This refers to alcohol that has been repeatedly distilled in a closed vessel so that the vapors return to the liquid, a process meant to "exalt" or purify the spirit.</note>. Let them digest in a flask <note>original: "stacco," likely a reference to a specific shape of distillation vessel or a "storta" (retort).</note> over ashes for six days. Afterward, let the circulated spirit be distilled from it and poured back over it; let this be done as many times as necessary until oil is found at the bottom. This is the mastery of amber <note>original: "arabis," likely a scribe's error for "carabis."</note>.

Once these are captured, stop immediately, lest the mastery acquire a foul smell from the fire. In the same manner, seeds, roots, barks, and similar things are brought to their own mastery.

*Cure for pains of the womb, hectic fever, and pleuropneumonia <note>original: "Pleripneumoniæ." An archaic term for inflammation of the lungs.</note>.*

## *Diaphoretic Mercury.*
<term>diaphoretic: a substance that induces sweating, often used in early medicine to "sweat out" a disease or toxin</term>
It is dissolved in water made from egg whites and calcined <term>calcined: reduced to a powder or friable state through the application of high heat</term> eggshells, so that once it has been made into a powder, the essence can be drawn from it by the liquor of vitriol <term>vitriol: a sulfate of a metal, here used as a powerful solvent or acidic liquid</term>, etc.

## *Diaphoretic Mercury.*
Take: Mercury coagulated with tin and ground; let it be placed in a gold dish, set deep enough but floating in the best wine vinegar. Let the best alcohol of wine, rectified <term>rectified: purified or concentrated through the process of distillation</term> into a spirit, be poured over the powder; let this be ignited, and let that be done several times. Namely, all the ingredients—the mercury, tin, and wine—will turn into an oily substance, which you should save. Its dose will be 1 grain <term>grain: a very small unit of weight; one grain is approximately 65 milligrams</term>, taken with the best and most noble wine, such as Traminer or Alsatian <note>original: "Tramineo, vel Alsatico." These are specific varieties of white wine from the Rhine and Alsace regions, prized for their quality.</note>. Let sweat be excited in bed by many blankets, as much as the patient can endure.

## *Preparation of loose mercury.*
<note>original: "Mercurij laxi præparatio." "Loose" likely refers to mercury that has been rendered into a fluid or medicinal state rather than its raw metallic form.</note>
Take: Mercury, and with egg-water drawn off so many times by distillation, let it be "corallated" <note>original: "coralletur." This is a chemical term of the era meaning to turn a substance red, or to give it the medicinal properties associated with red coral.</note>. Let eight grains of this corallated substance be given in a fine aromatic confection; it removes all fluxes <term>fluxes: an excessive or abnormal discharge of fluid from the body</term> collected from the plaster <term>sparadrap: a piece of cloth spread with an ointment or medicated plaster used as a bandage</term>.
*Great Surgery*, Part 5, Book 2, chapters 11 and 12 <note>This citation refers to the *Chirurgia Magna* (Great Surgery) by Paracelsus, a famous Renaissance physician and alchemist.</note>.

->102 ON THE CURE OF DISEASES<-

Let a water be distilled from vitriol <term>vitriol: a sulfate of a metal, used here as a potent acidic solvent</term> alone, and another from alum <term>alum: a chemical compound, usually potassium aluminum sulfate, used as an astringent and in dyeing</term> alone. Keep both waters separate; to half a pound <note>original: "lb. ß." The "lb" stands for libra (pound), and the "ß" is a symbol for "semis" or half.</note> of this [liquid], add 1.5 pounds of distilled vinegar and 4 pounds of egg whites that have been boiled until hard. Distill each of these twice, then a second time mixed together. For one pound of mercury, take three pounds of this final water. Once the dissolution has occurred, it must be distilled again as many times as necessary until a yellow-colored powder appears. Finally, after pouring on spirit of wine <term>spirit of wine: high-proof alcohol or ethanol</term>, let it be distilled as many times as seems fit. The dose is 3, 4, or 5 grains <term>grain: a tiny unit of weight, roughly 65 milligrams</term> in a small amount of excellent wine, or in an electuary <term>electuary: a medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</term> of roses, etc.

## *The greatest preparation of Mercury.*

Take: One pound of mercury or quicksilver. Let it be washed thoroughly with vinegar in which salt has been dissolved. Afterward, let it be mixed with half a pound of the purest salt and stirred in a marble mortar with a wooden pestle for as long as it takes for the salt to turn completely black and for it to have absorbed the quicksilver so that it no longer appears. When this is finished, let the salt be driven off from the mercury using a retort <term>retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</term> with its opening submerged in cold water, so that all of it, or whatever part is pure, descends into the water. This can be repeated as many times as necessary until it is seen to be perfectly purified. Finally, let them boil in the following lye <term>lye: a strongly alkaline solution, often made from wood ashes</term>. Take quicklime <term>quicklime: calcium oxide, a caustic alkaline substance produced by heating limestone</term>, over which you pour boiling water; stir constantly until it cools. Once the sediment has settled, let the clear liquid be strained through a filter sleeve shaped like a Hippocrates' sleeve <note>original: "sacci hypocratis." A conical fabric filter bag used by early chemists and apothecaries.</note>. This strained liquid [should be added] again to the well-burnt ashes of woad <note>original: "glasti cineres." Woad is a plant (Isatis tinctoria) used for blue dye, its ashes were used for their alkaline properties.</note>...

BOOK ONE. 103

...it should be poured on again and shaken as before, until it also cools, and then filtered through a **filtering sleeve** <note>original: "manicam filtreã" — a conical cloth bag used for straining liquids</note> once more. Mercury, having been previously purified, should be boiled sufficiently in that **lye** <note>original: "lixiuio" — a strong alkaline solution</note>, then removed and washed with vinegar in which salt has been dissolved, and driven through salt in a **retort** vessel. <term>retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</term> Finally, let one ounce [℥ j.] of it be dissolved in **Caesar’s water** or **King’s water** (the recipe for which we provide below), and let the solution be kept until it is needed.

### Recipe for Caesar’s or King’s Water

**Take:** Best **vitriol**, <term>vitriol: metal sulfates, often iron or copper, used as a source of acid</term> one pound [℔. j.]; **saltpeter**, one pound [℔. j.]; **burnt alum**, half a pound [℔. ß.]. Let a dissolving water be made according to the art, and into that water you should throw one pound [℔. j.] of **sal ammoniac**. <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used in alchemy to make acids more powerful</term> Then, it should be purified by throwing in thin plates of silver. For if the sal ammoniac were distilled together with the other ingredients, there would be a danger of the vessel breaking, on account of the violence of such a salt and the excessive vapors with which it fills the vessels and forces them to burst.

Next, take **antimony** reduced to a fine powder, purified saltpeter, and **calcined tartar**, <term>calcined tartar: potassium carbonate produced by heating the sediment from wine barrels</term> three ounces [℥ iij.] of each. Let them be melted in a melting pot, and let the vessel be struck with an iron rod so that the **regulus** <term>regulus: the metallic "heart" or button that settles at the bottom of a crucible after smelting</term> sinks to the bottom. Once the vessel has cooled on its own, let it be broken, and the regulus separated from the **dross** <note>original: "loppa" — the waste material or slag left over from smelting</note>, and ground into a powder. Take one ounce [℥. j.] of this powder and place it in a wide-mouthed glass vessel. Moisten it with the Caesar’s or King’s water and dry it over hot ashes; repeat this moistening and drying six times. Finally, let it be dissolved in the same **aqua regia** <note>original: "aqua regia" — "Royal Water," a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving noble metals</note>, and let the solution be kept separately.

Afterwards, in the same water, also dissolve separately one ounce [℥. j.] of gold, either in thin plates or beaten into leaves. Mix these three solutions together, and through a single vessel, very well sealed with **lute** <term>lute: a clay or paste used to seal chemical apparatus airtight</term> and a retort, [distill it] six times...

...or distill it seven times, each time pouring the distilled liquid and that which has been sublimated <term>sublimated: the process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid form to purify it</term> back onto its dregs <note>original: "fæces" - the solid residue left after distillation</note>. After the final distillation, let that which remains at the bottom be taken out, the distilled liquid having been discarded or set aside as a thing useless for this purpose. Once the material is ground and contained in a clay vessel, let it be constantly agitated and moved with an iron rod over moderate burning coals until it acquires a deep red color and the virulence of the *aqua regia* <term>aqua regia: literally "royal water," a highly corrosive mixture of acids used to dissolve metals</term> completely vanishes.

Finally, let this powder be washed with warm water as many times as necessary until it loses all its saltiness. Afterward, let it be dried, and then pour on enough of the spirit or most perfect essence of wine <term>essence of wine: high-proof distilled alcohol or ethanol</term> so that it stands three or four fingers' breadth above the powder. Contained in a closed vessel and placed among warm ashes, let it remain for five or six days, shaking it three times each day. Then, finally, let the essence of wine be separated from the material by means of a water bath <term>water bath: also known as a bain-marie, a method of gentle heating using a vessel placed in boiling water</term>.

Once the dry material is ground, let it be drenched in rose water mixed with musk <note>musk: a strong-smelling substance used in pharmacy for its aromatic and stimulant properties</note>, and let it remain in that for four or five days, disturbing the glass vessel by shaking it three times every day. Finally, after the liquid has been drawn off by the water bath, the material of the prepared mercury will remain at the bottom; once removed from the glass, let it be kept in a very well-closed container. The dose is from 1 grain <term>grain: a tiny unit of weight, about 65 milligrams</term> up to 3 grains—and up to 1 drachm <note>original: "ʒ. j." — approximately 3.9 grams</note> depending on the person and the disease. It should be administered by mouth for internal diseases in theriac or mithridatic water <term>theriac/mithridat: ancient, complex poly-pharmaceutical compositions used as universal antidotes and tonics</term>.

*The expulsion of liquid mercury*
*from the body, which has entered*
*through the many ointments*
*of unskilled*
*physicians.*

It can be expelled in three ways or means:
First, by means of *corallatum* <term>corallatum: a red medicinal precipitate, often a "calcined" form of mercury or other minerals, named for its coral-like color</term>.
Second, by means of diaphoretic gold <term>diaphoretic gold: a preparation of gold intended to induce sweating (diaphoresis) to purge toxins</term>.
Third, by means of Thermal Springs, such as the Baths of Jassa <note>possibly referring to the thermal springs in Baden (Aquae Helveticae) or a similar famous spa like Jassy/Iași, though Jassa often refers to specific therapeutic baths in alchemical literature</note>.

*Description of Corallatum.*
Take <note>The symbol ♃ is used here as a "Recipe" or "Take" command</note> the Liquor of the Eagle <term>Liquor of the Eagle: an alchemical term often referring to sal ammoniac (ammonium chloride) in solution</term>, which you have dried; let it be soaked in the liquor of prepared vitriol <term>vitriol: a sulfate of a metal, often iron or copper</term>. According to the process for spirit of tartar <term>spirit of tartar: a potassium-rich liquid distilled from the crusts left in wine barrels</term>, let it be drawn off until the prepared *corallatum* is finished. The dose is one grain in theriac <term>theriac: a famous ancient "polypharmacy" or universal antidote containing dozens of ingredients</term> or mithridate <term>mithridate: a semi-mythical herbal remedy used as an antitoxin</term>, according to the condition of the patient, taken a second or third time.

*Description of Diaphoretic Gold.*
Take 1 drachm <note>ʒ.j.</note> of this body <note>referring to the gold</note> and, from it, make two small bowls. Between these, by means of the essence of wine and the first degree of the Athanor <term>Athanor: a furnace designed to maintain a uniform and constant heat over a long period</term>, let it be resolved into a liquid.

*The precipitation of Mercury according*
*to Paracelsus.*

First, let it be congealed by means of alum water, and afterwards let it be ground into a very fine powder. Finally, let there be poured over it water made by distillation from egg whites and the lime of their shells, such that half of this mixture consists of the whites; let it be drawn off again by distillation, and again poured over and drawn off, and let this be done so many times until the mercury becomes red like coral. *Sweet Essence of Mercury.*

Let it be dissolved in water of saltpeter, and when drawn off by distillation, let it be reduced to a white powder. This powder, having been taken up in a melting vessel and placed over glowing coals, should have **aqua fortis** <note>literally "strong water"; a historical name for nitric acid</note>—derived from vitriol and alum—poured over it and then drawn off. Let the powder be washed several times with distilled common water so that it sets aside its sharpness <note>original: "acrimoniam"; the burning, corrosive quality of the chemicals</note>. Afterwards, pour on **burning water**, and draw it off by distillation; repeat this ten times. Finally, with an extremely fierce fire so that the vessel is nearly glowing, draw off almost all the moisture, until the powder of mercury turns red like blood and is perceived to be very sweet to the taste.

BOOK ONE. 109

...and let two parts of fragments of brick <note>original: "laterum fragmentorum"; calcined brick was often used in distillation to distribute heat or act as a catalyst</note> be thrown in, and again let *aqua fortis* <term>aqua fortis: nitric acid, a powerful solvent</term> be distilled with these and drawn off two or three times. ->The fourfold liquor of Mercury<-
->for various diseases.<-

Take: Corrosive Sublimate <note>original: "Sublimati"; Mercury(II) chloride</note>, 1 pound.
Prepared Antimony, 1/2 pound.
Let these, having been mixed, be distilled by means of a sand bath <note>original: "per arenam"; a method of indirect heating</note> until the helmet <note>original: "galea"; the top part of the still or alembic</note> clears. Let *aqua ardens* be poured over the dregs and let it be digested for 24 hours. Finally, let it be distilled again through the sand bath: first a "wine," then a transparent oil, then a yellow one; a red one remains in the bottom.

**The white liquor** <note>The text describes the "white liquor" as the first part to come forth</note>, which leaps out first, is effective against ulcers, spreading skin eruptions <note>original: "serpigines"</note>, corns, callouses, warts, etc. Likewise, it is of value for fistulas, cancer, gouty nodules, and all defects of the skin.

**The yellow liquor**, which flows out second, is administered in a dose of 2 or 3 grains against colic pains, pleurisy, gout, foulness of the breath, the spitting of blood, difficulty breathing, abscesses, and the loss of sensation in the limbs due to cold, etc.

**The red oil**, which flows last, is applied externally and is effective for all the conditions mentioned above.

**The wine**, which was drawn off from it, is used for ulceration of the lungs, inflammation of the sides, tightness of the chest, colic, etc. It is given by mouth with *aqua vitae* <term>aqua vitae: "water of life," a term for concentrated medicinal alcohol or brandy</term>.

110 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES

*Various types of mercurial essences and mineral laxatives, in order.*

**First:**
Vitriolated sulfur. Liquefied vitriol. <note>original: "Vitriolum liquefactum"; vitriol usually refers to metal sulfates, such as copper or iron sulfate</note>

**Second:**
Mercurial Seva, Lax Chimolea. <note>Seva and Chimolea are Paracelsian terms; Chimolea often refers to a type of medicinal earth or salt</note>

**Third:**
Mercury of metals. Precipitated and extracted mercury of minerals.

*Definitions of the Mercuries, according to the previous order.*

**Vitriolated sulfur** is a certain extraction of sulfur from vitriol by means of boiling <note>original: "decoctionem"</note>. Its dosage is the same as that of black hellebore <term>black hellebore: *Helleborus niger*, a potent plant used in small doses as a violent purgative</term>. **Liquefied vitriol** is that which is drawn from copper and does not coagulate further. **Mercurial Seva** is a liquid <note>original: "humor"</note> drawn from alums <note>original: "aluminibus"</note> in which mercury is generated.

**Lax Chimolea** is a powder drawn from the "flowers" <note>original: "floribus"; in alchemy, "flowers" refers to the fine, powdery substance produced through sublimation</note> of mineral salts.

**Precipitated mercury of metals** is the reduction of metals into their "first matter" <term>first matter: *materia prima*, the primordial, formless substance from which all metals were thought to originate</term>, and from there, a precipitation into itself. **Mercury of minerals** is an oiliness <note>original: "oleitas"</note> drawn from the minerals of gold and silver.

Mercurio- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "Mercurialia" or similar on the next page</note>

BOOK ONE. 109
*The three bodies of mercury.*

*The First.*
Before it breaks forth from its mineral matrix and the earth; this must be taken up with its own body <note>original: "cum suo corpore"; referring to the raw ore or "mother" material before the metal is extracted</note> and prepared for use in the preparation of mercury.

*The Second.*
After it has been born—that is, when it exists in itself, having been separated from its body by a medium fire.

*The Third.*
When it has been prepared by art and reduced into the form of a "burnt" metal <note>original: "adusti metalli"; likely referring to a calcined or oxidized state of the metal</note>.

*The first preparation of Mercury.*
Take: <note>The OCR symbol ♃, usually representing Jupiter or Tin, is likely used here as a shorthand for "Recipe" or "Take."</note> Of the ore in which mercury is born, as it is found in its first coagulation, not yet hardened nor formed into a body, 20 pounds. Let it be ground and cooked in rain water for one hour. The impurities will separate by themselves and rise to the surface; remove these by tilting the vessel, and let the sediment—namely the "body"—be dried, ground, and passed through a sieve. Afterward, you shall distill it from a cucurbit <term>cucurbita: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</term> using a most vehement fire. A very clear and white liquor will ascend; save this, and separate the phlegm <note>original: "phlegma"; the watery, inactive part of a distillate</note> from it using a water bath <term>balneum maris: a "bath of Mary" or double boiler used for gentle, even heating</term>. In the bottom there will remain a certain heavy oiliness, which is slightly less than the mercury itself. Use this "juice of the immature ore" at a weight of two grains, with six grains of oil of spike <note>original: "olei spicæ"; an essential oil derived from spike lavender, often used as a solvent or topical stimulant</note>. Use this according to mercurial practice.

*The second preparation.*
Take: Bodily mercury <note>mercury in its metallic form</note>, and amalgamate it with an equal weight of tin <note>original: "stanni"; also symbolized by ♃</note>. Let them flow together into water...

->A cleansing ointment and the preparation of others,

1 ounce of burnt alum, quenched several times in vinegar.
20 egg yolks.
½ pound of turpentine.
Let all these be stirred together into a pulp, and cooked slowly in a clean pan until they take the form of a blackish or chestnut-colored plaster, which you should use to draw out poison <note>original: "venenum"; in this context, it refers to infection, "bad humors," or literal venom</note>.

->An attractive ointment.<-
Take 20 fresh fir pine cones. Let them be boiled in water to extract the resin, which you should separate by boiling; add an equal amount of turpentine, and half as much of this last amount of resin. Let them boil together, and use.

->Another for the same effect.<-
Take ½ ounce of Opopanax gum.
2 ounces each of Serapinum <term>Serapinum: also known as sagapenum, this is a gum resin derived from an Eastern Mediterranean plant, historically used to treat wounds and inflammation</term>.
Galbanum.
And Ammoniacum.
6 ounces of Bdellium.
Let these be dissolved in vinegar and strained; then add 2 ounces of rhubarb, ½ ounce of the roots of non-lethal Ephemerum <note>original: "Ephemeri non lethalis"; likely referring to a specific variety of Iris or a plant from the Colchicum family used for its cooling or purging properties</note>, and ½ ounce of amber <note>original: "carabi"; a term often used for yellow amber in medieval and early modern pharmacy</note>. Once ground, let them be reduced into a plaster and use according to the usual custom.

*The Great Surgery of Paracelsus, Part 5, Book 2, Chapters 5, 6, 7, and 10.*

*The cure for the uterine mole.* <note>original: "Molæ matricis"; in early modern medicine, a "mola" or mole was a fleshy, non-viable mass in the uterus. This could refer to what we now know as a molar pregnancy or a uterine fibroid.</note>

1 drachm of Lapis lazuli.
Let a pessary be made with thickened opopanax <term>Opopanax: a gum resin with a strong odor, traditionally used as an antispasmodic and a binding agent for medicinal inserts.</term>.

It must be observed that this kind of medicine cannot be used safely at all unless the mole is still small, so that it may exit through the vulva before the term of a true birth; if you attempt to expel a large mole, you expose the woman to death.

*A preventative remedy for the mole.*
Take 1 drachm of trochiscated agaric <term>Trochiscated agaric: a preparation of the Larch fungus (Agaricum) that has been dried and formed into small medicinal cakes or "troches."</term>.
½ drachm of Euphorbium <note>original: "Euforbij"; the caustic, milky sap of the spurge plant, used here for its powerful irritating properties.</note>.
Strained and thickened opopanax, equal in weight to all the other ingredients. Make a pessary, and use it before the mole becomes large. And if the symptoms do not cease after one night, you shall stop the medication, because it is a sign of the generation of offspring <note>This was a common diagnostic check: if the "mole" resisted the medicine, the physician assumed a healthy pregnancy was occurring instead.</note>.
*Book on Apostemes <term>Apostemes: an archaic term for an abscess or a large, inflamed swelling.</term>, chapter 57.*

114 ON TREATING DISEASES

is melted, and to it three handfuls of broken brick fragments—or better yet, their very fine powder—should be added. Mix them together and distill the mixture from a glass flask <term>cucurbit; a gourd-shaped chemical vessel</term> that has been sealed with clay <term>luted; coated with "lute" or clay to protect the glass and seal joints</term> and stopped up. Use a slow fire at first until all the phlegm <note>original: "phlegma"; the watery, odorless part of a distillation</note> rises. When that stops, change the receiving vessel, and by increasing the fire slightly, a lemon-yellow liquid and the true oil of wax will flow forth. When cloudy drops begin to drip, change the receiver again and draw off the remaining liquid. Each of these three oils should be kept separately.

### Oil of iron.
<note>original: "Oleum ferri"</note>

Let iron filings be dissolved into a lime <term>calx; the powdery residue left after a metal is heated or chemically dissolved</term> or powder using nitric acid <term>aqua forti; literally "strong water," a highly corrosive mineral acid</term>. Once the acid has been drawn off, let the powder be washed with rainwater until all sharpness <note>original: "acrimonia"; referring to the corrosive acidity</note> is removed. Pour distilled vinegar over the dried lime and let it soften in a water bath <note>original: "balneo"; the *balneum maris* or "bath of Mary," a gentle heating method using hot water</note> until it dissolves. Afterward, let it be distilled and then add more vinegar; let it be digested and drawn off as before, repeating the process until the material remains at the bottom like an oil. This is a most excellent remedy for weeping ulcers and various other ailments.

### Oil of Juniper.
<note>original: "Oleum Iuniperi"</note>

Let the berries be crushed into a pulp, and for every pound of them, pour in a measure of spring water. Let them soak together for three or four days. Afterward, let the water and oil be distilled together from a copper vessel <note>original: "ænea vesica"; literally a "brazen bladder," referring to a copper distilling vessel</note>, using a slow fire at first, and finally a stronger one, until no more oil flows out. When it appears that only the [watery] liquid has flowed...

*Oil of honey.*

Honey, along with its honeycombs, is digested in horse dung <note>A standard alchemical technique where the natural heat of decomposing manure provided a gentle, steady temperature for "digesting" or softening substances</note>, contained within an earthenware vessel, until the portion floating upon the honey is separated from it. <meta>This page continues from the pharmaceutical preparations of Book One, transitioning from distilled oils of plants and honey to mineral-based chemistry—specifically the "Oil of Saturn" (Lead). It concludes the section on lead before briefly mentioning a treatment for broken bones.</meta>

126 ON CURING DISEASES.

*Oil from Lead* <note>original: "Oleum ex Saturno"; Saturn is the alchemical name for the metal lead</note>

Lead is calcined <term>calcination; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the air to reduce it to a powder</term> into a yellow lime, and distilled vinegar is poured over it so that it rises above the lead by three fingers' width. It is boiled over a fire, then removed and allowed to settle. The clear vinegar is poured off and fresh vinegar is added; this process is repeated until all the sweetness of the lead passes into the vinegar <note>Lead acetate, formed by this process, has a famously sweet taste and was known as "sugar of lead," though it is highly toxic</note>. This collected sweet vinegar is distilled in a water bath <term>balneum maris; a vessel of water in which a second container is placed to provide gentle, even heat</term>. A purple, sweet material remains at the bottom.

Let "heavenly water" <term>aqua coelestis; typically refers to highly rectified spirit of wine or a purified solvent</term> be poured over this; let it boil until a white foam floats on top, which should be collected. Then, finally, let the material be put back on the fire to boil. When it gives off a white foam again, collect it and set it aside with the other foam. Do this until the purple material at the bottom stops foaming; that purple material may then be discarded.

Let the collected white foams be dried again in a water bath from the remaining vinegar, until they become a white material settling at the bottom. This is dissolved again by pouring "strong water" <term>aqua fortis; nitric acid</term> over it, and the water is drawn off via the water bath. Fresh acid is poured on again and drawn off as before. After doing this three or four times, the material will emerge finer and whiter. "Heavenly water" is poured over this again and drawn off via the water bath; repeat this five or six times until all the "acrimony" <note>the sharp, stinging quality of the acid</note> has departed from the material and the Salt of Lead <note>original: "sale Saturni"</note>. In a humid place, it will resolve through "fluxion" <note>deliquescence; the process where a salt absorbs so much moisture from the air that it turns into a liquid oil</note> into a water or oil, used for all flaws <note>vices or ailments</note>.

*Cure for broken bones.*

Comfrey <note>original: "Symphitum"; also known as "knitbone" for its traditional use in healing fractures</note>, ground and cooked and placed on top, cures.
