*Cure for Panaritium or the "Worm."*

A **panaritium** <note>original: "Panaritius"; a painful, deep infection of the fingertip, commonly known today as a whitlow or paronychia.</note> or "worm" arises from the very substance from which nails are born, and it centers itself in the joints, such as the fingers of the hands and the toes of the feet.
The cure is threefold: one is performed through **characters** <note>original: "caracteres"; magical symbols or sigils inscribed on amulets or the skin.</note>, as is customarily done in Carinthia; the second is through **superstition** <note>This refers to folk charms, incantations, or ritualistic practices common in early modern medicine.</note>; the third is with natural medicines, such as a narcotic ointment made from **frog spawn** <note>original: "spermiolo ranarum"</note>, with which a small pouch, sewn in the shape of a finger, should be moistened and worn, and moistened frequently, etc.
*Little Surgery*, Book 3, Chapter 3.

*Cure for Panaritium, Pandalitium, or "the Passion."*

After cooling the area three or four times with frog spawn, you may cure it with pig dung cooked with vinegar.
*Book on Apostemes <note>term: "Aposteme"; an old medical term for an abscess or localized swelling filled with pus.</note>, Chapter 17.*

Cloths <note>original: "Panni"; likely the start of a new section on medical patches or eye films.</note>

BOOK ONE. 137

...of vitriol <note>original: "vitrioli"; naturally occurring sulfate minerals, often used as a source for sulfuric acid</note>, and 1 part saltpeter <note>original: "salis nitri"; potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in early chemical reactions</note>, let the mercury be placed with an equal weight of water among the ashes <note>original: "inter cineres"; a method of heating where the vessel is placed in hot ash to maintain a gentle, steady temperature</note>. Afterward, let the water be drawn off gradually through a sand bath <note>original: "per arenam"; heating a vessel nestled in sand to distribute heat evenly and prevent the glass from cracking</note> until a dry and reddish powder remains at the bottom. Once removed and ground, let it be heated gradually in a crucible <note>a ceramic or metal container used for melting substances at very high temperatures</note> among the coals, and by pouring on rectified spirit of wine <note>original: "vino rectificato"; highly distilled, concentrated alcohol</note> and setting it alight, let it be burned off several times. Finally, let it be washed with rose water and dried. Lastly, having set the spirit of wine on fire over it as before, let it be burned again, dried, and stored.

*Cure for a discharge.* <note>original: "Profluuij cura"; likely referring to an abnormal flow of bodily fluids, such as a hemorrhage, diarrhea, or flux</note>
Take: 1 drachm <note>a unit of weight, approximately 3.9 grams</note> of silver filings.
Reduce it into a lime <term>calx; the powdery residue or oxide left after a metal has been burned or chemically corroded</term> using aqua fortis <term>aqua fortis; "strong water," or nitric acid, used by early chemists to dissolve metals</term>.
Take: 2 drachms of this lime.
Half a drachm of crude tartar <note>original: "Tartari crudi"; the potassium bitartrate crust found in wine barrels, used as a chemical reagent</note>.
Let them be reduced in the fourth degree <note>the highest level of heat in early chemistry, usually involving an intense, glowing fire</note> and let the alkali <note>original: "alcali"; the soluble salts extracted from the ashes or residue</note> be extracted.

*The preparation of the Quintessence of Gold.*

Take as much gold as you wish, but let it first be purified by royal cement <note>original: "cemento regali"; a dry refining process used to separate gold from silver and other base metals</note>, or by antimony <term>antimony; a brittle, silvery-white metalloid used in alchemy to "devour" impurities in gold</term>. From this gold, you are to remove its metallic property or its malleability <note>the physical quality of being able to be hammered into sheets</note> by means of salt-water. Afterward, wash the residue with fresh water. Finally, extract its tincture <note>the medicinal essence or concentrated color</note> using spirit of wine <term>spirit of wine; concentrated alcohol or ethanol produced by distilling wine</term>. Lastly, separate the spirit from it by elevation <note>original: "eleuationem"; an alchemical term for distillation or the rising of vapors</note>; the fifth essence <term>quintessence; in alchemy, the "fifth element" or the purest, most concentrated medicinal core of a substance</term> will remain at the bottom of the vessel.

*The formula for Salt-Water.*
Take the cleanest and whitest salt, created by nature and not by human art <note>likely referring to natural rock salt or sea salt rather than salt produced through industrial or chemical processes</note>, ground and melted several times, and ground again; you shall distill this salt once it has been mixed and dissolved with the juice of radish roots. Mix this again with equal weights of blood-red plant <note>original: "sanguineæ"; likely referring to Blood Dock (Rumex sanguineus) or another plant known for red sap or stems</note> while it is still green, and distill it five times. In this liquor, gold in the form of thin plates is dissolved into the powder that we mentioned above. Let it be washed frequently with clear distilled water until all saltiness is gone; it is easily separated because it does not mix with the gold in the depths. Therefore, once the corrosive substance is removed, the gold remains through

*Extractions of Quintessences,*
*and first from metals.*

Afterward, let it putrefy again for a month. Afterward, two dark colors will ascend: one white, the other according to the nature and condition of the metal. And when they have ascended, they will be entirely separated in the receiver, so that the quintessence <term>quintessence; the "fifth element" or the purest, most potent medicinal core of a substance</term> will remain at the bottom, and the white color...

->*The fifth essence of marcasites.*<-

Our process and method for extracting the fifth essence from <term>marcasites</term><note>In early chemistry, this referred to various metallic minerals, often iron pyrites or bismuth, believed to contain the "seed" of metals.</note> is in every way similar to that used for metals. As for our statement that the fifth essence represents the highest powers and virtues of its source—this also applies to the <term>arcana</term><note>Secret, powerful medicines.</note>, <term>magisteries</term><note>Substances prepared without removing any elements, but by transforming the whole into a better state.</note>, elixirs, and so forth. *The Quintessence of Salts*

Having taken the salts, you must calcine <term>calcine; to heat a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or ash</term> them thoroughly; if they are volatile <term>volatile; substances that easily evaporate or change state when heated</term>, you should only burn them. Afterward, let them be resolved into a thin state and distilled into water. Place this water into putrefaction <term>putrefaction; in alchemy, the slow fermentation or "rotting" of a substance under gentle heat to break it down</term> for a month, then distill it using a bath <note>original: "per balneum"; a water bath or bain-marie used for gentle, even heating</note>. A sweet water will rise, which you should discard. When nothing more wishes to rise, place it back into digestion for another month and distill as before. Repeat this as many times as necessary until no sweetness is perceived, but only saltiness. In this way, you will have the quintessence of the salt at the bottom; from a burned pound of material, you will get barely two ounces. However, you must proceed differently with alum and vitriol <note>vitriol; a historical name for various sulfate minerals, often used in medicine and chemistry</note>, because these do not allow themselves to be calcined to the point of flow <note>original: "ad fluxum"; the state of melting or becoming liquid</note>, which is the state to which salts must be brought.

*The Quintessence of Precious Stones, Gems, and Large Pearls.*

Take gems, margarites <note>original: "margaritas"; an archaic term for pearls</note>, or large pearls <note>original: "vniones"; literally "unions," a term used for exceptionally large, singular pearls</note>, and let them be crushed into coarse fragments, not ground into a fine powder. Place these in a glass vessel with radical vinegar <term>radical vinegar; a highly concentrated, potent distilled vinegar used as a powerful solvent</term> poured over them, so that it rises four or five finger-breadths above the fragments, and let them be digested <term>digestion; a process of slow, gentle heating used to soften or decompose a substance</term> in horse manure for a full month. When this time has passed, the entire material will have become liquid. Wash this with more fresh radical vinegar, and let them remain mixed together so that the vinegar takes on the color of the stone. Pour this colored liquid into a glass vessel, and pour more vinegar onto the remaining material; repeat this until the vinegar no longer becomes colored. The quintessence <term>quintessence; the "fifth essence" or the purest, most concentrated medicinal force of a substance</term> is contained within these colored liquids.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of extracting quintessences, moving from minerals and gems to flammable substances like resins and then to "growing things" such as herbs.</meta>

146 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES
*The Quintessence of Combustible Substances.*

Combustible bodies are those which are not edible and contain pitch, oil, or resin within them, such as turpentine, fir, juniper, cypress, and other materials subject to burning. 

Take such materials and grind them until they fill a strong, glazed earthenware pot. This must be covered and sealed with the lute of wisdom <term>lute of wisdom; a specialized alchemical sealant made of clay, dung, and other fibers used to make vessels airtight</term> so that it does not "breathe" or leak vapor. Burn this with a circulatory fire <term>circulatory fire; a heat source that surrounds the vessel evenly</term> for twenty-four hours, ensuring the heat remains constant. Once the pot is glowing like burning coals, remove it from the fire and let the material be nourished <note>original: "nutricetur"; a term implies a slow development or maturation</note> in horse manure for four weeks. Afterward, let whatever wishes to rise be distilled. Place this in a "horse's belly" <note>original: "equino ventre"; a metaphor for a warm, moist environment like a heap of fermenting manure</note> to distill all the moisture from it, and let it putrefy again until no more moisture comes out. Finally, the quintessence of the substance you originally took remains at the bottom.

*The Quintessence from Growing Things.*

Take herbs, leafy branches, and similar materials. Let them be thoroughly crushed and stored in a vessel suited for this purpose. Place them in horse manure for four weeks. Afterward, distill them using a bath <note>original: "balneum"; likely a water bath</note>, and place them back into the horse manure for eight days. Distill them once more using a Mary’s bath <term>Mary's bath; also known as a bain-marie, a method of gentle heating using a vessel placed in boiling water</term>. The quintessence will ascend through the alembic <term>alembic; the capped top of a still used to capture and condense vapors</term> and the "body" <note>original: "corpus"; the physical dregs or exhausted material</note> will remain at the bottom. If any of the quintessence remains at the bottom with the dregs, let them putrefy as before and be distilled. To this distilled water, add the growing material once again, and—

<summary>This page details the alchemical extraction of the 'quintessence' from resinous woods and botanical materials using prolonged heat and fermentation in manure.</summary>
<keywords>quintessence, distillation, resins, herbs, lute of wisdom, horse manure, alchemy, medical preparation</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the instructions for extracting the quintessence from "growing things" (herbs and plants) and begins the process for aromatic substances like musk and camphor.</meta>

BOOK FIRST. 147

Let this also be digested through a pellican <note>a pellican is a circulatory distillation vessel with two "arms" that feed condensed vapors back into the main body, allowing for continuous cycles of evaporation and condensation</note> for six days; from this will emerge a thick color, which you shall draw off by means of a sea-bath <term>sea-bath; also known as a balneum maris or water bath, a method of heating a vessel by placing it in hot water to ensure a gentle, steady temperature</term>, and the "body" will retreat, with the quintessence remaining at the bottom. Separate this from the dregs according to the process of retorts—or otherwise, a press—and once finished, let the quintessence be digested for four days. In this manner, you will have it perfected in its odor, its juice, and its medicinal power, and of a thick substance.

*The quintessence of aromatics or*
*fragrant things.*

Take musk, civet <note>a pungent, perfumed substance secreted by the glands of the civet cat, highly prized in historical pharmacy</note>, camphor, and similar materials; let them be mixed with sweet almond oil, and stored in glass, let them be digested in the sun in their proper season until they are reduced to a paste. Afterward, let them be squeezed out from their dregs. In this way, the "body" <note>the physical, inert matter of the substance</note> is separated from the quintessence, which is now mixed with the oil; it shall be separated from it as follows.

Take rectified burning wine <note>original: "vinum ardens"; high-proof distilled alcohol or aqua vitae</note>, with which the said oil is to be doused, and let them be left in digestion for six days. Afterward, let them be distilled over ashes <note>original: "per cineres"; a method of distillation where the glass vessel sits in a bed of hot ash to distribute heat evenly</note>; the burning wine will ascend, and the quintessence with it, while the oil remains at the bottom without any substance of the quintessence left in it. Finally, let this burning wine be distilled through a sea-bath, and the quintessence will remain at the bottom, separated in the form of an oil.

k 2 Quinta

148 ->ON CURING DISEASES<-

*The quintessence from edible things,*
*and drinkable things.*

Take edible matter cut up very finely, which should boil in a closed and luted <note>sealed with "lute," a paste of clay or other materials used to make a vessel airtight</note> jar for three days. Afterward, let it be squeezed through a linen cloth or a sieve, and be distilled by means of a sea-bath <term>sea-bath; also known as a balneum maris, a method of gentle heating by placing the vessel in a larger container of hot water</term>. First, a certain watery liquid rises; after it has all ascended, the quintessence will be left in the bottom. This is the supreme nourishment above all others.

*Concerning drinkable things.*

Take any drinkable thing as it is with its whole substance; let this be stored in a pellican <note>a circulatory distillation vessel with two arms that feed condensed vapors back into the base</note> and digested in horse dung <note>fresh horse manure was used in alchemy as a source of steady, gentle heat for long-term chemical reactions</note> for a month, though it will be better for a year or more, and you will find a certain substance digested in the pellican. Separate this first by means of a sea-bath, afterward through ashes <note>distillation over hot ashes, which provides a higher heat than water</term>, and finally through fire <note>direct heat</note>. In this manner, you will have three quintessences, which are present in all drinkable things, for many reasons, as we have set forth in the books regarding their generations. Let each be kept separately in glass; let the two later ones be digested more broadly and placed in a sea-bath, and more of the first quintessence will rise. Do this same thing repeatedly until no more of the first quintessence rises. In this way, they are most excellently separated.

Book of the Archidoxes <note>The *Archidoxis* is one of Paracelsus's most famous works on the "Arch-Secrets" of nature</note>, on the quintessence

*Treatment for a Ranula,*
<note>original: "Ranula cura." A ranula (literally "little frog") is a fluid-filled cyst that forms on the floor of the mouth under the tongue, so named because it was thought to resemble a frog's throat.</note>

Afterward, let them be washed very well with the waters of <term>Brassatella; likely Adder’s Tongue fern</term>, lady’s mantle, <term>diapensia; also known as sanicle, an herb used for healing wounds</term>, agrimony, etc., or with oil of St. John’s wort, or oil of Centaury flowers. BOOK ONE. 151

Cornelian stone held in the hand, or suspended from the neck. <note>original: "Carneolus"</note>
Ashes from burnt frogs.
<term>Hematite; a reddish-black iron oxide mineral whose name derives from the Greek word for blood, traditionally used as an astringent</term>.
Wool that has been tied and compressed within a nut shell for a long time.

A vein that is not constricted by these means—and especially by the remedies mentioned previously—will be a case of difficult and dangerous compression, unless it occurs after a proper amount of blood has already flowed out. It should be noted, therefore, that blood flows most abundantly from wounded people due to an excess of <term>choler; also known as yellow bile, one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids that pre-modern medicine believed determined a person’s health and temperament</term>. This flow cannot be stopped by any remedy unless the choler is first calmed; if it is stopped by force, it is to be feared that the blood will be forced to flow internally within the body, which would be most dangerous. One must wait, therefore, until the natural subsiding of any symptoms has arrived.

**Exposition**

Now the opportunity arises to discuss the furnace used for preparing <term>calcined; materials reduced to a powder or friable state by heat</term> and <term>reverberated; materials heated in a furnace where the flame is "thrown back" or reflected onto the substance</term> substances. Two furnaces are constructed in the shape of those in which bread is usually baked, or perhaps slightly lower on the inside. Between these, another intermediate furnace is built, but one foot lower, containing a grate. In this middle section, a flaming fire is built from very dry wood, so that the flame has no vent within that specific furnace; instead, it passes into the side furnaces through certain vents. In this way, the flame "reverberates" all around the vessels containing the materials. Without these vents on the other side of each lateral furnace, the flame and heat would otherwise not be able to enter.

Vessels <insert>containing</insert> the material

### *Water or oil of salt.*

Let common salt be liquefied by fire, into which you should throw glowing fragments of bricks <note>original: "laterum"; porous brick fragments were used to provide surface area for distillation</note>, and drive it through a <term>retort; a glass vessel with a long downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</term>, for the dissolving of metals.

...finely ground, 1 pound; of <term>circulated salt; salt that has been repeatedly distilled or digested to increase its chemical activity</term>, 4 pounds. Therefore, it is right that we look at their first essences. ### *The first essence of gems.*

Take 1 ounce <note>original: "℥. j."</note> of emeralds, or other gems ground as finely as possible. Calcine them with dissolved salt until they are turned white. Afterward, let them be dissolved, and in a <term>hermetically sealed phial; a glass vessel whose neck is melted shut to make it airtight</term>, place them over an open fire with the glass bare, so that the matter is suspended high up and does not touch the bottom. Do this until it falls from its spiritual nature and state to the bottom like a body, and like a liquid of metals or honey.

### *The first essence of herbs.*

Take <term>Celandine; a flowering plant in the poppy family</term>, <term>Lemon Balm; a fragrant herb in the mint family</term>, or any other herb crushed into a pulp. Hidden in a glass vessel closed with <term>Hermetic lute; a specialized clay or paste used to seal laboratory equipment</term>, let it be digested in horse dung for a month, or even in a sand-bath <note>original: "per arenam"</note>. Finally, let the pure be separated from the impure. Place the pure part into a glass with dissolved salt, and let the vessel be closed,

### *Oil of Saturn from lead.*

<term>Lead is reduced into a lime; through calcination, where the metal is heated until it breaks down into a powdery ash or oxide</term> by itself, and is <term>digested; a method of slow, gentle heating in a liquid to extract its properties</term> with distilled vinegar for two days in hot ashes: then the vinegar is poured off, and fresh vinegar is poured on, digested, and poured off with the rest; this is done for so long until all the sweetness has been extracted by the vinegar <note>The "sweetness" extracted from lead by vinegar is lead acetate, famously known as "sugar of lead."</note>. All this collected liquid is distilled in a <term>water bath; a vessel of water used to heat another container, ensuring the temperature does not exceed boiling</term>, so that a white matter like salt remains at the bottom. This is brought into an oil in a moist place by <term>deliquescence; the process where a substance absorbs moisture from the air until it dissolves into a liquid</term> for the care of wounds and the <term>filling of flesh; the regeneration of healthy tissue in deep or open sores</term> in them, the soothing of tumors, etc. It is also given by mouth, in a weight of 3 or 4 grains, for intestinal and colicky pains <note>While used historically, lead is highly toxic; its "sweetness" and medicinal use led to many cases of chronic poisoning.</note>.
                                                                    *For the itch* <margin>Original: *Scabiei*. This refers to scabies or general skin eruptions and irritations.</margin>

<summary>This page details chemical recipes for medicinal sulfur and an "oil of lead" (Saturn). These preparations were used to treat a variety of conditions ranging from deep wounds and tumors to internal colic and skin diseases.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur, lead, Saturn, myrobalans, saffron, hepatic aloes, sublimation, deliquescence, wound care, colic, alchemy</keywords>

### The Cure for Scabies and Venereal Itching

Take:
10 pounds of Alum.
1 pound of Vitriol <note>original: "Vitrioli"; a historical name for various metal sulfates, often used for their astringent properties.</note>.
Half a pound of Calcined Tartar <note>original: "Tartari calcinati"; potassium carbonate produced by burning crude tartar, used as an alkaline agent.</note>.
3 pounds of Fused Salt.

Let all these be cast into water in which **hops** <note>original: "lupulus"</note> have previously been soaked; in this bath, the sick may be washed. If the disease is long-standing, extinguish **glowing mercury** <note>mercury heated until it vaporizes or glows</note> four times in a fourth part of dissolved vitriol, and use it as an ointment. This material may be thickened in the following manner:

Take:
1 ounce each of Calcined Alum and <term>Plumose Alum<note>original: "aluminis plumosi"; a fibrous form of alum or asbestos-like mineral used in ancient medicine for its drying effects.</note></term>.
Calcined Tartar.
4 ounces each of <term>Colcotar Vitriol<note>original: "Vitrioli colchotarini"; the red iron oxide residue left after the distillation of sulfuric acid from vitriol.</note></term>.
Rock Alum.
Black Salt.
1 pound each of Raw Vitriol.
A quantity of **cheese prepared into a gluey mass** <note>original: "Casei præparati in viscum"; casein from cheese was often used as a binding agent or adhesive in early medicinal pastes.</note> equal to the weight of all the other ingredients.

Let all these, once mixed, be reduced to a paste, which becomes as hard as **horn** in the air; and when you wish to use it, cast it into hot water, and it will dissolve.

A salt made from **feverfew** <note>original: "parthenio"</note>, **serpentine** <note>original: "Serpentina"; likely referring to bistort or another plant used to treat snakebites and skin sores.</note>, and **briony** <note>original: "Prionia"</note>, when added to the prescribed water, cures scabies and the itching born from the <term>venereal disease<note>In the 16th century, this usually referred to the "Great Pox" or syphilis, which caused significant skin lesions.</note></term>.

Likewise, a <term>phlegm<note>original: "phlegma"; in early chemistry, this referred to the watery distillate or "spirit" pulled from a substance through heat.</note></term> distilled from saltpeter, when mixed with thermal water, far surpasses the power of Mercury in the treatment of those suffering from <term>dropsy<note>original: "hydropicorum"; an old term for edema or swelling caused by fluid retention.</note></term> and ulcers arising from the venereal disease. It is equally effective for **herpetic** <note>original: "herpeticis"; spreading or creeping skin eruptions.</note> conditions.

### *Cure of <term>Sephirus; a term derived from Arabic medicine referring to a hard, scirrhous tumor or a non-suppurating abscess that does not come to a "head" or produce pus</term>.*

Sephirus is a hard abscess, produced from dry salt <note>a reference to the humoral theory where an excess of "salty" humors caused hardening of tissue</note>, not ripening, tinged with many colors, and curable only by resolution <note>the process of the swelling dissolving and being reabsorbed by the body rather than bursting</note>.

Juice of <term>Althea; the Marshmallow plant, prized for its mucilaginous, soothing, and softening properties</term> as much as suffices to incorporate.

Mix them together, and after a digestion <term>Digestion; the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to break it down</term> of one month, add one and a half scruples of the quintessence of gold <note>original: "quintæ eſſentiæ auri"</note>. Once these are mixed, it will be a specific Anodyne <note>a medicine that kills pain</note>, for removing any pains whatsoever, whether internal or external.

*A specific narcotic of a*
*sweat-inducing nature,*

Take: Ginger, 1 pound.
Long pepper
Black pepper, 1 and a half ounces of each.
Cardamom, 3 drachms.
Grains of paradise, 1 drachm <note>a pungent spice related to ginger, also known as alligator pepper</note>.
Let them be ground into a powder and placed in a glass vessel with half a drachm of very well-ground camphor, as well as two ounces of solvent water. With the glass sealed, let them remain in a sand bath <note>original: "in arena"; a container of sand heated from below to provide a steady, even temperature</note> for the time required to complete their digestion. Finally, separate the solvent water from them, let them putrefy for one month, and let them be circulated for one week <term>Circulation; a process where a liquid is heated so its vapors rise and then condense back into the original vessel</term>. Afterward, you shall squeeze the mixture and save the liquid. This is the best and most powerful diaphoretic <note>a medicine that induces sweating</note> for removing all "cold" diseases residing under the skin, in which the "fifth essence" <note>quintessence</note> achieves nothing. A case of *cossatum* <note>a term likely referring to a hard tumor or chronic skin affliction</note> of twenty years was healed by this diaphoretic, etc.

*A Purgative*

### A Specific Purgative, a specified essence.

**Take:** Magistery of tartar <term>Magistery; a chemical precipitate or highly refined extract of a substance</term> and magistery of vitriol <term>Vitriol; a metal sulfate, often iron or copper, used in early chemistry</term>; mix these together. Afterward, add equal parts of the essence of saffron. Then, let the contents be digested <term>Digestion; the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to break it down</term> in a pelican <note>original: "pellicano"; a circulatory distillation vessel with two side arms that look like a pelican's beak</note> for one month in a sand bath, and let them be preserved most carefully as a medicine. This remedy purges all things indifferently, regardless of the patient's complexion <note>a person's unique balance of the four bodily humors</note> or qualities. It not only causes living animals to flourish, but even plants. For the magistery of vitriol restores a flower <note>original: "florem anthos"; 'anthos' is Greek for flower, often used specifically for rosemary</note> that is beginning to wither, or even one that is nearly dry, and restores it to its former vigor and greenness, etc.

### A Specific Attractive.
<note>An "attractive" or "drawing" medicine was used to pull thorns, splinters, or "corrupt humors" out of the body.</note>

**Take:** The quintessence of every kind of gum, one quarter part of a pound.
The fiery element of amber <note>original: "carabis"; from the Arabic-derived 'karabe', meaning amber</note>, 1 pound.
Magistery of magnet <note>lodestone</note>, one quarter and a half.
The fiery element of mastic <note>a resin from the mastic tree</note>.
Myrrh, one quarter and a half of each.
The element of Scammony <note>a resin from a vine root, used as a powerful purge</note>, 10 drachms—otherwise, Savin <note>a type of juniper bush</note>.
From this, let a cerate <term>Cerate; a stiff ointment made of wax, oil, and medicinal ingredients</term> be made with common wax, tragacanth, and turpentine.

### A Specific Styptic.
<note>A styptic is a substance used to stop bleeding by contracting the tissues.</note>

**Take:** Quintessence of medicinal clay <note>original: "boli"; likely 'Bole Armoniac', a red earthy clay used to stop blood</note>.
Quintessence of iron.
Quintessence of *Cathebes* <note>possibly catechu, a plant extract used for its astringent properties</note>, 1 pound of each.
Let them be digested in ashes for one month; afterward, add half a pound of dried acacia.

170 ON CURING DISEASES.

*A specific corrosive.*

**Take:** One pound of nitric acid <term>Aqua fortis; literally 'strong water', a powerful mineral acid</term> rectified <term>Rectified; purified by repeated distillation</term> from its own "dead head" <term>Caput mortuum; the solid, useless residue remaining after distillation</term>.
One quarter and a half of sublimated mercury <note>Mercuric chloride, a highly toxic and corrosive compound used here as a potent chemical agent</note>.
Two ounces of sal ammoniac <term>Sal ammoniac; ammonium chloride, used in alchemy to help dissolve metals</term>.

Let all these, once mixed together, be allowed to evaporate. Next, mix in an equal weight of mercurial water and preserve it. To this corrosive, even a diamond cannot resist, etc.

*A specific corrosive by which the skin is removed.*

**Take:** One pound of the juice of flammula <note>original: "flammulæ"; spearwort or buttercup, plants known for their ability to irritate and blister the skin</note>.
One quarter and a half of Spanish flies <term>Cantharides; dried beetles used in early medicine to induce blistering or as a topical irritant</term>.
Two ounces of "Hellfire," so-called <note>original: "Ignis gehennæ"; likely a contemporary alchemical name for a highly caustic mineral acid or alkali</note>.

Mix these and prepare as described above. By this cautery <term>Cautery; a treatment involving the burning or dissolving of tissue</term>, the skin is removed in cases of leprosy, morphea <note>a skin condition causing discolored, hardened patches</note>, shingles <note>original: "serpigine"; a term for creeping skin eruptions</note>, freckles, skin spots <note>original: "pano"; a medical term for a film or discoloration on the skin</note>, etc., and in its place, new skin is drawn over.

A specific for the womb to restrain the menses. It is the skin of a fig; as soon as its smoke is received into the womb, the disease is expelled. The quintessence of corals and the element of iron produce the same effect.

*A specific to provoke the menses.*

It is the spleen of a castrated ox, reduced into its magistery <term>Magistery; a concentrated medicinal extract or precipitate</term> or into its quintessence.

From the *Book of the Archidoxes, on Specifics.*

*Spirit of wine.*

BOOK ONE. 171

...and for so long until little veins <note>original: "venulæ"; these are the streaks of condensation that appear on the inside of the glass during distillation</note> are seen extending to the middle of the helmet <term>Helmet; also called a 'galea' or alembic head, the top part of a still where vapors condense</term>. The receiver having been removed, let another be applied, and let as much of the liquid be drawn off as before. Finally, this receiver being moved away and another applied, let that which remains be drawn off. These three distilled liquids of this kind are to be kept separate. 

Next, let the remainder of the wine be distilled through ashes <note>A method of heating the vessel indirectly by burying it in a bed of ashes to ensure a gentle, even temperature</note> until a thick and black matter remains in the bottom of the vessel. To this, let the liquid drawn in the third stage be poured; let it be drawn off and poured back again, and this for so long until the matter attains the thickness of honey. Finally, when placed in a damp place, it will yield reddish little stones <note>Likely crystals of tartar or specific mineral salts precipitating out of the concentrated wine lees</note>; take these out and preserve them. 

Let the matter again be poured over with the third liquid, and let it be cooked as before, continuing until no more little stones are produced. Let the collected spirits be washed with phlegm <term>Phlegm; in early chemistry, the watery, tasteless, and inactive portion of a distillate</term>, and having been poured over with the liquid from the second distillation, let them be digested <term>Digestion; the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to mature it</term> and dried out as many times as necessary until they turn white. Lastly, let them be macerated with the liquid from the first distillation and separated until they become volatile <term>Volatile; a substance that easily evaporates or passes into a gaseous state</term>. 

Let an amalgam of the Sun <note>original: "Solis"; the alchemical name for Gold</note> and mercury be burned with sulfur; let the calx <term>Calx; the powdery metallic oxide or residue remaining after a metal has been roasted or "burnt" in a furnace</term> be washed with warm distilled water. Take half an ounce [℥.ß.] of this and one and a half ounces [℥.j.ß.] of the little stones, and let them be digested in rectified aqua vitae <term>Aqua vitae; 'water of life', a high-proof alcohol used as a powerful solvent</term> for fifteen days. Then, from an upright cucurbit <term>Cucurbit; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</term>, let one part be drawn off, and the "water of the Sun" <note>Gold in a liquid or spirituous state</note> shall ascend, tinted with a saffron color. Again, let fresh little stones be added to the dregs, let them be allowed to digest, and let them be distilled as many times as necessary until all the gold is resolved into a spirit.

### Spirit of Vitriol.

Let it be distilled with a violent fire nine times in an upright cucurbit.

172 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES

...in a cucurbit <term>Cucurbit; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</term>, always cohobating <term>Cohobation; the process of repeatedly redistilling a liquid by pouring it back onto the solid remains (the dregs) to concentrate its medicinal or chemical properties</term>, or by pouring it back over the dregs. Finally, with a very strong fire, let all the spirits be extracted through a retort <term>Retort; a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances that require high heat</term>. In this manner, you will have the most potent spirit of vitriol <note>original: "spiritum vitrioli"; a strong acidic liquid, essentially sulfuric acid, highly valued in early chemistry</note>.

->*Oil of Colcothar*<-
From red colcothar <term>Colcothar; the reddish-brown powder (iron oxide) remaining after the distillation of vitriol</term> crushed by itself, or with salt and distilled wine added—if you wish—by a very strong fire, a dark red oil will flow out from a lying pot <note>original: "olla iacente"; a horizontal retort designed to withstand high temperatures and allow heavy oils to flow out</note>, which is called oil of vitriol.

Finally, an alkali salt <note>original: "sal... alcali"</note> is extracted from the dregs of the vitriol. When horizon leaves <note>original: "foliis orizontis"; this likely refers to a specific herb or perhaps an alchemical symbolic term for the "horizon" or surface of the substance</note> are added to the oil and digested with it, the "foliated" oil of vitriol is made.

->*Vegetable Spirit from Wine*<-
Let the most noble wine be contained in a very large glass vessel, so that it occupies barely a fourth part of the capacity; let it be digested in horse dung <note>Decaying horse manure was a common source of gentle, steady heat for long-term chemical processes</note> or a water bath for 30 days, or for as long as a liquid resembling oil is seen appearing upon the wine. Here, with the fire greatly reduced, let it be separated until the spirit ceases to rise. And let it be digested again in a water bath for 24 days, so that from 1 pound <note>original: "℔. j."</note> of wine, barely 1 scruple <note>original: "℈. j."; a small unit of apothecary weight, about 1.3 grams</note> of this kind of spirit is obtained. It is most useful for the prolongation of life, and also for extracting tinctures.

->*The Cure of Quinsy through a Healing Gargle*<-
<note>Quinsy, or "Squinancia," was a term for a severe, often life-threatening sore throat or tonsillar abscess</note>

->*Gargle*<-
Take 4 ounces of rose honey.

->*The Cure for Quinsy and Carbuncles*<-

Quinsy <note>original: "Squinancia"; a severe inflammation of the throat or tonsils</note> is an abscess arising from all the lower veins running upward, or the upper veins running downward, meeting at the neck where they place their center; it is an abscess similar to a carbuncle <note>original: "carbunculo"; a cluster of painful, pus-filled boils</note>. For this reason, it is included under the same treatment.

FIRST BOOK. 177
from phlegm <note>original: "à phlegmate." In the humoral theory of medicine, phlegm was the cold, moist humor; sulfur was often used as a "hot" remedy to counteract it.</note>.

### *Various oils of sulfur.*

℟. <note>Recipe; take the following</note> Ground sulfur: ℥. ij. <term>two ounces; approximately 56 grams</term>
Calcined tartar <note>original: "Tartari calcinati"; potassium carbonate, produced by heating the sediment left in wine barrels</note>: ℥. j. <term>one ounce; approximately 28 grams</term>
Quicklime <note>original: "Calcis viuæ"; calcium oxide</note>: ℥. viij. <term>eight ounces; approximately 224 grams</term>

*Or:*
℟. Ground sulfur: as much as you wish.
Enough tartar, at your discretion; let them be drenched in distilled vinegar and distilled into a balsam-oil <note>original: "balsamoleum"; a thick, oily medicinal preparation</note>. For various diseases, whether administered by mouth or applied externally, it cures most excellently.

### *Vitriolated sulfur.* <note>Sulfur that has been treated with "oil of vitriol" (sulfuric acid)</note>

Let its oil be dried into a powder by sublimation <note>the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifying it again to purify it</note>, repeatedly pouring the same oil back onto it and always sublimating with a very intense fire. Afterward, having poured on this kind of oil, let it be digested <note>kept at a gentle, steady heat to allow for chemical change</note> for a month, and afterward let it be distilled. It can be made better from flowers of sulfur <note>a fine, bright yellow powder produced by sublimating natural sulfur</note> and oil of vitriol <note>concentrated sulfuric acid</note>. It soothes all pains, induces sleep, and produces healthy flesh, etc.

### Quintessence of Sulfur

**Take:** Flowers of sulfur <term>florum sulphuris: the fine powder collected after heating sulfur into a vapor and letting it cool</term>, half a pound.

### The Form of a Penetrating Spirit of Life

**Take:** Waters of lemon balm, roses, wallflower <note>original: "cheiri"; the common wallflower, used in herbal medicine for heart health</note>, sage, and balsam, one pound of each.

Finally, a roasted apple, crushed, and the following spices must be added to this liquid.
**Take:** Cinnamon, cloves, and mace, two drams of each.

### Certain curative experiments for removing any symptoms or complications in wounds.

#### First experiment.

℟. <note>Recipe; take the following</note> Hepatic aloe <note>original: "Aloe epaticum"; a dark, liver-colored variety of aloe prized for its purgative and wound-healing properties</note>, and with raw Tereniabin <term>a Persian-derived term for "manna," a sweet resinous sap used as a mild laxative and binding agent</term>, distill into an oil, which by the best right can be called a Balsam. For by it are cured gangrenes and cavernous <note>deep or hollow</note> ulcers, which ruined and ruining doctors falsely and with a fabricated name call the "Penance of St. John" and "Vengeance," etc. <note>This likely refers to historical folk names for severe skin conditions like "St. Anthony’s Fire" or similar afflictions attributed to divine punishment.</note>

#### Second experiment.

℟. Sal ammoniac <note>original: "Salis armoniaci"; ammonium chloride, often used in alchemy and medicine as a cleansing agent</note>: ℥. iij. <term>three ounces; approximately 84 grams</term>
Common salt: ℥. ß. <term>half an ounce; approximately 14 grams</term>
Let them be placed in water and boiled. Afterward, add "ink-gum" <note>original: "gummi atramentarij"; likely a reference to vitriol or iron sulfate, which was used to make black ink and as an astringent in medicine</note>: ℥. iij. <term>three ounces</term>
Let them boil again, and be cooked down until they reach a hard consistency. Cast a portion of this, the size of a bean, into a measure of water; by washing with this water, any ulcers are cleansed and become very fresh, most excellently disposed for healing.

#### Third experiment.

Let three veins existing at the feet be opened <note>A reference to bloodletting, a standard practice to balance the "humors" and draw inflammation away from a wound.</note>. Afterward, let hepatic aloe and raw Tereniabin be cooked together. Let the ulcers be washed with that decoction.

#### Fourth experiment.

Place feverfew <note>original: "Parthenionem"</note> and goldenrod <note>original: "consolidam auream"</note>, crushed together, upon the ulcers; let them remain for three [days...] <note>The text cuts off here at the bottom of the page.</note>

It notably criticizes contemporary physicians for using religious folklore to name diseases they cannot cure.</summary>

### The Secret of Tartar.
<term>Arcanum; a "secret" or high-level medicinal preparation in the tradition of Paracelsus, believed to have potent life-restoring properties.</term>

Take: 3 lbs of tartar <note>the potassium bitartrate crust found in wine barrels</note> burnt in a reverberatory furnace <note>a furnace that reflects heat back onto the material</note> until it is very white. Let it be dissolved in the finest "ardent wine" <note>original: "vino ardente"; distilled alcohol or brandy</note> in a Mary's bath <note>balneum mariæ; a double boiler or water bath used for gentle heating</note> for seven days to allow for putrefaction <note>a period of sitting where the components begin to break down and transform</note>. Afterward, let this material be distilled through a very strong earthenware vessel, first with a light fire, and toward the end a very violent one, following the process for making *aqua fortis* <note>nitric acid; the text refers to a high-heat distillation method</note>.

First, a blackish liquor will rise along with the ardent wine, which can indeed be separated from the substance of the tartar by a water bath. Then, let the remaining dregs be burnt again in the reverberatory furnace for twenty-four hours, having first been moistened with an equal weight of ardent wine. You shall repeat this process as many times as necessary until nothing more of the tartar remains from the distillation. Finally, once you have finished drying all such collected liquors—having been highly purified <note>rectified</note>—into a powder by the heat of the sun; and from that material, the spirit, as
m 3 ex <note>The catchword "ex" indicates the start of the next page</note>

<note>original: "ex calcinato tartaro..."</note> ...previously made from calcined tartar, being entirely drawn out. The dose is 1 grain with 1/2 drachm <note>original: "ʒ. ß."</note> of Mithridate <note>a famous, complex ancient "universal" antidote and cure-all</note>, in 1 1/2 oz <note>original: "℥. j. ß."</note> of chicory water or distilled plantain water; it should be administered several times, according to the needs of the individual. *Liquor of Tartar.*

Let tartar <note>the potassium bitartrate crust from wine barrels</note> from white wine be calcined <note>reduced to ash through high heat</note> until white, and by the process of oil of vitriol <note>sulfuric acid</note>, let it be distilled into a liquor. Afterward, with a cucurbit <note>a gourd-shaped glass distillation vessel</note> set upright, let the phlegm <note>the watery, inactive portion of a distillate</note> be separated from the oil. Then let this be distilled through ashes into an oil of golden color, for curing ulcers, especially those that take their origin from the venereal disease <note>syphilis</note>.

*Formula for Water of Tartar.*

You shall rectify <note>purify through repeated distillation</note> the best tartar, very sharply distilled into a liquor, in a Pelican vessel <note>a specialized circulatory vessel with "arms" that feed the distillate back into the main body</note>; with the addition of an equal part of rectified "water of life" <note>original: "aquæ vitæ"; concentrated distilled alcohol</note>, you shall administer ten drops at each time.

### Treatment for a "Testudo" or "Celsa." 
<note>A 'testudo' (literally "turtle") was a medical term for a soft, rounded swelling or cyst, often on the head, which resembled a turtle's shell; 'celsa' is a related term for a protrusion.</note>

The area should be surrounded with feather alum <note>original: "alumine plumoso"; a fibrous, feather-like form of naturally occurring alum or asbestos used as a styptic</note>, and after the scab <note>original: "eschara"; the dry crust or "eschar" resulting from a burn or cautery</note> has fallen off, let Opodeldoc <note>original: "Oppodeltoch"; a famous medicinal plaster or liniment popularized by Paracelsus, typically containing soap, camphor, and various oils</note> be applied; in this way, it putrefies toward ejection. Let it be closed with common plasters.
*From the "Book on Abscesses," Chapter 31.*

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on systemic healing, specifically concluding the theoretical introduction to the restoration of the blood's temperament before detailing the practical alchemical steps for extracting a medicinal tincture from gold.</meta>

BOOK ONE. 197

...restoration and renewal of the blood to its original temperament <note>The "temperament" refers to the balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to govern health</note>, as will be described in the section on administration.

->**The practice of extraction.**<-

The "body" of gold must be removed from its metallic nature through corruption <note>In alchemy, "corruption" is the process of breaking down a substance's physical form to release its inner essence</note>, so that it can no longer withstand the strike of a hammer. This is achieved through "water of salt." The residue is then washed with fresh water; the tincture is finally extracted using spirit of wine <note>original: "spiritum vini"; high-concentration alcohol or ethanol</note>; and lastly, the spirit is evaporated away from the tincture, which will remain at the bottom of the vessel.

->**Preparation of the water of salt.**<-

It is made from liquid salt, or from salt that has been liquefied, which exists by its own nature—aside from the boiling or artificial preparation by which salts are usually made. Let it be liquefied several times; then, once powdered, let it be mixed with the juice of radish roots and shaken until it dissolves, then distilled. Let this distillate be mixed with <term>bloodstone</term> <note>original: "sanguineæ"; likely refers here to a mineral like hematite or a specific plant known for its red juice</note> while it is still green; once mixed, let them be distilled again five times. In this liquor, thin sheets of <term>Sol</term> <note>the alchemical name and symbol for Gold</note>—which have been purified by antimony—are dissolved into a powder. This powder should then be washed carefully with pure, fresh, distilled water so that all the acrimony <note>the sharp, corrosive quality</note> of the salt departs. Indeed, it is easily washed because the substance does not mix deeply but allows the water to separate, so that a "sweet" <note>meaning non-corrosive or neutralized</note> powder results; and it remains true to its own nature, etc.

->**Extraction of the spirit of wine.**<-

->*Elucidation of the more obscure passages contained in the practice.*<-

The greatest difficulty consists in the <term>corruption</term> <note>in alchemy, "corruption" is not decay, but the essential first step of breaking down a substance's physical form to release its inner spirit</note> of gold; for it is a common opinion that this can hardly be broken down by art, nor overcome by any fire. But our author teaches in this place that it is done with the "water of salt." Gold can be <term>calcined</term> <note>reduced to a powder through heat</note>, or reduced to a fine dust by Mercury or by the "smoke of Saturn" <note>original: "fumo Saturni"; lead vapors</note>, though without much use; and also by <term>aqua regia</term> <note>literally "royal water," a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold</note>.

However, this powder or lime <note>original: "calx"</note> returns again to its original state—that is, into a malleable metal—by the use of bellows and fire. Paracelsus by no means intends for this simple powdering to be considered "corruption." Instead, he expressly suggests to us that the golden body, the most solid among all metals, must be "corrupted" in such a way that it no longer retains or sustains its metallic nature, and is entirely removed from its metallic essence.

He teaches that this is done by the "water of salt," though he speaks quite obscurely. This is so that this secret, most excellent above all others, might not be made plain to the unworthy, who are enemies of nature no less than of art. Nevertheless, as much as I am able, I shall reveal that salt to the "sons of art" <note>a traditional term for sincere alchemical practitioners</note> and those studious of the mysteries. 

In the bowels of the earth and in the mountains, a salt of nature is hidden, naturally "cooked" <note>matured by the earth's internal heat over time</note>, which is called "stony" <note>original: "petreum"; likely referring to saltpeter or rock salt</note>, not formed by any human art. Once dug out and melted by fire, it sometimes acquires powers that are most destructive to the physical form of gold, especially if it is afterwards dissolved in the juice of radish roots. In this

200    ON CURING DISEASES

In this place, many self-styled experts <note>original: "nasutuli"; literally "people with big noses," meaning those who think they are clever or overly critical</note>, rather than true philosophers, are accustomed to hesitate over whether the discussion concerns the vegetable radish root or some mineral substance. Their doubting minds will not delay me from understanding these words entirely according to the letter. For in this passage, we must consider not only the degree of sharpness but also the method of dissolving the prescribed salt, though both must be weighed by us. Lullus <note>Ramon Llull, a 13th-century philosopher and major influence on alchemy</note> says that the <term>menstrua</term> <note>solvents used to extract the essence of a substance</note> of the philosophers' sharpness are found in certain vegetables. Leaving those aside, let us return to the dissolution of salt.

What common man would doubt that any salt is dulled when dissolved in common water or other fresh water? It is very well known to the most experienced that the more often it is washed, the milder it becomes and the more of its sharpness it loses; consequently, it is rendered less corrosive. Hence it happens that salt is taught to be dissolved in sharper liquids and from more acrid vegetables, which are by no means lacking in their own natural corrosive salt.

I have no doubt that this very thing is to be understood regarding "sanguinary" <note>likely *Sanguinaria* or "bloodwort," a plant used in traditional medicine</note> while it is green; even if it seems otherwise to several—indeed, to very many—who wish it to be understood as a mineral and turn their attention to <term>vitriol</term> <note>metallic sulfates, often green or blue in color</note>. The passage is manifest in itself; by these words ("while it is green"), he implies that the juice of such a vegetable must be extracted at the time when it still flourishes. It is as if he were saying: there is no reason for you to allow it to dry out or waste away.

One cannot suspect this refers to vitriol, since vitriol is always green; and although it sometimes turns pale according to the qualities of the places from which it is dug, tending toward a sky-blue color, yellow, or white, the greenness is nonetheless more or less

->BOOK ONE. 201<-

...is seen to be present to a greater or lesser degree. It must also be considered that the author said **sanguinary** <note>original: "sanguineam"; the feminine form of the word</note> and not **sanguineous** <note>original: "sanguineum"; the neuter/masculine form</note>, in order to imply a herb <note>The Latin word for herb, *herba*, is feminine; the author argues the grammar proves a plant is intended</note>, and not a vegetable substance in general, or something else. Therefore, excellent reader, seek a herb endowed with no less sharpness than the radish itself, wherever you might find it, which is called "sanguinary." In the juices of this herb and the radish, let the aforementioned salt be dissolved and "sharpened" <note>made more chemically reactive</note> for greater powers and faculties in breaking down gold.

Now we come to the understanding of **cold distillation**. However, the author did not say that the process itself is literally "cold," but rather that it is "completed coldly." This means it is performed with a very mild, tempered heat in comparison to the other distillations usually employed in different extractions. He believes a sign should be noted in the **helmet** <note>original: "gallea"; the cap or head of a distilling apparatus, also called an alembic</note> of the distilling vessel when rays, filaments, or small droplets of physical moisture appear. For the spirit must be invisible while it rises, and it is not perceptible until it has condensed by the cold into a "body," which serves as its vehicle. 

Indeed, it is necessary for our chemical spirits to have a body in which they are enclosed; otherwise, they would escape our hands and our power. Yet this body is so rare <note>thin or ethereal</note> that unless it is restrained by some vessel, it vanishes entirely. **Alcohol** <term>Alcohol</term> <note>In this period, "alcohol" did not refer to liquid spirits, but to an extremely fine, impalpable powder, often produced by sublimation or intense grinding</note> refers to a powder that has not been crushed by a pestle or a mortar, but only by "resolutions" <note>chemical breakdowns or reductions</note>.

It remains to explain what the author wished to keep hidden under a riddle. You have **stumbled** <note>original: "pedem offendistis"; literally "tripped your foot"</note> against this **Lydian stone** <note>a touchstone used to test the purity of gold; here used metaphorically as a difficult test of understanding</note> (you who have translated Paracelsus in this place). This is clear because the goal of the whole business consists in that very thing—namely, in that "graduation" <note>the process of increasing the potency or purity of a substance</note>. The riddle is this: (that it is by no means dealt with beyond the **quintessence** <note>the "fifth essence," the purest and most medicinal part of a substance</note>...

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Paracelsian alchemical methods, transitioning from the distillation of plant juices to the specific numerical and symbolic logic used in the creation of the "quintessence" and the "fixation" of substances.</meta>

202      ON CURING DISEASES

...it permits itself to be handled thus, that is, in 2. 4. 0. But if anyone should presume to go further, it is **burned**. Listen, I ask you. Have you ever seen the **quintessence**? <note>The "fifth essence" or purest part of a substance, beyond the four elements</note> You do not even know the process for preparing it. Do you know it is made through **circulation**? <note>A process of continuous distillation and condensation in a closed vessel, meant to "refine" the spirit</note> What else does Paracelsus strive to suggest to the "sons of his doctrine," than that the circle is driven toward infinity through a multiplication that is always doubled, until it rests in **unity**.

This is clear through the first number: 2. 4. 8. 16. 32. 64. 128. 256. 512. and **0**, which is a circular mark designating infinite motion, ending in the character of rest—that is, of **fixation** <note>original: "fixionis"; the process by which a volatile substance is made stable and resistant to fire</note>—and unity. Understand this no differently: this passage in the **German copy** <note>original: "exemplari germanico"</note> (which says "doubled five times, that is, five times in twice twenty-four"). 

Please see by what reasoning they were led astray when they tried to explain these numbers. In **2. 4. 0.**, they did not notice that among the significant marks of arithmetic, the points between 2 and 4 denote not 24, but 2 and 4, the beginning of a binary or doubled multiplication. Because of this, they corrupted the author’s intent, which was proposed to us as a **riddle** <note>original: "ænigma"</note> through a polished series of words, and they perverted it into a chimerical fantasy that signifies nothing except their own imagination. 

They should have said instead: "which is doubled through 2. 4. etc., ending in 0." The experts of the mystical matters of this art and the writings of Paracelsus would have revealed this to us and to everyone. Or they should have interpreted it in this way: "which is doubled twice, that is, twice in twice four, etc." and in **0**, rolling in a circle, it returns to unity, by which the **fixation** of the **graduated substance** <term>graduatæ: a substance that has been raised to a specific degree of potency or purity through alchemical stages</term> is signified, not that with which **gold** is endowed...
                                                                    
<summary>The author critiques a mistranslation in German editions of Paracelsus, arguing that numerical sequences like '2. 4. 0.' are not literal numbers but symbols for the alchemical process of doubling and circulation. He explains that these sequences represent the path to chemical 'fixation' and the creation of the quintessence.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, quintessence, circulation, binary multiplication, fixation, alchemical notation, German manuscript, numerical symbolism, graduation</keywords>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Paracelsian alchemical methods, moving from the mathematical symbolism of the "circle" to the physical process of "fixation" and the correction of errors in previous German-to-Latin translations.</meta>

BOOK ONE. 203

**Gold** is the most stable of all bodies in fire, but only insofar as its **spirits** <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are the volatile, active components of a substance that can be driven off by heat</note> are brought to such a high degree of simplicity that they cast off all "number"—that is, they cast off the physical body. Conversely, a body is also considered **fixed** <note>original: "fixum"; rendered stable and resistant to the transformative power of fire</note> when it is freed from every volatile spirit, or when it is compacted with a united, fixed spirit and made into a single entity. At this point, it can no longer be divided into different parts; it becomes one and the same thing.

Therefore, the **fixation of spirits** is their reduction to their own simplicity and "penetrative power." Through this power, they enter into simple or united bodies and afterwards embrace each other so closely that they can hardly be separated by any art. They either remain together in the fire or they depart together. If, however, they happen to be separated by some artifice, they turn into nothing else but "one and one," which cannot agree with anything except unity.

This refers to the **riddle** <note>original: "ænigma"</note> that **Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot and occultist who influenced Paracelsus</note> proposes in his secrets: *“Let the binary and the ternary be rejected, and the trinity is reducible to the simplicity of unity.”* <note>original: "Reiiciatur binarius & ternarius ad vnitatis ſimplicitatem eſt reducibilis"</note>

Nor should we forget the error committed at the very start of the description of this **tincture** <note>A potent medicinal or alchemical extract</note>. In that text, it is written: “The color transcends into the spirit, and a white powder remains at the bottom; separate it, and make the powder flow, and from there will be made a **watery metal** <note>original: "metallum aqueum"</note>, or **metallic water**.” 

You should read this not as "watery metal," but as **white metal**. This error arose from the **German language**, where the word *Wasser* <note>German for "water"</note> was mistaken for *Weisser* <note>German for "white"</note>.

Now we must move on to other tinctures.

### *On the Secret of the Tincture of Corals.*

Let its body be ground into **alcohol** <note>original: "alcool"; here meaning a "micro-fine powder" rather than a liquid</note>, and let its tincture be extracted, just as it is from gold through **spirit of wine** <note>Distilled ethanol</note>. Afterwards, let them **ascend** <note>Distill or vaporize</note> together sixteen times, and finally—

...that they may be better separated. The "ascent" <note>In distillation, "ascent" refers to the vapor rising from the heated substance</note> is performed by distillation over an **open fire** <note>original: "nudum ignem," literally "naked fire," referring to direct heat rather than a sand or water bath</note>, and the separation is done in a water bath six times by pouring the liquid back over the dregs. The oil should be saved, which is to be administered in theriac water, in doses from one <term>scruple</term><note>A unit of weight, roughly 1.3 grams</note> to one <term>drachm</term><note>A unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams</note>, etc.

### *Preparation of Theriac Water*
℞. <note>The "Recipe" or "Take" symbol</note> **Spirit of wine** <note>original: "Spiritus vini," a high-proof alcohol or brandy</note>: 5 <term>ounces</term>.
**Theriac**: 2 ½ ounces. <note>A famous ancient "poly-pharmaceutical" compound used as a universal antidote</note>
**Myrrh and red roses**: 1 ounce, 2 drachms.
**Oriental saffron**: 2 drachms.
All these mixed together are to be distilled once.

### *Its use in administration*
Let 1 ½ drachms of the **Arcanum** <note>A "secret" or highly purified medicinal essence</note> or **tincture of corals** be mixed with 10 ounces of **chicory water**, or **germander** <note>original: "chamedrios aut gamandreæ," medicinal herbs in the mint family</note>. Give a quarter-ounce of this mixture to the patient in the morning and evening, five hours before lunch and five hours after dinner; do this for six or seven continuous days. The patient should eat lighter foods, and for drink, use distilled water of **fumitory** <note>original: "fumo terræ," literally "smoke of the earth," a plant used for skin and liver ailments</note>, chicory, or similar plants, which should be used only in moderation. When no more pus is seen to flow from the ulcers, the use of the tincture must be stopped, as is done in other cases.

### *Concerning the Tincture of Balsam*
℞. **Balsam**: ½ ounce.
**Spirit of wine**: 20 ounces.
Let them be circulated together in a **pelican** <note>A specific alchemical vessel with two side-arms that loop back into the body, allowing vapors to condense and flow back into the liquid for continuous "circulation"</note> in the sun for the space of one month. Afterward, let both be distilled through an **alembic** <note>A traditional distillation apparatus with a cooling cap</note>: this method of distillation is better than using a **retort** <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck</note>. This mixture remains as a single composition, to which finally another half-ounce of balsam should be added; let it be digested again in a closed glass vessel and mixed with the others.

the patient [takes it] for three, four, or more weeks; other potions should be omitted, and the ulcers should be lightly covered with common plasters <note>Simple medical dressings or bandages</note>. For the tincture works sufficiently from within to heal the external parts.

->*All the difficulty lies in this word, alcohol.*<-

Some people, quite thoughtlessly, interpret this word (**Alcohol**) and too rashly claim it refers to a very finely ground powder, as if it were made with iron pestles and mortars or other hard tools. However, the error is sufficiently proven by the fact that in various places Paracelsus writes about the "alcohol of wine," by which he means a rectified **ardent water** <note>original: "aquam ardentem," referring to high-proof distilled spirits or ethanol</note>, which has a far different form and is entirely unlike a powder. It is clear, therefore, that the **Alcohol** of any things or bodies is nothing other than their purer and cleaner substance, separated from its own impurity.

Let us see, then, what the "alcohol of antimony" is. In this case, a distinction must be made between solids and liquids. Solids can also be reduced to a most minute substance by means other than those mentioned before—namely, by fire, heat, solutions, freezing, sublimations, and so on. This is our author's <note>Referring to Paracelsus</note> opinion: that antimony should not only be ground into a powder with a pestle and mortar, but also into a certain volatile substance, unchanged from its native color. This must be achieved solely through the benefit and governance of fire and heat, so that after it has been ground in the common way, it may finally be ground **philosophically** <note>In alchemy, "philosophical" grinding or preparation refers to a spiritual or chemical refinement using heat and "secret" solvents rather than just physical force</note>.

<summary>This page explains that the Tincture of Antimony heals ulcers internally and provides a crucial definition of 'Alcohol' as a purified essence rather than just a fine powder. The author emphasizes that 'philosophical' preparation through heat is superior to physical grinding.</summary>

BOOK ONE. 207
...be worn down by sublimations <term>Sublimation: the process where a solid turns into vapor and solidifies again, used here as a chemical metaphor for purification</term>, which are the pestles and mortars of the philosophers. He <note>Paracelsus</note> teaches in another place that this sublimation of antimony must be done without any "dead head" <term>Caput mortuum: the useless, inert residue left over after a chemical process like distillation or sublimation</term>. It is sufficiently clear, therefore, that nothing [impure] remains with it.

    This sublimation of antimony <note>original: "stibii," a Latin term for the mineral ore of antimony</note> is necessary first; otherwise, the work is frustrated, and the labor undertaken for the "flowers" <term>Flowers: a term in early chemistry for the fine, flaky powder or crystals produced by sublimation</term> and the tincture of antimony is in vain. The majority of those who perform the "anatomy" <note>An "anatomy" here refers to the chemical breakdown or analysis of a substance</note> of Paracelsian preparations are deceived when attempting this secret. Immediately after the common grinding of the antimony, they sublimate it into a white soot. But they have often experienced to their own loss that they could sooner climb to heaven than reach the true lemon-yellow and red flowers of antimony [through that method]. This is not a common method, but a purely philosophical artifice, known to very few until now. It must therefore be revealed to the sons of doctrine <note>A traditional term for dedicated students of alchemy and hermetic medicine</note>. A sublimation of this kind must be done with a very well-tempered fire, so that the powder of antimony does not melt, but nevertheless ascends, just as the "flower of flour" <note>fine flour dust</note> is seen sticking to the walls of mills. Behold, you now have the entire secret of this business, and (unless you are a blockhead) you can understand what I mean.

### *On the Tincture of the Salt of the Philosophers.*

| Ingredient | Amount |
| :--- | :--- |
| Salt of Gold | half a dram |

| Salt of Lemon Balm | half a dram |
| Pure, common Rock Salt | 8 ounces |

    Mix and eat spread upon a piece of bread.
Do not wonder if it is called "Salt of Gold" or "Salt of Antimony," which cannot properly be called salts, but rather alkalis <note>The author is clarifying that these "salts" are actually alkaline substances extracted through chemical means, rather than table salt</note>...

...made, let the first ointment be formed from these.
*The second ointment.*
Let it be made from an ointment of egg yolks mixed with alum <term>Alum: a naturally occurring mineral salt used historically as an astringent to dry wounds and stop bleeding</term>.
*The third.*
Let it be made with an ointment of white lead <term>Ceruse (white lead): a lead-based pigment used in antiquity and the Renaissance to treat skin ailments, despite its toxicity</term> and calcined vitriol <term>Vitriol: a sulfate of a metal (like iron or copper) that has been "calcined" or heated until it becomes a dry powder</term>.
Treatment should first begin with the second ointment. If the ulcer cannot be cured by that, one must return to the first ointment, and if it refuses to be cured by that one, the third will cure it most effectively. The order of treatment and the sequence of ointments must not be changed, even if we use the second ointment first, etc.

[The first] is by the Bath: the second by the removal of tumors <note>In this period, "tumor" usually refers to any localized swelling or inflammation</note>, the third by cleansing, the fourth by consolidation <note>promoting the healing of tissue</note>, the fifth by the conclusion.

### Bath over vapors.

℞ <note>Recipe or "Take"</note>. Swallow-wort <note>original: hirundinariæ; likely *Vincetoxicum hirundinaria*</note>, 2 ounces <note>original: ℥.ij.</note>.

Elderflowers, 3 handfuls.
Chamomile flowers, 2.5 handfuls.

### Cleansing.

℞. Ointment made from egg yolks, which becomes more potent when mixed with calcined material <note>powdered minerals or substances reduced to ash by heat</note>. Let this be replenished twice every day for as long as it takes until you perceive the base [of the ulcer] to be in the best condition, and free from foulness and stench, etc.

Every twelve hours let the moistening of the linen be repeated and applied, and this for a third time; when this is finished, it will cast off a thin eschar <note>original: excaram; a dry crust or scab of dead tissue that must be removed for a wound to heal properly</note>.

->*Preparation of Mercury.*<-
Coagulate mercury using alum water, and reduce the mercury coagulated in this way into a powder. It affects the patient with no pain.

Varnish, 2.5 ounces <note>original: ℥. ij. ß.</note>.

Use this to wash the wound to encourage the growth of new flesh <note>original: ad incarnationem</note>, and sprinkle the ulcers with a powder ground from these same ingredients. This will be your most certain remedy for ulcers influenced by the stars.

220 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.
The *Great Surgery*, Part II, Book III, Treatise II, Chapters 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12.

### *Definitions and Treatment of Accidental Symptoms or Complications of Wounds.*

#### *Paroxysm in Wounds*
This is said to occur when wounds first become intensely hot, and afterwards a chill sets in, much like a fever, occurring on alternating days or hours.

#### *Burning*
Where heat arises in the wound which extends through the whole body, then ceases, and returns at its own determined time, with no chill following it.

#### *Numbness*
<note>original: Stupefactio</note>
This is said to happen when the wound seems to "fall asleep," as if ants were scurrying about inside it. <note>This sensation is known in medicine as formication.</note>

### *Treatment of these complications*
♃ <note>The symbol ♃ (Jupiter) is used here as the "Recipe" or "Take" command.</note> Calamine stone. <note>A mineral ore of zinc, used for skin irritations.</note>
Litharge. <note>Lead(II) oxide.</note>
Minium <note>Red lead.</note>, of each half a pound. <note>The symbol ℔ represents a pound; ß represents a half.</note>
Tutty <note>A crude form of zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note>, 2 ounces.
Wax.
Myrtle oil, of each 9 ounces.
Cooked together into a wax-salve <term>ceratum: a stiff ointment made of wax, oil, and medicinal ingredients</term>, let these be applied. Similarly, oil of camphor may be smeared on the wounds. Also, half an ounce of Mithridatic conserve <note>Mithridate was a famous "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, believed to cure any poison or exhaustion.</note> taken as a drink; these things cure individually on their own, or may be applied together as necessity requires.

#### *Swelling of Wounds, or "The Bag"*
<note>original: Saccus</note>
This occurs when the wound swells up along with heat, as if...

**Wasting of a limb, or dryness.**
This is said to occur when wounds are poorly treated, so that the natural fluids find the passages not free but obstructed; nature then consumes these fluids, and due to a lack of nourishment, the limbs are forced to wither.

**Tremor.**
When trembling invades the limb and the wound, even after healing, so that they cannot be at rest.

**Contracture.**
When a limb is rendered powerless and is too weak to lift even the slightest weight.

**Contraction.**
Whenever a limb remains stretched out or bent, so that it can neither be flexed nor extended.

226 ON CURING DISEASES

The Cure.
℞. Arsenical thread, with which you may cut away the growth <note>original: fungum; "fungus" in this context refers to a soft, spongy, morbid growth or "proud flesh," not a botanical fungus</note>.
Stop the blood, and thoroughly anoint the wound with burning oil. By that oil, the warts and growths will be consumed from their very roots and the branches from which they arise. Once the consumption is complete, you shall treat the pores with a plaster of Mummy. If, however, the blood should flow out too abundantly, loosen the vein behind the growth and cool it, as has been said concerning the suppression of blood.

Pustulous fistula.
It is detected when a wound is suddenly closed up too much before it has established a foundation <note>meaning before the deep tissue has healed properly</note>, and for that reason, it rots.

The Cure.
℞. Liquid of amber <note>original: Liquorum carabis; "carabe" was a common term for amber used in pharmacy</note>.
Asphalt <note>bitumen</note>.
Ammoniacum, of each half a dram <note>original: ana ʒ.ß.; "ana" means equal parts, ʒ is the symbol for a dram, and ß represents a half</note>.
Wax-salve of Mummy, 3 drams.
Mix together into a plaster and apply.

Obstruction.
<note>original: Opilatio; a medical term for a blockage or stoppage of the body's natural excretory paths</note>
It occurs from a weakness of nature <note>the body's vital force</note> due to an excessive loss of blood, which results in the patient having no movement of the bowels nor of the bladder.

The Cure.
Let the patient be purged with Sagapenum <note>original: Serapino; a gum resin from the *Ferula* plant used as a laxative</note>, or overseas Mummy, with a proper weight mixed into the wound-healing potions, which must be used together with them.

Hemoptysis and the spitting of blood.
<note>original: Hæmoptoica; the coughing up of blood from the respiratory tract</note>
It proceeds from congealed blood which, having been retained internally and not at all purged from the beginning, seeks an outlet through the mouth.

The Cure
