228 ON CURING DISEASES

...be mixed with sulfur powder. Let it be distilled and the oil separated from the water for thoroughly healing ulcers.

*The most excellent cure of ulcers through the*
*oil of Mercury:*

Let mercury be calcined <note>Reduced to a powder through heat</note> using nitric acid <note>original: aquam fortem; literally "strong water," the historical term for this corrosive acid</note> several times, and afterwards let it be washed with spirits of wine <note>original: aqua ardente; literally "burning water," referring to high-proof alcohol or brandy</note> so that it sheds the corrosive nature of the nitric acid. Let it be digested in vinegar, and its salt extracted through a filter. Let the vinegar be driven off using a water bath <note>original: per balneum; likely a "balneum Mariae" or double boiler, used for gentle heating</note> so that it turns into a dry white powder. Let this be washed with greater celandine water <note>original: aqua chelidoniæ; celandine was a common herb in medieval medicine used for skin and eye issues</note>; finally, once dried, let it be dissolved in a moist place into a very clear liquor for thoroughly healing any ulcers whatsoever.

*The cure of ulcers in the mouth.*
Take: Cemented copper, 2 ounces <note>The symbol ℥ represents an ounce; "cemented" refers to a specific chemical purification process involving heat and salts</note>.
Burnt white alum.
Rock alum, of each 6 ounces <note>original: ana; a medical term meaning "equal parts of each"</note>.
Distilled vinegar, 1 pound <note>The symbol ℔ represents a libra or pound</note>.
Let the alkali be extracted.
*There may be added:*
To this, flowers of copper, 1 ounce <note>original: floris æris; usually refers to verdigris or the fine powder produced by the oxidation of copper</note>.
Celandine water, 3 ounces.
Alum water, 1 ounce.
From these, let a gargle be made.
From the *Book of Preparations*, concerning copper.

*The cleansing of ulcers, through Cardonus*
*made from vitriol.*

Let 5 ounces of this be suspended in twenty measures of fresh wine, or grape juice <note>original: musti; unfermented grape juice</note>, and let them be purged with it; let one spoonful of it be taken at a time.

### Book II, on the openings of the skin, Chapter III.

*The cure of ulcers through balms.*

Take: 4 scruples <note>original: ℈.iiij.; a scruple is an apothecary weight equal to about 1.3 grams</note> of leaf gold <note>original: Solis foliati; "Sol" or "The Sun" was the alchemical name for gold</note>.
1.5 ounces <note>original: ℥.iß.</note> of spirits of wine <note>High-proof alcohol</note> infused with pine, Lemon Balm, etc.

Finally, extract the balm by means of a water bath <note>original: balneum; a Bain-Marie</note>. This cures things far greater than ulcers.

*Balm from iron.*
First, let it be reduced into <term>crocus</term> <note>original: crocum; a fine metallic powder or rust, specifically iron oxide or "Saffron of Mars"</note> by means of distilled vinegar; from that, let <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>Ammonium chloride, used in alchemy as a flux or to help dissolve metals</note> be drawn off nine times, and afterward let it be reduced into a perfectly sweetened oil.

*The use of both is:*

Let the Golden [balm] be applied to the ulcers three times every week.
But let the Iron one be applied once every twelve hours.

*Balm from copper.*
Reduce the copper again into its <term>vitriol</term> <note>Sulfate of copper, often called blue vitriol</note>; [turn] that into water, and the <term>colcothar</term> <note>The brownish-red residue left after the distillation of vitriol</note> into a red oil.

*Balm from lead, or the sweetness of Saturn.*
Let the lead be reduced to ashes, from which its spirit may be drawn off by distilled vinegar, so that a sweetness of this kind <note>A reference to "sugar of lead" or lead acetate, which has a deceptively sweet taste</note> may pass into an oil.

Balm <note>Catchword for the next page</note>

### *The cure of ulcers through internal medicines.*

Let these be boiled in wine, in the manner of a wound-healing potion, and add 1 ½ ounces <note>original: ℥. j. β.</note> of **oil of cloves** extracted by distillation. Let three equal drafts be administered every day, etc.

### *The cure of such fistulas by anointing.*

Distill together:
3 ounces of **oil of bricks**. <note>original: olei laterini; a traditional chemical preparation made by heating bricks soaked in oil, believed to have "resolving" properties</note>
6 ounces of oil of turpentine.
1 ½ ounces of oil of cloves.

To this distillate, you shall add:
**Myrrh**,
**Frankincense**,
**Mastic**, 2 ounces of each. <note>original: ana ℥. ij.; "ana" indicates equal parts</note>
**Mummy**, 3 ounces. <note>original: Mummiæ; a substance used in historical medicine, often referring to bitumen or prepared remains, believed to stop bleeding and heal wounds</note>

And you shall distill it a second time. You shall inject the fluid through a syringe into the opening, all the way to the bottom, twice every day, and wash the area frequently with salt water or wine.

**Oil of Saturn**. <note>original: Oleum Saturni; a preparation of lead, often used for its cooling and drying properties on the skin</note>

Hematite prepared in this way is most useful for wounds and for ulcers, **lupus**, **herpes**, etc., when blood flows from them. <note>In historical medicine, "lupus" (the wolf) referred to any corrosive or gnawing skin disease</note> For it constricts the veins, so that no blood can flow from them...

### The Care of Internal Ulcers

Take: **Mastic** <note>original: "masticis"; a resin from the mastic tree, used to strengthen the stomach and dry up excess moisture.</note>, half a drachm; **Hermodactyls** <note>original: "hermodactilorum"; a root used historically to treat joint pain and "phlegmatic" conditions.</note>, half a drachm.

...from it a lye <note>original: lixiuium; an alkaline solution made from ashes</note> should be prepared, cooked down to dryness, and solidified.

As much of both types of **petroleum** <note>likely referring to "white" and "black" naphtha or rock oils</note> as is sufficient. Let them be dissolved by cooking into a liquid, which is then dried out.

*The second.*

**Myrrh** with **Tragacanth** <note>a natural gum used as a stabilizer</note> is first distilled by descent <note>original: per descensum; a distillation process where the substance is heated from above so the essence flows downward into a receiver</note> and then dried out. By this, every kind of rotting ulcer is cured, both internally and externally.

*Corrosive experiments, reduced to sweetness.* <note>In early chemistry, "sweetening" (dulcoration) was the process of washing or refining harsh, acidic, or corrosive substances—like mineral salts—to make them mild enough for the human body</note>

The **sweetness of Tartar** <note>potassium bitartrate residue from wine making</note> and **Mummy** should be calcined <note>burned to a powder</note> with a most powerful fire for 24 hours. Afterward, let it be sweetened with the **water of Culicula** <note>original: aqua Culiculę; possibly referring to a specific distilled preparation or a specialized chemical vessel</note>, dried out, and then distilled fifteen or sixteen times again with **rectified wine**; it should always be dried out through extraction. Finally, let it be dissolved upon a marble slab into a water—one that is neither fatty nor oily—which will emerge "sweet" for the thorough healing of ulcers and wounds.

*Sweetness of Vitriol or Balsam.* <note>Vitriol refers to metal sulfates; "sweet vitriol" often referred to the resulting medicinal liquid after the corrosive sulfuric acid was removed or neutralized</note>

**Take**: **Colcothar of vitriol**, <note>original: Colcotar; the reddish-brown iron oxide residue left after the distillation of oil of vitriol</note> and extract the redness from it...

BOOK ONE. 246

...distill it with rainwater, which you shall solidify; and from that same solidified substance, extract the redness again. Do this five or six times, or even more often, until that material thickens into a hardness like a stone, which, when applied to the tongue, brings a sensation of extreme sweetness. By this remedy, *syrones* <note>original: syrones; likely referring to skin mites or the small, itchy ulcers caused by scabies</note> and other very serious ulcers are cured. *The sweetness of Antimony, or its Balsam, or Mummy.*

Dissolve **Antimony** with **sal ammoniac** <term>ammonium chloride, used as a flux and solvent in alchemy</term>; then, finally, sweeten it with rainwater. Afterward, once it is dried, dissolve it upon a marble slab as many times as necessary until it possesses a very deep red color that is clear and transparent. With this medicine, fistulas and cancerous ulcers complicated by the venereal disease <note>original: venereo morbo; referring to syphilis, which was often treated with these heavy metal preparations</note> are cured most excellently.

Saffron of Mars <term>Crocus Martis; an alchemical preparation of iron oxide, usually red or yellow, used in external applications to dry wounds and stop bleeding</term>, half a pound.

If you take these prescribed recipes into your hands (which I know you will not easily do), then you will first understand, by experience, how enormous the errors were that have been committed and allowed in your own recipes until now. But if perhaps these recipes of ours seem too worthy for you to accept (which is the common condition of men of your grain: the better things are, the less they value them), I will now propose even higher things to you, so that you may have something truly significant to despise.

**Another**

Take: Turpentine.
Linseed oil, 3 pounds of each.
Flowers of copper <term>Flos aeris; a chemical byproduct of copper, often verdigris, used for its ability to "eat away" dead flesh and cleanse ulcers</term>.
Saffron of Mars.
Molten salt <note>original: Salis fusi; salt that has been melted or purified by high heat to alter its properties</note>, 1 ounce of each.
Liquid of Feverfew, 4 ounces.
Let them be boiled together into a sufficiently thick body.

And although I do not call this recipe a "balsam," as is your custom to do, you may gather all the balsams you possess and—once again—see if they can "pluck out the eye" <note>original: oculum eruite; a Latin idiom meaning to surpass or outshine something so completely that it is put to shame</note> of this single one, if you have any power at all to achieve anything.

**Another**

*Another [Recipe].*

Take: Turpentine.

<term>Snakeweed</term> <note>original: "Serpentariæ"; likely Aristolochia or Polygonum bistorta, plants believed to draw out toxins</note>, one pound of each.

Search through these recipes of mine to see if you can find a single <term>overseas ingredient</term> <note>original: "ultramarinum"; literally "from beyond the sea." Paracelsus famously advocated for using local plants rather than expensive imports</note>. I tell you that you have burdened people more with those overseas remedies of yours than you have acted honorably toward them. Furthermore, believe me when I faithfully assert that Greek and Arabic recipes utterly mislead all other regions except their own. <note>The author is criticizing the traditional Galenic and Arabic medical systems, which relied on ingredients not native to Europe.</note>

*Another recipe in which <term>Mummy</term> <note>original: "Mammia"; a variation of Mummia, a substance derived from bitumen or preserved remains, believed to contain concentrated vital energy</note> is the most powerful ingredient, for the cure of all wounds.*

Take: Dill oil.
Linseed [oil], one pound of each.
Washed turpentine, 4 ounces.
Ground <term>Amber</term> <note>original: "Carabis"; from the Arabic "kahraba"</note>, 1 ounce.
<term>Colcothar</term> <note>original: "Colchotaris"; the red oxide of iron remaining after distillation</note>.
<term>Saffron of Mars</term> <note>original: "Croci Martis"; a name for iron oxide or rust</note>.
<term>Flowers of Copper</term> <note>original: "Florum æris"; likely verdigris or copper acetate</note>, two ounces of each.
Let it be made into a sticky paste.

*Another similar one.*

Take: Egg oil, 10 ounces.
Nut oil, 1 pound.
Linseed oil, half an ounce <note>original: "℥.ß."; the "ß" symbol indicates "semis" or half</note>.
Crushed <term>Amber</term> <note>original: "Carabis"</note>, 2 ounces.
Let them be boiled into a paste, and add: extract of <term>Greater Comfrey</term> <note>original: "conſolidæ maioris"; known for its ability to "knit" or heal tissues</note> and earthworms, half an ounce of each.

Why is it necessary for me to set down many and various recipes, when only two or three are sufficient for all wounds...

Even the smallest of those things I have prescribed in this place—of this I am more certain than certain—excels everything you have ever written. *A Plaster for Punctured and Stabbed Wounds.*

Take: <term>Wax</term> <note>original: "Ceræ"</note>, <term>Litharge</term> <note>original: "Litargyrij"; lead oxide, commonly used in plasters to aid drying and healing</note>, one pound of each.

Let the colophony, oil, and wax be melted together over a gentle fire. Afterward, the litharge and calamine, ground into a powder, should be added gradually and cooked to the thickness of a plaster, without being burned. Afterward, the following ingredients must be added:

*Another form of "stictic" plaster for wounds and punctured ulcers.*
<note>The term "stictic" comes from the Greek word for "punctured," indicating this plaster was specifically formulated for deep, narrow wounds like those from a blade or needle.</note>

Take:
<term>Galbanum</term>,
<term>Opoponax</term>, two drachms of each.
<term>Ammoniacum</term>,
<term>Bdellium</term>, <note>These four ingredients are aromatic gum-resins traditionally used to draw out infection and soften hard swellings.</note> two drachms of each.

Let all these be ground very finely, placed in a glazed earthenware pot, and soaked in strong wine vinegar; let them macerate for a natural day <note>a full 24-hour period</note>. Afterward, let them be cooked over a gentle charcoal fire so that the gums gradually melt. Once this is achieved, pour them into a bag to be strained, squeezed out, and separated from the dregs, which are to be discarded. Let this strained liquid boil again in the pot until the vinegar has evaporated, stirring constantly so that the gums do not happen to burn; keep it aside for a while. Afterward, take two pounds of olive oil and half a pound of new wax, which, when placed in the pot, should melt gradually. Finally, throw in one and a half pounds of <term>litharge</term> <note>lead oxide</note> ground very finely, and let everything be stirred together...

->*Uses and Powers.*<-

The virtues of this plaster are countless for old as well as recent ulcers: it dries, cleanses, produces good flesh, consolidates <note>to "consolidate" a wound meant to close the edges and encourage the formation of a scar.</note>, and heals in one week more than another would in a whole month. It does not allow good flesh to rot or be corrupted...

### *The cure of ulcers through prepared antimony.*

| Ingredient | Amount |
| :--- | :--- |
| Antimony | 1/2 pound |
| Calcined tartar <term>Tartari calcinati; potassium carbonate produced by heating the crusty sediment from wine barrels to a high temperature until it becomes a white powder</term> | 1/2 pound |
| Alcohol of wine | One and a half measures |

Let these be mixed together and distilled through an alembic <note>a chemical apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for purifying substances through evaporation and condensation</note> until they are dissolved. Take 1 ounce of this dissolution and 1/2 ounce of alcohol of wine; let it dry out gradually, and afterwards let it be dissolved into an oil upon a marble slab in a damp place. A greater cure for wounds can hardly be found, although wounds of the head are excepted.

*The following may be added:*

| Ingredient | Amount |
| :--- | :--- |
| This oil of antimony | 7 ounces |
| Juice of raw tartar | 5 ounces |
| Oil of myrtle | 5 ounces |

Let a mixture be made. If this is applied to wounds only once every other day, in the manner of a liniment, they are rendered safe from complications <note>original: "accidentibus"; a medical term referring to secondary symptoms or the sudden worsening of a condition, such as infection or fever</note>.

From the *Book of Preparations*, Treatise 1, on Antimony.

654 ON TREATING ILLNESSES
*The treatment of wounds by means of*
*Balsam.*

Take: Mummy <note>original: "Mummiam." In historical medicine, "mummy" referred to a substance made from ground embalmed remains or bitumen, believed to have potent healing properties for closing wounds.</note> with an equal weight of honey, with flowers of St. John's Wort <note>original: "hyppericonis." *Hypericum perforatum* is still known today for its wound-healing and anti-inflammatory properties.</note> mixed in; let these be placed in a closed, sealed glass vessel to digest for four weeks. Afterwards, let this digested mixture be kept, with which wounds are to be constantly moistened until they are fully healed.
From a specific booklet on the surgery of wounds.

*The treatment of wounds by means of Balsam*

Take: as much seed of St. John's Wort as you wish, and from it make an oil by pressing or by distillation; to this, let an equal weight of turpentine <note>original: "terebinthinæ." A resin harvested from pine trees, used as a traditional antiseptic and binding agent in balsams.</note> be added. Let these boil with the best red wine for one hour. Next, let as much olive oil be added as would be half the weight of the other ingredients, and let them boil again for an hour as before. Then, finally, let a good quantity of "perforated" flowers <note>original: "florum perforatæ." Another name for St. John's Wort, referring to the translucent "perforations" visible on its leaves.</note> be added, along with wine as before; let them boil for six hours, or until all the wine has been consumed, the oils and flowers having been digested in the sun for a month.

*Additions that can be made to improve and*
*increase its strength.*

Flowers of centaury <note>Centaury was a herb frequently used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance to treat fevers and skin conditions.</note>, frankincense, prepared myrrh, and mummy—each of them, or not, according to your pleasure. Likewise, distilled turpentine, poured over flowers of St. John's Wort, and afterwards digested for a month in the sun.

340 ON THE CURING OF DISEASES.

| Ingredient | Amount |

### A marvelous, magnetic cure for wounds.

Take as much as you wish of the herb Persicaria <note>also known as Smartweed or Water-pepper; a plant common in damp areas known for its acrid taste</note>, otherwise called water-pepper or water-blood, washed in fresh water or simply moistened. Let it be crushed and applied over the wound, or let it only touch the wound for a short time; afterwards, let it be buried in some damp place and weighted down with a massive stone so that it rots more quickly. As soon as the herb has rotted, the wound that it touched will be healed. <note>This is a "sympathetic" remedy: the belief was that as the herb decayed in the earth, the infection or "poison" of the wound would likewise vanish.</note> Likewise, if a horse is injured in the back, let the herb be applied whole and entire between the saddle and the back; the more the horse is worked and fatigued, the better its wound is cured. Seek the cause of this.

<margin>From the little book on the preparation of Persicaria.</margin>

Let these be mixed and stored in a closed vessel for three months; then, at last, it will be an excellent balsam.

BOOK ONE. 261

...to our [patients], to avoid complications. Let the wounded be fed dry foods, and let them be content with little drink.

From the *Great Surgery*, Part 1, Book 1, Chapter 2, Section 7.

*The care of wounds through distillates and sublimates.*

*The care of wounds by oil of Antimony.*

Take: Antimony.
Rock salt <note>original: "Salis gemmæ"; a pure form of sodium chloride or halite</note>, calcined <note>heated to a high temperature to drive off moisture and make it friable</note>, 3 pounds of each.
Distill them together through a **retort** <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note> and a very strong **Athanor** <note>original: "Athanar"; a furnace designed to maintain a steady heat over long periods, often self-feeding with charcoal</note>, and after three natural days, you will have a very red oil, whose strengths and powers in desperate wounds can scarcely be expressed.

*The care of wounds by sublimated copper.*

Take: The best copper, and burn or calcine it with sulfur first. Afterward, dissolve this **lime** <note>original: "calcem"; the powdered residue left after metal is burned or heated</note> with **aqua fortis** <note>literally "strong water"; nitric acid, used by alchemists to dissolve metals</note>, then congeal <note>evaporate the liquid to leave a solid</note> it, and add to this congealed powder double the amount of liquefied salt. Afterward, **sublimate** <note>the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again upon cooling, used here to purify the medicine</note> it through a **reverberatory furnace** <note>a furnace that reflects heat from the roof onto the material being treated</note> into a green and light powder which, when sprinkled on wounds, heals them very quickly and perfectly.

*Explanation of those things which are required for the chemical art in this place.*

Distillation, of which mention was made in the preceding sections, is not unlike that which is done for **oil of vitriol** <note>sulfuric acid</note> in an Athanor—that is, in a furnace covered at the top, with certain air-holes opening at the sides. First, let the glass retort vessel be protected on all sides by very compact clay or **lute of wisdom** <note>original: "luto sapientiæ"; a specialized airtight sealant made of clay, dung, and other binders used to prevent glass from cracking and to keep vapors in</note>, and let a light fire be kindled in the manner of the first...

262 ON CURING DISEASES.

of the first degree, namely: After some time, or a fourth part of the duration, the heat must be increased up to the second degree. Again, at the midpoint of the time, it should be strengthened up to the third degree, so that the coals <note>original: "prunæ"; glowing embers used to provide steady heat</note> touch the **retort** <note>a glass vessel with a long, curved neck</note> and rise up to nearly the middle part of said vessel. When three-quarters of the distillation time have passed, one must reach the fourth degree of **reverberatory fire** <note>the highest heat in this system, where heat is reflected onto the top and sides of the vessel</note>; that is, so that the coals entirely surround the upper vessel and the sides. Fire of this kind must be continued until the receiving vessel, which holds the distilled liquid, is no longer colored by the **spirits** <note>volatile vapors or essences released during heating</note> of the material. Once this is observed, and you are certain that no spirits remain in the retort, let everything finish cooling, and save what is contained in the receiving vessel. 

It must be noted, however, that the regimen of fire just established is for the shortening of time. Furthermore, it is tedious for a man to be constantly bound to a single distillation for three **natural days** <note>full 24-hour periods</note>, night and day, even if he has a partner to relieve him in turns. I have found by experience with **oil of vitriol** <note>original: "oleo vitrioli"; concentrated sulfuric acid, a highly corrosive and important chemical in early medicine</note>, according to the process I have given, that the whole business can be completed in eight hours—and more precisely than by sweating over it for four days or more, including their continuous nights (as many have written, and even **Paracelsus** <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist known for using chemicals in medicine</note> himself). Unless the spirits are driven from the mineral media in eight hours by the graduated fire we have taught, it will not happen in as many days and nights even with the most intense fire, whether of flame or of fusion. There are, however, other reverberatory furnaces built for **calcination** <note>reducing a substance to powder through high heat</note> and not for distillation...

*An external remedy for wounds.*

Take **Sameth** <note>a Paracelsian term typically referring to tartar or a specific prepared salt</note> very well burned and **calcined** <note>reduced to a powder through high heat</note> to whiteness, to which add a smaller amount of the **circulated** <note>original: "circulatum"; a substance, often a solvent like vinegar or spirits, that has been purified through repeated cycles of distillation</note>. Distill afterward so that a very dry **dead head** <note>original: "caput mortuum"; the chemically exhausted residue remaining in the vessel after all volatile parts have been driven off</note> remains at the bottom, and so that the glass glows with a vehement fire. Thereafter, pour on fresh liquid and do as before, and repeat this as long as necessary until the "circulated" substance comes away completely sweet, just as it is in itself. Finally, let it be dissolved by itself, and the dissolved substance will be a remedy for every wound, better than all others.

# 264 ON THE CURING OF DISEASES.

*External remedy for ulcers.*

Take: One pound each of balsam prepared for wounds (according to the process for external remedies in the *Archidoxes*) made from <term>Sameth|A term used in Paracelsian alchemy, often referring to a specific preparation of tartar or a secret mineral salt</term>, and balsam made from <term>rust|In this context, 'rubigo' or rust refers to the oxidation of metals, particularly iron, processed alchemically for its drying and healing properties</term>. Once these have been mixed into one, add half a pound of oil of iron. Let all these, mixed together, be applied over the ulcers; let them be washed daily as shall seem expedient, and let the consolidative <note>original: *consolidativum*; a healing plaster intended to close and firm up a wound</note> plaster be applied, which we have taught for ulcers.

Therefore, you shall continue the bath treatments until the very end of the healing process. The limbs are to be very well compressed with bandages, for the suppression of concurrent <note>original: *accidentium*; referring to the secondary symptoms or complications that arise alongside the main disease</note> symptoms.

*External remedy for blemishes.*

Above, we have sufficiently explained with a specific <term>corrosive|A substance that wears away or 'eats' the skin, used by early doctors to remove diseased or discolored tissue</term> the method by which the skin and blemishes are to be removed; but now, let us hear by what remedy the skin may be brought back over its flesh.

Take: Balsam for the ulcer, prepared according to the teaching of the *Archidoxes* regarding external remedies. Take equal parts of washed turpentine, oil of <term>earthworms|Commonly used in early modern medicine for their supposed ability to soothe nerves and restore suppleness to the skin</term>, and oil of eggs. With these, you shall wash all the flesh that has been stripped of its skin. After this care, nothing further is required. The property of this medicine is such that it brings about a new color with new skin, and a natural hardness <note>meaning a healthy firmness or durability of the skin</note>, so that it cannot be further contaminated by the preceding blemishes.

Book of the *Archidoxes*, on external remedies.

<meta>This page continues the surgical recipes from the previous section, transitioning from the methods used by "the ancients" to the specific pharmaceutical preparations of Paracelsus. BOOK ONE. 267

One pound of Great Comfrey <note>original: "Consolidæ maioris"; a plant traditionally used in herbal medicine to promote the healing of wounds and bones</note>.
One and a half pounds of Adder’s Tongue <note>original: "Ophioglossi"; a species of fern once highly valued for its wound-healing properties</note>.
Half a pound of cleansed earthworms <note>original: "vermium terrestrium purgatorum"; in historical medicine, earthworms were often used as a source of "animal oil" believed to be soothing and regenerative</note>.
Three ounces of green Birthwort <note>original: "Aristolochiæ viridis"; a plant used since antiquity to treat skin conditions and facilitate childbirth, hence its name</note>.

Let all these be crushed into the likeness of a poultice and kept in a cold place; if any moisture is separated, let it be discarded. Thus far has been the usage of the ancients; but now, hear what the practice of Paracelsus is.

->*The third wound ointment.*<-

Take <note>original: "℞", the symbol for *recipe*</note> the green roots and herbs which you prefer to choose from those mentioned before and those to follow, and crush them into a poultice. To these, add a little of the best wine until they are covered. Cook this in a double vessel <note>original: "duplici vase," referring to a bain-marie or water bath</note> within a closed jar for ten hours. After everything has cooled, take them out and crush them together, and pass through a linen cloth or a hair sieve <note>original: "setaceum"; a sieve made of stiff hair, used for filtering coarse materials from liquids</note> whatever parts are able to pass.

Let these be cooked again in a closed jar together with raw honeycomb (the honey not having been separated), or with butter alone, or with both mixed together. While hot, let them pass through a cloth or hair sieve, or be squeezed with a press. This juice should be placed in the sun in a beaked alembic <note>a distillation vessel with a downward-pointing spout</note> so that it may be purged of its phlegm <note>in this context, the watery or inactive byproduct that separates from the medicinal oil</note>. Afterward, placed in a sealed glass bottle, it should be exposed to the sun and reserved as an ointment.

->*Fourth wound ointment with*<-

Take <note>original: "℞", the symbol for *recipe*</note> 20 egg yolks; mix them with one pound <note>original: "libra"; a standard unit of weight in apothecary practice</note> of larch resin <note>original: "Resinæ lariceæ"; a sticky substance harvested from larch trees, used for its adhesive and antiseptic qualities</note>, until a yellow ointment is formed from it. *A <term>consolidative<note>A substance used to knit or close the edges of a wound together.</note></term> or <term>incarnative<note>A medicinal treatment intended to promote the growth of new flesh in a wound.</note></term> for wounds, and also for ulcers.*

From the *Book of Preparations*, regarding quicksilver <note>original: "argento viuo"; an archaic name for mercury.</note>.

*An incarnative for wounds.*

Take one pound <note>original: "℔.j."</note> of lead ashes or <term>ceruse<note>original: "cerussæ"; white lead, often used in historical ointments despite its high toxicity.</note></term>.
Let them be cooked in vinegar for four hours; they will be an excellent consolidative for wounds and will produce healthy flesh.
From the *Book of Preparations*, regarding lead.

*Washes for deep wounds.*

Take honey, wine, alum, salt, etc., in whatever amount seems appropriate; drive it in and draw it out through a syringe <note>original: "syringam"; a plunger-tool used for irrigating deep injuries.</note> repeatedly, until that liquid comes out of the wound clear. In the meantime, wound-healing potions and wound-healing plasters should be used.

*A remedy for poisonous bites from dogs.*

Let cold water or beer be given to the patient to drink, and in such great abundance that they induce significant nausea and vomiting. And the food [should be] very cold— <note>The text ends mid-word at "frigidissi-", likely "frigidissimus" meaning "very cold".</note>

### *Another stictic plaster for punctured wounds, for removing symptoms.*

### *A method of preparing stictic plasters for punctured* <note>original: "fodicatorum;" refers to wounds caused by stabbing or piercing</note> *wounds.*

*The best method, according to Paracelsus.* <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a revolutionary Swiss physician and alchemist who championed the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine.</note>

Take golden litharge: as much as you wish. Let this be cooked in varnish for ten hours, until it becomes a hard mass that allows itself to be ground into powder. Once dissolved in any of the aforementioned cerates, let it be mixed with the rest of the ingredients, such as the gums and powders, according to the prescribed order. This specific method, among all others, has been most successful for me.

_Formula for Arsenical Liquid._
Take <note>original symbol: "℞," abbreviation for *recipe*.</note> Raw and white arsenic, half a pound <note>original: "℔. ß."</note>.
Saltpeter <note>original: "Salis nitri;" potassium nitrate</note>, 1 pound <note>original: "℔. j."</note>.
Rock salt <note>original: "Salis gemmæ;" high-purity common salt</note>, half an ounce <note>original: "℥. ß."</note>.
Grind very finely and submit to an open reverberation <note>heating in a furnace where the flame is reflected back onto the material</note> for 24 hours. Once these are combined, the arsenic glows for three hours, then it liquefies; once this is observed, let it be poured into water, and its alkali <note>In this context, the soluble salt or residue resulting from the chemical reaction</note> is extracted.

_Additions can be made._
To 15 ounces <note>original: "℥. xv."</note> of this alkali.
Pomegranate peels, 6 ounces <note>original: "℥. vj."</note>.
Frankincense <note>original: "Thuris"</note>, 1 ounce <note>original: "℥. j."</note>.
Mucilage of *botin* <note>original: "Muſcilaginis de botin;" likely referring to a resinous mucilage derived from turpentine or larch resin</note>, 3 ounces <note>original: "℥. iij."</note>.
Let a mixture be made.

It should be noted in these processes that the first two reverberations are done in closed vessels, in the manner of sublimation, but the third in an open vessel, etc.
*From the Book of Preparations regarding Arsenic.*

#### *Explanation.*

It is necessary to explain to those who are ignorant of the art of distillation what the author means in this place by a "closed jar."
A tin vessel <note>original: "Amphora stannea"</note> with its lid very well-fitted should be taken. The bruised wound-healing herbs are to be placed inside with wine and stored there. Immediately, the joint of the lid must be sealed with a paste made of white wheat flour and beaten egg whites, so that once dried...

--- Page 304 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation (Expositio) of distillation and decoction methods from page 283. It specifically completes the instructions for sealing a "closed jar" and describes the setup for a cooling condenser used to extract oils and spirits from juniper berries.</meta>

284 ON CURING DISEASES

...[wheat flour] pastes applied with a linen cloth, so that not even the smallest amount of the spirit of wine can escape. Once this is completed, a cauldron should be prepared, more than half-filled with water, in which the aforementioned jar is so placed that it sits on the bottom of the cauldron, while its head protrudes out of the water by at least a span and a half <note>original: "sesquipalmo" — approximately 12 inches</note>. Afterward, let a fire be lit beneath the cauldron, and this gradually, so that the water boils for a little while. By this artifice, it will happen that the virtues of the herbs and the wine are mixed together without loss. 

Through the ignorance of both the ancients and the moderns, decoctions which are made in open vessels are weak, because all the medicinal powers of the things being boiled evaporate, and nothing remains but something dreggy, tasteless, and empty, which is more harmful to the sick than beneficial or useful.

Similarly, the distillation of juniper berries, which was mentioned above, is to be understood in this manner. After they have been crushed together with the herbs and placed in a copper vessel tinned on the inside (similar to those used by people who work at distilling "burning waters" <note>term: burning waters (aquas ardentes) — a historical term for high-proof distilled spirits or alcohol</note> from wine). 

All joints having been very well sealed, let a long pipe be fitted to the beak of the vessel's helmet <note>term: helmet (galea) — the top part of a still, often called an alembic head, where vapors collect</note>. This pipe should pass through a wooden vessel of sufficient size and filled with cold water; they call this middle part the "refrigerator" <note>term: refrigeratorium — a cooling vat or condenser used to turn medicinal vapors back into liquid</note>. At the end of the pipe protruding from the cooling vessel, let a glass vessel be fitted to receive the liquor flowing forth. In that receiver, the phlegm <note>note: in this context, "phlegm" refers to the watery byproduct of distillation</note> is seen with the oil floating on top. Use these according to the true order of medicine. 

So that readers may attain a clearer knowledge of preparations of this kind, it is worthwhile...

<summary>This page explains the "bain-marie" method for infusing herbs in wine using a sealed jar and describes an early distillation apparatus featuring a cooling condenser (refrigerator) to capture volatile oils.</summary>
<keywords>distillation, decoction, juniper berries, laboratory equipment, alembic, refrigerator, pharmaceutical chemistry, medical theory</keywords>

--- Page 305 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of pharmaceutical preparation, moving from specific distillation setups (like the juniper berry method on page 284) to a theoretical framework for how "simples" (individual medicinal ingredients) should be handled and purified by a surgeon.</meta>

->BOOK ONE. 285<-

It will be worthwhile to add here several matters concerning simple bodies and mixed medicines composed of those simples, of which no true Surgeon ought to be ignorant.

Every natural body of a **simple** <note>term: simple — a single ingredient used in medicine, usually a herb, mineral, or animal product, as opposed to a compound "mixed" medicine</note> is twofold: pure and impure. The assembly of more learned physicians has judged that the impure part must be cast aside through separation, while the pure is reserved as a vehicle—or, if I may call it so, a tether—for the soul of each simple. The learned school of the **Spagyrists** <note>term: Spagyrists — early chemists or alchemists who followed the methods of Paracelsus, focusing on the separation and recombination of natural elements to create medicine</note> calls this kind of separation "preparation," and they place the adaptation of simples under a fourfold **mastery** <note>term: mastery (magisterium) — a technical term for a refined alchemical process or the resulting perfected substance</note>.

Some simples do not wish to lose either body, but are prepared in both. Certain others desire to be imbued and mixed into a foreign body, in which they operate far more effectively than in their own. Some, indeed, if not removed from their natural state—or while remaining within it—accomplish nothing at all, yet once purified they are most efficacious. Still others desire to be separated from the other body through **corruption** <note>term: corruption (corruptionem) — a process of breakdown, such as fermentation or putrefaction, used in early chemistry to release the active "spirit" of a substance</note>...

<summary>This page introduces the theoretical principles of "Spagyric" medicine, explaining that natural ingredients must be separated into pure and impure parts to release their medicinal "souls." It describes how different substances require different methods of preparation, such as purification, mixing with foreign bodies, or fermentation.</summary>

<keywords>simples, Spagyrists, alchemical preparation, purification, mastery, corruption, medicinal essence, surgery</keywords>

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>This page concludes the medical instructions following the theoretical discussion on the preparation of medicines on page 285. It applies those principles to a specific ailment, providing a practical conclusion to the section of the book.</meta>

286 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES
*The cure for Vndimia.*

Vndimia <note>term: Vndimia — in the Paracelsian medical system, this refers to a soft, watery abscess or tumor caused by an imbalance of the "salt" element in the body</note> is an abscess arising from a resolved salt, separated from the healthy part, in that place where it establishes its center. It is not opened <note>meaning: it should not be surgically lanced</note> because a sweet and non-corrosive salt produced it: you will see it exude liquid upon compression.

| Recipe: | |
| :--- | :--- |
| Realgar <note>term: Realgar — a bright red mineral composed of arsenic sulfide</note> | 1 drachm |
| Lime <note>term: Calcis — quicklime or calcium oxide, often used in caustic plasters</note> | 3 drachms |
| Oak mistletoe | as much as is sufficient for incorporation |
| **And then make a plaster.** | |

After it has been opened, apply our Opodeldoch <note>term: Opodeldoch — a signature medicinal plaster or liniment formulated by Paracelsus to heal wounds and reduce inflammation</note>.

Let its cleansing agent be:
Recipe: Colophony <note>term: Colophony — a sticky resin derived from pine trees</note>, 1 drachm.
Wax, 4 drachms.
Turpentine resin <note>note: original: "Resina de botin" — a specific resin from the larch tree favored by Paracelsian doctors</note>, as much as is sufficient for incorporation.

After the cleansing, you shall consolidate <note>meaning: to heal or close the wound</note>.
Recipe: Greater Comfrey.
Round Birthwort, 1 drachm of each.
Egg yolks.
Turpentine, as much of each as is sufficient.
Let a plaster be made.

*Small Surgery*, Book 3, Chapter 3.

->THE END<-
